## 9.2 Empat C Tambahan GASING: Ketika Kompetensi Menjadi Karakter

Ada yang sering terlewat dalam perdebatan tentang pendidikan abad ke-21.

Orang berbicara tentang *skill*. Tentang kompetensi. Tentang kesiapan kerja. Tapi jarang ada yang bertanya: kesiapan untuk menjadi *apa*, sebetulnya? Mesin yang produktif? Individu yang adaptif? Atau manusia yang utuh?

Kerangka P21 dengan empat kompetensinya, Creativity, Critical Thinking, Communication, dan Collaboration, adalah sumbangan besar bagi dunia pendidikan. Tidak ada alasan untuk meragukannya. Tapi kerangka itu lahir dari konteks tertentu, dan konteks Indonesia bukan semata-mata perpanjangan dari konteks itu.

Indonesia adalah negara dengan 700-an bahasa daerah, 17.000 pulau, dan sejarah yang tidak bisa diringkas dalam satu narasi. Indonesia juga adalah negara yang sedang bergerak cepat ke dalam era digital, sementara sebagian anak-anaknya masih belajar di ruang kelas tanpa listrik yang stabil. Di sinilah P21 menemukan batas kemampuannya, bukan karena salah, tapi karena tidak cukup.

Metode GASING merespons batas itu dengan menambahkan empat kompetensi:

$$\text{4C (P21)} + \text{4C (GASING)} = \text{8C}$$

Keempat tambahan ini bukan pelengkap. Bukan ornamen. Mereka adalah jawaban atas empat pertanyaan mendasar yang tidak dijawab oleh P21:

**Computational Logic** menjawab pertanyaan: *Bagaimana anak Indonesia berpikir di era kecerdasan buatan, terutama mereka yang tidak pernah memegang laptop?*

**Character** menjawab pertanyaan: *Apa gunanya kecerdasan yang tidak berakar pada nilai?*

**Culture** menjawab pertanyaan: *Globalisasi milik siapa, jika identitas lokal harus dikorbankan untuk meraihnya?*

**Compassion** menjawab pertanyaan: *Apa artinya "kompeten" jika kepada sesama kita tidak peduli?*

Keempat kompetensi ini tidak ditambahkan karena teori. Mereka ditambahkan karena lapangan bicara lebih keras dari teori.

### 9.2.1 Computational Logic: Ketika Matematika Mengajarkan Cara Berpikir Seperti Mesin

> *"Setiap kali seorang murid menyelesaikan soal pengurangan, perkalian, atau pembagian dengan Metode GASING, ia sedang berlatih berpikir seperti seorang programmer: memecah masalah besar menjadi langkah-langkah kecil yang dapat dieksekusi secara sistematis."*
>
> Prinsip inti Metode GASING

#### Sebuah Kisah Sebelum Teorinya

Oktober 2024. Lima anak sekolah dasar dari berbagai penjuru Indonesia berdiri di atas panggung internasional di Korea Selatan, dalam ajang *9th World Innovative Technology Challenge*.

Mereka pulang membawa emas, perak, dan tiga perunggu dalam bidang *coding*.

Dua dari mereka berasal dari Papua. Satu dari Kalimantan Tengah. Satu dari Maluku Utara.

Yang membuat pencapaian ini luar biasa bukan medalinya. Yang luar biasa adalah ini: kelima anak itu baru belajar *coding* selama enam bulan, setiap Sabtu saja. Tidak ada kelas khusus selama hari sekolah. Tidak ada lab komputer setiap hari. Tidak ada beasiswa akselerasi.

Lalu dari mana datangnya kemampuan itu?

Jawabannya bukan di pelajaran *coding*-nya. Jawabannya ada jauh sebelum itu, di dalam cara mereka belajar matematika.

#### Apa Itu Computational Logic, dan Mengapa Ia Bisa Tumbuh dari Matematika

*Computational logic*, atau logika komputasional, adalah kemampuan mengurai masalah kompleks menjadi langkah-langkah algoritmik, mengenali pola sistematis, dan merancang prosedur yang bisa direplikasi. Ini adalah fondasi pemrograman komputer sekaligus fondasi berpikir sistematis di era digital.

Jeannette Wing (2006), dalam makalahnya yang sangat berpengaruh tentang *computational thinking*, mendefinisikan kapasitas berpikir ini melalui empat komponen: *decomposition* (memecah masalah kompleks menjadi bagian-bagian kecil yang dapat dikelola), *pattern recognition* (mengidentifikasi pola dan keteraturan yang berulang), *abstraction* (mengabaikan detail yang tidak relevan dan berfokus pada inti masalah), serta *algorithm design* (membangun langkah-langkah sistematis untuk menemukan solusi yang dapat direplikasi).

Selama ini, *computational thinking* dianggap hanya bisa diajarkan melalui mata pelajaran informatika atau *coding* tersendiri. Metode GASING membuktikan sebaliknya.

#### Tidak Diajarkan. Ditumbuhkan.

Ini yang membedakan Metode GASING dari pendekatan mana pun yang ada: *computational logic* tidak pernah diajarkan secara langsung. Tidak ada sesi "berpikir komputasional" dalam jadwal pelatihan. Tidak ada modul *coding* untuk anak SD di daerah terpencil. Yang ada hanyalah matematika, tapi matematika yang dirancang untuk mengembangkan delapan kecerdasan secara bersamaan.

Dan dari sana, *computational logic* muncul dengan sendirinya sebagai *emergent property*, sifat yang timbul bukan dari instruksi, melainkan dari kondisi yang tepat.

**Tabel 9.17** Jenis Kecerdasan yang Dikembangkan dan Kontribusinya terhadap Logika Komputasional.

| Jenis Kecerdasan yang Dikembangkan | Aktivitas GASING | Kontribusi terhadap Logika Komputasional |
|---|---|---|
| Logis-Matematis | Operasi langkah demi langkah; penalaran bersyarat (periksa kondisi lalu putuskan) | Berpikir sekuensial; analisis bertahap; penalaran *if-then* |
| Spasial-Visual | Memvisualisasikan alur operasi; melihat pola dalam deretan bilangan | Kemampuan "melihat" struktur algoritma secara mental; representasi alur proses |
| Intrapersonal | Merefleksikan proses berpikir sendiri; mendeteksi dan memperbaiki kesalahan diri sendiri | Kapasitas menelusuri dan memperbaiki kesalahan dalam alur pikiran sendiri: *mental debugging* |
| Linguistik | Menjelaskan langkah-langkah dengan presisi dan urutan yang benar | Mengartikulasikan algoritma dengan jelas, analogus dengan menulis *pseudocode* yang dapat dieksekusi |

Kolom kanan tabel di atas mengungkap sesuatu yang signifikan: setiap kecerdasan yang dikembangkan melalui Metode GASING berkontribusi pada satu atau lebih komponen kerangka Wing (2006). Ini bukan kebetulan. Ini adalah bukti desain pembelajaran yang secara implisit mencakup seluruh komponen *computational thinking*, tanpa pernah menyebutnya demikian.

#### Algoritma yang Tersembunyi di Balik Soal Sederhana

Ambil satu contoh yang tampak sepele: 64 dikurangi 7.

Bagi kebanyakan orang dewasa, ini soal mudah yang diselesaikan dalam satu detik. Tapi perhatikan apa yang sebenarnya terjadi dalam pikiran seorang anak yang memahaminya dengan benar.

*(Lihat Gambar 9.26: Algoritma 64−7: dekomposisi langkah demi langkah yang sesuai dengan computational thinking.)*

Alur di atas adalah algoritma lengkap: ada kondisi yang diperiksa (*if-then*), ada keputusan yang diambil, ada urutan langkah yang harus diikuti, ada keluaran yang dihasilkan. Ini persis seperti logika pemrograman komputer, hanya saja mediumnya adalah pensil dan kertas, bukan layar dan *keyboard*.

Anak-anak GASING melatih pola ini puluhan kali setiap hari, di setiap operasi BAKALKUBAGI, tanpa pernah menyadari bahwa mereka sedang melatih diri untuk berpikir seperti programmer.

**Tabel 9.18** Aktivitas dan Mekanisme GASING yang Memunculkan Logika Komputasional.

| Mekanisme | Kecerdasan yang Dikembangkan | Bagaimana Logika Komputasional Muncul |
|---|---|---|
| Prosedur langkah demi langkah | Logis-Matematis, Linguistik | Setiap operasi matematika dijelaskan dalam urutan yang jelas dan dapat diikuti, analogus dengan *pseudocode* yang dapat dieksekusi oleh siapapun yang mengikuti langkah-langkahnya. |
| Pengenalan pola | Logis-Matematis, Spasial-Visual | Urutan perkalian (1→10→2→9→5→kuadrat→3→4→8→7→6) didasarkan pada pola logis: pengenalan pola dalam aksi nyata. |
| Menjelaskan "mengapa" | Linguistik, Logis-Matematis, Intrapersonal | Murid dilatih menjawab "mengapa 9+4=13?" dan "bagaimana membuktikan 12×4=48?", membangun kebiasaan mengartikulasikan logika di balik suatu prosedur. |
| Menjelajahi pola kuadrat | Logis-Matematis, Spasial-Visual | Pola bilangan kuadrat untuk 41–50 dan 51–60 membangun kapasitas abstraksi: melihat aturan umum dari kasus-kasus khusus, lalu menggeneralisasikannya. |

#### Pemetaan Formal: GASING dan Kerangka Wing (2006)

Tabel berikut menunjukkan pemetaan eksplisit antara komponen *computational thinking* versi Wing (2006) dan implementasinya dalam Metode GASING. Pemetaan ini bukan sekadar analogi: ia adalah korespondensi yang sangat spesifik.

**Tabel 9.19** Komponen *Computational Thinking* (Wing, 2006) dan Implementasinya dalam Metode GASING.

| Komponen *Computational Thinking* (Wing, 2006) | Implementasi dalam Metode GASING |
|---|---|
| *Decomposition* | Memecah operasi kompleks menjadi langkah-langkah kecil yang sekuensial: misalnya, 64−7 diuraikan menjadi: periksa kondisi, uraikan puluhan, kurangi, gabungkan, tentukan hasil. |
| *Pattern Recognition* | Urutan perkalian berdasarkan pola (1→10→2→9→5→kuadrat→3→4→8→7→6); pola bilangan kuadrat; pasangan komplemen-10 dalam penjumlahan. |
| *Abstraction* | Pola bilangan kuadrat untuk 41–50 dan 51–60 membangun kapasitas generalisasi: menemukan aturan umum dari kasus-kasus khusus, lalu menerapkannya ke bilangan mana pun. |
| *Algorithm Design* | Setiap operasi BAKALKUBAGI adalah prosedur langkah demi langkah yang dapat direplikasi secara konsisten, persis seperti algoritma yang dapat dieksekusi oleh komputer. |

Pemetaan di atas memiliki implikasi kebijakan yang penting. Selama ini *computational thinking* dianggap hanya bisa diajarkan melalui mata pelajaran *coding* atau informatika tersendiri. Bukti dari Metode GASING menunjukkan sebaliknya: fondasi *computational thinking* dapat dibangun secara alami melalui pembelajaran matematika yang dirancang dengan baik, bahkan untuk anak-anak di daerah terpencil yang sama sekali tidak memiliki akses ke komputer.

Anak-anak ini sebelumnya tidak bisa menghitung 4 + 3. Dari tidak bisa menjumlahkan 4 + 3, kini mendemonstrasikan 7 + 5 dengan pemahaman mendalam: sebuah transformasi yang luar biasa.

Penjelasan mereka mengikuti jalur dari benda konkret ke representasi semi-abstrak ke angka abstrak. Inilah jalur konkret-ke-abstrak yang membangun logika komputasional sejati, sebuah kemampuan yang muncul secara alami dari proses pembelajaran yang tepat.

Sebelumnya, ia menghitung perkalian menggunakan metode lidi yang lambat dan mekanis. Kini Esra bekerja dengan alur berpikir yang logis dan sistematis, jauh lebih cepat dan efisien. Berpikir terstruktur seperti inilah yang akan memunculkan logika komputasional, kemampuan yang membentuk fondasi kuat untuk menghadapi era digital.

Keempat video ini secara konsisten menampilkan pola yang sama: anak-anak dari daerah yang selama ini paling terpinggirkan dalam peta pendidikan Indonesia menunjukkan kemampuan berpikir algoritmik yang sangat kuat. Ini membuktikan bahwa kapasitas *computational thinking* bukan hak eksklusif anak-anak kota yang memiliki akses ke komputer dan kelas *coding*. Dengan desain pembelajaran yang tepat, fondasi ini dapat dibangun di mana saja.

## 9.2 The GASING Additional 4C: Four Competencies the World Forgot to Include

There is a question that almost never gets asked in education policy debates.

Everyone talks about skills. About competencies. About workforce readiness and 21st-century preparedness. But almost no one stops to ask: readiness to *become what*, exactly? A productive unit? An adaptable professional? Or a complete human being?

The P21 Framework's four competencies, Creativity, Critical Thinking, Communication, and Collaboration, represent a genuine and important contribution to education worldwide. There is no reason to dispute that. But frameworks are always children of their context, and Indonesia's context is not simply an extension of the context that produced P21.

Indonesia is a country of more than 700 living languages, 17,000 islands, and a history that resists every attempt at a single narrative. It is also a country accelerating rapidly into the digital era, while some of its children still study in classrooms without reliable electricity. In that gap between aspiration and reality, the P21 framework runs out of road, not because it is wrong, but because it is incomplete.

The GASING method responds to that incompleteness by extending the framework to eight:

$$\text{4C (P21)} + \text{4C (GASING)} = \text{8C}$$

The four additions, **Computational Logic**, **Character**, **Culture**, and **Compassion**, are not ornaments attached to the outside of a working structure. They are answers to four questions that P21 does not ask.

**Computational Logic** answers: *How does an Indonesian child learn to think in the age of artificial intelligence, especially one who has never owned a laptop?*

**Character** answers: *What is intelligence worth when it has no ethical foundation to stand on?*

**Culture** answers: *Whose globalisation is it, if local identity must be surrendered as the price of admission?*

**Compassion** answers: *What does it mean to be "competent" if the person next to you no longer matters?*

These competencies were not added because theory demanded them. They were added because the evidence from the field spoke louder than any theoretical framework could.

### 9.2.1 Computational Logic: When Mathematics Teaches Children to Think Like Machines

> *"Every time a student solves a subtraction, multiplication, or division problem with the GASING method, he is in fact practising thinking like a programmer: breaking a large problem into small steps that can be executed systematically."*
>
> Core principle of the GASING method

#### A Story Before the Theory

October 2024. Five primary school students from across Indonesia walked onto an international stage in South Korea, at the 9th World Innovative Technology Challenge.

They walked off with gold, silver, and three bronze medals in coding.

Two of them came from Papua. One from Central Kalimantan. One from North Maluku.

What made this remarkable was not the medals themselves. What made it remarkable was the timeline: these children had been learning to code for six months, every Saturday only. No specialist lab, no daily computer class, no accelerated scholarship programme. Just one morning a week.

So where did the ability come from?

Not from the coding lessons. The ability was already there, built in, before anyone had mentioned a keyboard. It had grown quietly inside the way these children had learned mathematics.

That is the story this section tells.

#### What Computational Logic Is, and Why Mathematics Can Build It

Computational logic is the capacity to decompose complex problems into algorithmic steps, recognise systematic patterns, and design procedures that can be reliably replicated. These are the foundational skills of computer programming and, more broadly, of systematic thinking in a digital world.

Jeannette Wing (2006), in her landmark paper on computational thinking, identifies four core components of this capacity. The first is *decomposition*: breaking complex problems into smaller, manageable parts. The second is *pattern recognition*: identifying regularities and recurring structures. The third is *abstraction*: setting aside irrelevant detail to focus on the essential logic of a problem. The fourth is *algorithm design*: constructing systematic, replicable steps toward a solution.

For decades, educators and policymakers have treated these skills as the exclusive property of computer science education. The GASING method shows that assumption to be wrong.

#### Not Taught. Grown.

Here is what distinguishes the GASING approach from every other approach that claims to develop computational thinking: computational logic is never directly taught. There is no scheduled "algorithmic thinking" session in the training programme. There is no coding module introduced to children in remote areas who have never seen a computer. There is only mathematics, but mathematics designed with deliberate care to develop multiple intelligences simultaneously.

And from that soil, computational logic emerges on its own.

This is what researchers call an *emergent property*: a capability that arises not from direct instruction, but from the conditions the right design creates. Table 9.17 shows how each intelligence developed through the GASING method contributes to a specific component of Wing's framework.

**Table 9.17.** Intelligence Types Developed Through GASING and Their Contributions to Computational Logic.

| Intelligence Developed | GASING Activity | Contribution to Computational Logic |
|---|---|---|
| Logical-Mathematical | Step-by-step operations; conditional reasoning (check condition, then decide) | Sequential thinking; step-by-step analysis; if-then reasoning |
| Spatial-Visual | Visualising the flow of operations; seeing patterns in number sequences | Ability to mentally "see" algorithmic structure; representation of process flow |
| Intrapersonal | Reflecting on one's own thought processes; detecting and correcting one's own errors | Capacity to trace and repair errors in one's own thinking: mental debugging |
| Linguistic | Explaining steps with precision and in correct sequence | Articulating algorithms clearly, analogous to writing executable pseudocode |

The right-hand column of this table reveals something important. Every intelligence the GASING method develops contributes to one or more components of Wing's framework. Logical-mathematical intelligence underpins decomposition and algorithm design. Spatial intelligence supports abstraction and process-flow visualisation. Intrapersonal intelligence supports debugging. Linguistic intelligence supports the ability to communicate an algorithm. This is not coincidence. It is the fingerprint of a learning design that implicitly encompasses the entire architecture of computational thinking, without once using that term.

#### The Algorithm Hidden in a Simple Subtraction

Consider 64 minus 7. To most adults, this is a one-second mental calculation. But look carefully at what is actually happening inside the mind of a child who truly understands it.

*(See Figure 9.26: The 64−7 algorithm: step-by-step decomposition matching computational thinking.)*

The flow above mirrors computer programming logic exactly. There is a condition to be checked (*if-then*), a decision to be made, an ordered sequence of steps to follow, and an output to be produced. GASING children practise this exact pattern dozens of times each day, across every BAKALKUBAGI operation, without ever realising they are training themselves to think like programmers.

The computational logic skills built this way do not stay confined to simple arithmetic. They become the foundation for more advanced mental calculation: multiplying three-digit numbers in one's head, computing quadratic roots without a formula, and a range of operations that initially seem impossible to perform mentally.

**Table 9.18.** GASING Activities and the Mechanisms Through Which Computational Logic Emerges.

| Mechanism | Intelligence Developed | How Computational Logic Emerges |
|---|---|---|
| Step-by-step procedure | Logical-Mathematical, Linguistic | Every operation is explained in a clear, followable sequence, analogous to pseudocode that anyone can execute by following the steps. |
| Pattern recognition | Logical-Mathematical, Spatial-Visual | The multiplication sequence (1→10→2→9→5→squares→3→4→8→7→6) is built on logical pattern: pattern recognition in real action. |
| Explaining "why" | Linguistic, Logical-Mathematical, Intrapersonal | Students are trained to answer "why does 9+4=13?" and "how do we prove 12×4=48?", building the habit of articulating the logic behind every procedure. |
| Exploring square-number patterns | Logical-Mathematical, Spatial-Visual | The patterns for squares of 41–50 and 51–60 build abstraction capacity: seeing the general rule from specific cases, then extending it to any number. |

#### A Formal Mapping: GASING and the Wing Framework

Table 9.19 makes the correspondence explicit, showing precisely how each component of Wing's (2006) computational thinking framework appears in the GASING method. This is not an analogy. It is a point-for-point alignment.

**Table 9.19.** Computational Thinking Components (Wing, 2006) and Their Implementation in the GASING Method.

| Component (Wing, 2006) | Implementation in the GASING Method |
|---|---|
| Decomposition | Complex operations are broken into small sequential steps: 64−7, for instance, is decomposed into check condition, separate tens, subtract, recombine, state result. |
| Pattern Recognition | The multiplication sequence based on pattern (1→10→2→9→5→squares→3→4→8→7→6); square-number patterns; complement-of-ten pairs in addition. |
| Abstraction | Square-number patterns for 41–50 and 51–60 build generalisation capacity: finding the general rule from specific cases, then applying it to any number. |
| Algorithm Design | Every BAKALKUBAGI operation is a step-by-step procedure that can be replicated consistently, exactly like an algorithm executable by a computer. |

The policy implications here are direct. If the foundations of computational thinking can be built naturally through well-designed mathematics learning, then access to computers is not the precondition it has long been assumed to be. Children in the most remote areas of the archipelago, with no computers in their classrooms at all, can still develop the algorithmic thinking that the digital era demands.

#### From the Highlands of Papua to the International Stage: Field Evidence

The four videos below document how computational logic surfaces naturally across radically different locations in the Indonesian archipelago, from Central Papua to Bali, from the Papua Highlands to Morotai Island.

*(See Figure 9.27: Student, Puncak Regency, Central Papua, explains 39+34 using P and S notation.)*

In this first video, children in Puncak Regency, Central Papua, who previously could not perform addition, explain 39+34 with striking systematic clarity. The steps they articulate are decompose, group, add, combine. This is not merely arithmetic. This is coding expressed through mathematics. The algorithmic thinking pattern they are building here will serve them in the digital era.

*(See Figure 9.28: Students, Lanny Jaya, Papua Highlands, demonstrate 7+5 with collaborative algorithmic understanding.)*

In the second video, children from Lanny Jaya in the Papua Highlands demonstrate 7+5 using concrete manipulatives. They collaborate, correct one another, and build logical-mathematical thinking patterns together, all without any awareness that their computational logic is growing in the process. These children previously could not calculate 4+3. From unable to add 4+3 to demonstrating 7+5 with deep understanding: the transformation is extraordinary.

*(See Figure 9.29: Students, Badung Regency, Bali, understand the conceptual difference between 6×8 and 8×6.)*

Children in Badung Regency, Bali, in the third video do not merely memorise 6×8=48. They understand it. They know that 6×8, meaning six groups each containing eight objects, is conceptually different from 8×6, meaning eight groups each containing six, even though the products are equal. Their explanation follows the path from concrete objects to semi-abstract representation to pure abstraction: the exact progression that builds genuine computational logic, arising naturally from the right learning process.

*(See Figure 9.30: Esra, Morotai Island, North Maluku, advances from tally-mark counting to systematic multiplication.)*

In the fourth video, Esra, a student from Morotai Island, North Maluku, undergoes a remarkable transformation. She once calculated multiplication by counting tally marks one by one, a slow and mechanistic process. She now works with a logical, systematic, and far more efficient flow of thinking. This is precisely the kind of structured cognition that generates computational logic as a natural by-product, the foundation for facing a digital era confidently.

Across all four videos, the same pattern holds: children from the historically most under-served regions of Indonesian education display remarkably strong algorithmic thinking. The evidence challenges a widespread assumption head-on. Computational thinking capacity is not the exclusive privilege of urban children with computers and coding tutors. With the right learning design, this foundation can be built anywhere in Indonesia, including the places that have been left furthest behind.

#### From Mental Arithmetic to the World Stage

The most compelling evidence that computational logic transfers directly into programming ability came in October 2024, at the *9th World Innovative Technology (WIT) Challenge* in South Korea.

---

**9th World Innovative Technology (WIT) Challenge**
*South Korea, October 2024*

- **Gold:** Felicia Dahayu, Banyuwangi, East Java
- **Silver:** Jose Norotouw, Jayapura City, Papua
- **Bronze:** Cressya Wianopa, Gunung Mas, Central Kalimantan
- **Bronze:** Esra Samuel Weyai, Biak Numfor, Papua
- **Bronze:** Uril Algifari, Central Halmahera, North Maluku

*All five primary school students, including those from 3T (frontier, outer, and disadvantaged) regions, had been learning coding from zero for only six months, every Saturday only.*

---

A contextual note is in order: the WIT Challenge is not the equivalent of the International Mathematical Olympiad, and it should not be presented as such. But that is precisely the point. For children to compete credibly on any international stage after only six months of Saturday-only lessons, technical coding skill alone is not sufficient. They need a firmly established foundation of algorithmic thinking built long before the first lesson. That is exactly what the GASING method had provided.

**Table 9.20.** The Transformation in Students' Computational Logic Capacity After GASING Training.

| Before Training | After Training |
|---|---|
| Memorises procedures without understanding the underlying pattern or logic | Algorithmic thinking: able to generalise patterns and transfer skills to new domains |
| Dependent on rote memorisation; easily lost under pressure | Possesses a strong thinking foundation, enabling very rapid absorption of coding |
| Cannot explain the procedure used; only knows "the answer is correct" | Able to articulate steps algorithmically, analogous to writing pseudocode |

*(See Figure 9.32: Transformation pathway: computational logic as emergent property leading to international achievement.)*

The transformation documented above is not merely a performance shift. It reflects something deeper: a move from memorisation toward genuine understanding, from procedure without meaning toward algorithms that can be explained and defended, and from dependence on the teacher toward independent thinking. That is precisely the cognitive profile most needed by the generation that will live and work in the era of artificial intelligence.

---

> **Key Principle: Computational Logic in GASING Is an Emergent Property**
>
> Computational logic emerges when logical-mathematical, spatial-visual, intrapersonal, and linguistic intelligences are developed simultaneously through multimodal activities.
>
> Not: *"Let us teach coding."*
>
> But: *"Let us build the foundation of algorithmic thinking through mathematics, and coding ability will emerge on its own."*

---

### 9.2.2 Character: The Person Behind the Competence

> *"I never imagined I could be good at mathematics. In my village, no one has ever become a graduate. But now I know: I can become a professor!"*
>
> Student, Tolikara, Papua

Read that again.

Not "I can pass the exam." Not "I am less afraid of numbers now." But: *I can become a professor.* From a village where no one had ever finished a degree. From a child who, not long before, could not add single digits without counting on his fingers.

Something happened to that child that had nothing to do with mathematics. Or rather, it happened *through* mathematics, but its destination was somewhere deeper. What changed was not his arithmetic. What changed was who he believed he was.

That is Character.

#### What Character Actually Is

The World Economic Forum (2015) identifies character as one of the three foundational pillars of 21st-century education, alongside literacies and competencies. It encompasses curiosity, initiative, persistence, adaptability, leadership, and social and cultural awareness. These are not personality traits a person is born with or without. They are qualities that form, or fail to form, depending on what a person experiences.

In the GASING context, character can be understood across three mutually reinforcing layers, each one building on the one beneath it.

The first layer is **psychological capacity**: self-efficacy and grit as the internal foundation that allows a person to function under pressure, persist through difficulty, and keep going when the easier option is to stop. This is the layer most abundantly documented in GASING field data, from the child who once froze when asked a simple question and now volunteers to perform in front of the class, to the teacher who refused to go home even when the lights went out.

The second layer is a **moral compass**: the honesty, courage, and integrity that direct psychological capacity toward the right ends. A student who dares to correct the teacher is not simply confident; he is simultaneously practising honesty and courage. A trainer willing to dress as a clown to make children love learning is not simply creative; he is demonstrating integrity in service to a purpose larger than his own dignity.

The third layer is **adaptability**: the capacity to face a world that does not hold still. In the GASING method, this grows naturally, because students are taught to understand *why* something works, not merely *how* to do it once. A person who understands the underlying pattern will always be better prepared for a new situation than one who has only ever memorised a single procedure for a familiar one.

These three layers do not operate independently. Psychological capacity without a moral compass produces capability without conscience. A moral compass without psychological capacity produces good intentions that collapse under pressure. And neither of these, without adaptability, will survive contact with a future that looks nothing like the past.

#### Character: Grown, Not Declared

Indonesia has invested significantly in character education. The Character Education Strengthening Programme, the *Profil Pelajar Pancasila*, and various school-based initiatives all reflect a genuine national commitment to forming students of good character. The aspiration is right.

But there is a question that rarely gets a satisfying answer: how is character actually formed, as opposed to merely declared?

Lectures about honesty do not reliably produce honest people. Posters about courage do not produce courageous ones. Character formed this way tends to remain on the surface: it shows up in survey responses and behaviour when someone is watching, then disappears under pressure when no one is.

The GASING method takes a different position, and it is one supported by decades of research in developmental psychology. Character is not taught through the transmission of values. It grows through the accumulation of specific experiences, experiences in which the right qualities are exercised, tested, and gradually become habit. This is precisely what Bandura (1977) demonstrated in his foundational work on self-efficacy: the most durable form of self-belief is not built through verbal encouragement but through the direct experience of mastery, through actually doing something hard and succeeding.

In GASING sessions, this happens continuously, quietly, and without the students realising it.

**Table 9.21.** Intelligence Types Developed Through GASING and Their Contributions to Character Formation.

| Intelligence Developed | GASING Activity | Contribution to Character |
|---|---|---|
| Intrapersonal | Self-reflection; noticing progress; experiencing "I can do it!" | Self-confidence, self-awareness, recognition of previously hidden potential |
| Interpersonal | Helping classmates, peer tutoring, collaboration in games | Empathy, care, loyalty: a classmate's success becomes one's own |
| Bodily-Kinaesthetic | Repeated finger practice; daily mental arithmetic exercises | Discipline and persistence (grit): the habit of showing up and doing the work |
| Logical-Mathematical | Confronting difficult problems; trying different strategies | Resilience: "not yet able" rather than "unable" |
| Linguistic | Acknowledging uncertainty; asking questions without embarrassment | Academic honesty: "I cannot yet" is more valued than cheating to appear capable |

The pattern visible across this table is precise: character traits do not arise from character lessons. They arise from specific types of experience. Persistence does not come from a lecture about persistence. It comes from the repeated experience of facing a difficult problem, not surrendering, and eventually succeeding. Self-confidence does not come from excessive praise. It comes from the experience of genuine achievement, so that the student knows from direct personal evidence, not from someone else's words, that she is capable.

#### Four Mechanisms, Four Traits

Four principal character qualities are formed through concrete mechanisms that operate in every GASING session.

**Discipline** is formed through the commitment to consistent practice. Daily mental arithmetic exercises, working through each step without skipping, arriving on time for every session: these accumulate into a deeply embedded habit that transfers far beyond mathematics.

**Persistence**, the quality that Angela Duckworth (2016) would call grit, is cultivated when students encounter problems they cannot immediately solve and are encouraged to try a different strategy rather than give up. The GASING philosophy replaces "I cannot" with "I cannot yet," and that small shift in language carries a profound shift in orientation: difficulty becomes a challenge to investigate, not evidence of fundamental incapacity.

**Academic honesty** grows from a learning culture in which admitting uncertainty is modelled and respected. When the trainer says "I don't know yet, let's find out together," and when students learn that saying "I cannot yet" is valued far above pretending to know, honesty becomes the natural default rather than the uncomfortable exception.

**Self-confidence** emerges when real competence is built, not when praise is inflated. This is the crucial distinction. The confidence that grows in GASING training is not the fragile kind that collapses when the first real difficulty arrives. It is the kind rooted in actual demonstrated ability, and for that reason it holds.

**Table 9.22.** Mechanisms of Character Development in the GASING Method.

| Mechanism | Intelligence Developed | How Character Emerges |
|---|---|---|
| Hard work | Bodily-Kinaesthetic, Logical-Mathematical, Intrapersonal | Students work through hundreds of mental arithmetic problems, building mental endurance and the habit of genuine effort. |
| Love of learning | Musical, Interpersonal, Intrapersonal | The trainer's energy and songs create contagious enthusiasm: learning becomes something to look forward to, not something to survive. |
| Striving for excellence | Logical-Mathematical, Intrapersonal | Problems are designed to encourage excellence, not merely adequacy. Students develop the habit of giving maximum effort as the norm, not the exception. |
| Loyalty and discipline | Interpersonal, Intrapersonal | The commitment to attend every session on time builds habits that then transfer into every other area of a student's life. |

#### The Deepest Transformation: When Identity Shifts

Among all the dimensions of character that develop through the GASING method, the transformation of self-confidence is the most visible and, ultimately, the most consequential. But it is important to be precise about what kind of self-confidence this is.

*(See Figure 9.37: Student from Kediri teaches 8,616,488 ÷ 8 to classmates: confidence as emergent property.)*

Notice what the accompanying note says about this video: this confidence was not trained. There was no scheduled session on "confidence-building." There was no curriculum objective that said "by the end of this lesson, students will feel capable." What existed instead was a learning environment so accessible, so genuinely enjoyable, and so designed for real success, that the child's sense of his own capability grew as a natural consequence of actually becoming capable. When a child truly understands what he is doing, confidence follows on its own. This is the emergent property of genuine competence, not its cause.

*(See Figure 9.38: Student transformation from trembling when asked 8+4 to answering two-digit multiplication aloud and fast.)*

This second video documents a transformation that anyone who has worked in education in under-resourced communities will recognise as extraordinary: a child who once trembled when asked what 8+4 equals, now calls out two-digit multiplication answers loudly and without hesitation. The mathematics changed. But more importantly, the child's relationship to difficulty changed. And more importantly still, his image of himself changed.

*(See Figures 9.42, 9.43: Students from across Indonesia, from fear to joy; from fleeing problems to racing toward them.)*

These videos capture what transformation looks like at scale. Students from Sabang to Merauke speak in one voice: mathematics is no longer frightening. Children who once fell silent when a problem appeared now shout to be the first to answer, competing with each other not out of anxiety but out of genuine enthusiasm. The classroom that was once held in the grip of fear has become, as these videos show, an arena of exuberant, almost chaotic joy.

That sight had never been seen before. Particularly not in Papua.

#### From Fear to Aspiration: A Measurable Shift

**Table 9.23.** Character Transformation in Students After GASING Training.

| Before Training | After Training |
|---|---|
| Regards mathematics as a frightening obstacle to be avoided | Regards mathematics as an enjoyable challenge to be pursued |
| Reluctant to try; avoids difficulty | Eager and enthusiastic; races to attempt problems |
| Lacks self-confidence; feels academically incapable | Confident, grounded in demonstrated real competence |
| Afraid of making errors; will not answer even when knowing the answer | Dares to try; dares to stand before the class |
| Has no academic aspirations because no one has ever believed in them | Dares to dream: "I can become a professor!" |

These five transformations are not independent of one another. They form a chain, and the chain runs in one direction. When mathematics anxiety is resolved, the love of learning becomes possible. When the love of learning grows, real competence develops. When real competence develops, self-confidence follows. When genuine self-confidence takes root, the courage to attempt difficult things emerges. And when that courage is established, aspirations that were previously unthinkable begin to grow.

This is not a change in arithmetic ability. It is a change in how a person understands his own place in the world, and what possibilities he believes are available to him.

Children who had never imagined an academic future, because no one had ever told them they were capable of one, now dare to dream.

They are no longer the same people they were.

*(See Figure 9.44: Group of GASING students: new identities, new aspirations.)*

---

> **Key Principle: Character in GASING Is an Emergent Property**
>
> Character forms when intrapersonal, interpersonal, bodily-kinaesthetic, logical-mathematical, and linguistic intelligences are developed simultaneously through multimodal activities that require genuine effort and deliver genuine success.
>
> Not: *"Let us lecture about Character."*
>
> But: *"Let us create experiences in which a child discovers she is capable, and Character will grow on its own."*

---

### 🤖 Claude:
 The user wants me to write the four elaborations for Table 9.17 (Computational Logic), following the style of the Collaboration supplementary note. Let me write rich, engaging prose for each of the four intelligences: Logical-Mathematical, Spatial-Visual, Intrapersonal, and Linguistic. Each should answer its specific question and feel alive, not dry.### Supplementary Note 9.X: Why Computational Logic Emerges, Not Is Taught

---

**In the Logical-Mathematical activity**, GASING trains something that looks, on the surface, like arithmetic practice. A child works through a subtraction. Then another. Then another. The numbers change; the structure does not. And that is precisely the point.

Every BAKALKUBAGI operation in the GASING method is a procedure with a fixed architecture: check the condition, make a decision, execute the steps in order, produce the result. The child who has worked through this structure fifty times in a single session has not merely practised arithmetic. She has internalised a *way of approaching problems*. She has learned, at a level below conscious strategy, that every complex situation can be entered through the same door: find the condition, decide what it requires, follow the steps.

This is if-then reasoning, and it is the skeleton of every program ever written.

What makes GASING's version of this so effective is that the reasoning is never abstract. The condition is always real: does the top number have enough units to subtract from, or does it not? The decision is always consequential: if yes, proceed; if no, regroup. The child is not learning a rule about rules. She is practising a habit of mind through thousands of small, concrete decisions, until the habit becomes invisible because it has become automatic.

Computational logic emerges from logical-mathematical training when the child no longer needs to be told to check the condition before deciding. She simply does it, in mathematics, and then, without realising the transfer has occurred, everywhere else.

---

**In the Spatial-Visual activity**, the child is asked to do something that most mathematics education never asks of anyone: *see* the number before touching it.

In GASING training, a marble box is not a teaching aid. It is a translation device. It turns an abstract quantity, say, 6 × 8, into something the eye can take in whole: six boxes, eight marbles each, the total distributed visibly across a spatial field. The child who looks at that arrangement is not counting. She is reading a structure. And reading structure is exactly what a programmer does when she looks at a block of code and sees, before executing a single line, what the program will do and why.

This perceptual habit, once formed, does not stay inside the marble box. A child whose spatial intelligence has been developed through GASING begins to see the *shape* of every problem she encounters. She can look at 64 minus 7 and see, in one moment, that the units column needs help before the subtraction can proceed. She is not following a rule. She is reading a diagram that exists only in her mind, but that she has learned to construct with the same clarity as if it were drawn on paper.

This is process-flow visualisation, and it is one of the core capacities Wing (2006) identifies as essential to computational thinking. A programmer who cannot visualise the flow of an algorithm before writing it will write code that surprises her. A child who has been trained by GASING to see the structure of a mathematical operation before executing it has already learned to think one step ahead of the procedure. That habit, transferred into a coding environment, is not a beginner's skill. It is the mark of a programmer who understands what she is doing rather than simply following instructions.

Computational logic emerges from spatial-visual training when the child stops executing steps and starts reading structures, when the procedure becomes, in her mind's eye, a picture she already knows how to interpret before she begins.

---

**In the Intrapersonal activity**, something happens in a GASING session that has no equivalent in a conventional mathematics class. A child gives a wrong answer, in front of everyone. And then, instead of sitting down in shame, she is asked to look at what she did, find where the thinking broke, and fix it.

This is debugging. Not in the language of computer science, but in the most human form it can take.

Every programmer knows that writing code is, in practice, mostly finding out why the code does not work. The skill that separates a programmer who improves from one who stagnates is not the ability to write correct code on the first attempt. It is the ability to look at incorrect code without panic, trace the logic back to the point where it diverged from what was intended, and repair it without losing confidence in the overall design. That skill has a name in software development: debugging. And it requires, before anything technical, a particular psychological disposition. The programmer must be able to look at her own mistake steadily, without defensiveness or distress, as a source of information rather than evidence of inadequacy.

GASING builds exactly this disposition, not through a lesson about how to handle mistakes, but through the repeated lived experience of making them and surviving them well. A child who has practised *mencongak* publicly, who has given a wrong answer in front of the class and been guided to find the error herself, who has experienced that a mistake does not destroy her standing in the room, that child has already rehearsed the most important non-technical skill in programming. She has learned that wrong does not mean stop. It means look more carefully.

Computational logic emerges from intrapersonal training when the child's first response to a wrong answer is not to hide it but to hunt for it. When she turns toward the mistake instead of away from it. That orientation, built through mathematics, is the same orientation that will one day make her a programmer who can fix what she builds.

---

**In the Linguistic activity**, GASING makes a demand that most mathematics education never makes: the child must not only find the answer. She must explain why it is the answer, in words, in order, to someone who does not yet understand.

This sounds like a communication exercise. It is, in fact, a programming exercise in disguise.

A computer program is, at its core, a precise description of a procedure. It tells the machine what to do, in what order, under what conditions, and what to do when those conditions are not met. Every ambiguity in that description is a bug. Every missing step is a failure. The program does exactly what it is told, nothing more and nothing less, which means the person writing it must have thought through every step with a clarity that ordinary conversation never demands.

When a GASING student is asked to explain how she solved 39 + 34, she is being held to exactly this standard. She cannot say "I just knew." She cannot point at the answer and nod. She must say: first I separated the tens, then I separated the units, then I added the tens together, then I added the units together, then I combined the results. Each step must follow the previous one. Each step must be necessary. If she skips a step, the listener gets lost, and the listener getting lost is immediate, visible feedback that the explanation was incomplete.

This is pseudocode. Not in the technical sense, not yet. But in the cognitive sense, it is exactly that: a sequential, unambiguous, step-by-step description of a procedure, expressed in human language, verified by whether another person can follow it to the same result.

The child who has practised this enough times has built something more valuable than mathematical fluency. She has built the linguistic habit of thinking in procedures, of moving through a problem one clear step at a time, of not assuming that the listener already knows what she means. When that child eventually sits in front of a code editor and is asked to translate her thinking into a programming language, she will find that she has already done most of the hardest work. The structure was already there. The language simply becomes more precise.

Computational logic emerges from linguistic training when explaining a procedure becomes indistinguishable from writing it, when the child has learned that a step not stated is a step not done, and that clarity is not a courtesy but a requirement.

---

> **The Four Together**
>
> Logical-mathematical intelligence provides the if-then skeleton. Spatial-visual intelligence provides the ability to read that skeleton as a whole before executing any part of it. Intrapersonal intelligence provides the capacity to turn back when the skeleton breaks, and repair it without losing confidence. Linguistic intelligence provides the ability to make the skeleton visible to another mind, which is the only true test of whether it was ever really understood.
>
> No one of these four produces computational logic alone. Together, through the accumulated pressure of GASING activities that develop all four simultaneously, they produce a child who thinks algorithmically, not because she was taught to, but because every condition for that thinking to emerge has been met.

---

## 9.2.1 Computational Logic: When Mathematics Teaches Children to Think Like Machines

> *"Every time a student solves a subtraction, multiplication, or division problem with the GASING method, he is in fact practising thinking like a programmer: breaking a large problem into small steps that can be executed systematically."*
>
> Core principle of the GASING method

#### A Story Before the Theory

October 2024. Five primary school students from across Indonesia walked onto an international stage in South Korea, at the 9th World Innovative Technology Challenge.

They walked off with gold, silver, and three bronze medals in coding.

Two of them came from Papua. One from Central Kalimantan. One from North Maluku.

What made this remarkable was not the medals themselves. What made it remarkable was the timeline: these children had been learning to code for six months, every Saturday only. No specialist lab, no daily computer class, no accelerated scholarship programme. Just one morning a week.

So where did the ability come from?

Not from the coding lessons. The ability was already there, built in, before anyone had mentioned a keyboard. It had grown quietly inside the way these children had learned mathematics.

That is the story this section tells.

---

#### What Computational Logic Is, and Why Mathematics Can Build It

Computational logic is the capacity to decompose complex problems into algorithmic steps, recognise systematic patterns, and design procedures that can be reliably replicated. These are the foundational skills of computer programming and, more broadly, of systematic thinking in a digital world.

Jeannette Wing (2006), in her landmark paper on computational thinking, identifies four core components of this capacity. The first is *decomposition*: breaking complex problems into smaller, manageable parts. The second is *pattern recognition*: identifying regularities and recurring structures. The third is *abstraction*: setting aside irrelevant detail to focus on the essential logic of a problem. The fourth is *algorithm design*: constructing systematic, replicable steps toward a solution.

For decades, educators and policymakers have treated these skills as the exclusive property of computer science education. The GASING method shows that assumption to be wrong.

---

#### Not Taught. Emergent.

Here is what distinguishes the GASING approach from every other approach that claims to develop computational thinking: computational logic is never directly taught. There is no scheduled "algorithmic thinking" session in the training programme. There is no coding module introduced to children in remote areas who have never seen a computer. There is only mathematics, but mathematics designed with deliberate care to develop multiple intelligences simultaneously.

And from those conditions, computational logic emerges, not as a taught skill, but as an inevitable consequence of the right design.

This is what researchers call an *emergent property*: a capability that arises not from direct instruction, but from the conditions the right design creates. Table 9.17 shows how each intelligence developed through the GASING method contributes to a specific component of Wing's framework.

**Table 9.17.** Intelligence Types Developed Through GASING and Their Contributions to Computational Logic.

| Intelligence Developed | GASING Activity | Contribution to Computational Logic |
|---|---|---|
| Logical-Mathematical | Step-by-step operations; conditional reasoning (check condition, then decide) | Emerges when sequential, step-by-step analysis becomes habitual: if-then reasoning appears naturally as a thinking pattern |
| Spatial-Visual | Visualising the flow of operations; seeing patterns in number sequences | Emerges when the mind learns to "see" structure: the ability to mentally represent process flow and algorithmic architecture surfaces on its own |
| Intrapersonal | Reflecting on one's own thought processes; detecting and correcting one's own errors | Emerges when self-monitoring becomes routine: the capacity to trace, identify, and repair errors in one's own thinking, the cognitive equivalent of debugging, grows from within |
| Linguistic | Explaining steps with precision and in correct sequence | Emerges when articulating a procedure out loud becomes natural: the ability to express an algorithm clearly, analogous to writing executable pseudocode, follows as a direct consequence |

---

#### Supplementary Note 9.17: Why Computational Logic Emerges, Not Is Taught

**In the Logical-Mathematical activity**, GASING trains something that looks, on the surface, like arithmetic practice. A child works through a subtraction. Then another. Then another. The numbers change; the structure does not. And that is precisely the point.

Every BAKALKUBAGI operation in the GASING method is a procedure with a fixed architecture: check the condition, make a decision, execute the steps in order, produce the result. The child who has worked through this structure fifty times in a single session has not merely practised arithmetic. She has internalised a *way of approaching problems*. She has learned, at a level below conscious strategy, that every complex situation can be entered through the same door: find the condition, decide what it requires, follow the steps.

This is if-then reasoning, and it is the skeleton of every program ever written.

What makes GASING's version of this so effective is that the reasoning is never abstract. The condition is always real: does the top number have enough units to subtract from, or does it not? The decision is always consequential: if yes, proceed; if no, regroup. The child is not learning a rule about rules. She is practising a habit of mind through thousands of small, concrete decisions, until the habit becomes invisible because it has become automatic.

Computational logic emerges from logical-mathematical training when the child no longer needs to be told to check the condition before deciding. She simply does it, in mathematics, and then, without realising the transfer has occurred, everywhere else.

---

**In the Spatial-Visual activity**, the child is asked to do something that most mathematics education never asks of anyone: *see* the number before touching it.

In GASING training, a marble box is not a teaching aid. It is a translation device. It turns an abstract quantity, say 6 × 8, into something the eye can take in whole: six boxes, eight marbles each, the total distributed visibly across a spatial field. The child who looks at that arrangement is not counting. She is reading a structure. And reading structure is exactly what a programmer does when she looks at a block of code and sees, before executing a single line, what the program will do and why.

This perceptual habit, once formed, does not stay inside the marble box. A child whose spatial intelligence has been developed through GASING begins to see the *shape* of every problem she encounters. She can look at 64 minus 7 and see, in one moment, that the units column needs help before the subtraction can proceed. She is not following a rule. She is reading a diagram that exists only in her mind, but that she has learned to construct with the same clarity as if it were drawn on paper.

This is process-flow visualisation, and it is one of the core capacities Wing (2006) identifies as essential to computational thinking. A programmer who cannot visualise the flow of an algorithm before writing it will write code that surprises her. A child who has been trained by GASING to see the structure of a mathematical operation before executing it has already learned to think one step ahead of the procedure. That habit, transferred into a coding environment, is not a beginner's skill. It is the mark of a programmer who understands what she is doing rather than simply following instructions.

Computational logic emerges from spatial-visual training when the child stops executing steps and starts reading structures: when the procedure becomes, in her mind's eye, a picture she already knows how to interpret before she begins.

---

**In the Intrapersonal activity**, something happens in a GASING session that has no equivalent in a conventional mathematics class. A child gives a wrong answer, in front of everyone. And then, instead of sitting down in shame, she is asked to look at what she did, find where the thinking broke, and fix it.

This is debugging. Not in the language of computer science, but in the most human form it can take.

Every programmer knows that writing code is, in practice, mostly finding out why the code does not work. The skill that separates a programmer who improves from one who stagnates is not the ability to write correct code on the first attempt. It is the ability to look at incorrect code without panic, trace the logic back to the point where it diverged from what was intended, and repair it without losing confidence in the overall design. That skill has a name in software development: debugging. And it requires, before anything technical, a particular psychological disposition. The programmer must be able to look at her own mistake steadily, without defensiveness or distress, as a source of information rather than evidence of inadequacy.

GASING builds exactly this disposition, not through a lesson about how to handle mistakes, but through the repeated lived experience of making them and surviving them well. A child who has practised *mencongak* publicly, who has given a wrong answer in front of the class and been guided to find the error herself, who has experienced that a mistake does not destroy her standing in the room: that child has already rehearsed the most important non-technical skill in programming. She has learned that wrong does not mean stop. It means look more carefully.

Computational logic emerges from intrapersonal training when the child's first response to a wrong answer is not to hide it but to hunt for it. When she turns toward the mistake instead of away from it. That orientation, built through mathematics, is the same orientation that will one day make her a programmer who can fix what she builds.

---

**In the Linguistic activity**, GASING makes a demand that most mathematics education never makes: the child must not only find the answer. She must explain why it is the answer, in words, in order, to someone who does not yet understand.

This sounds like a communication exercise. It is, in fact, a programming exercise in disguise.

A computer program is, at its core, a precise description of a procedure. It tells the machine what to do, in what order, under what conditions, and what to do when those conditions are not met. Every ambiguity in that description is a bug. Every missing step is a failure. The program does exactly what it is told, nothing more and nothing less, which means the person writing it must have thought through every step with a clarity that ordinary conversation never demands.

When a GASING student is asked to explain how she solved 39 + 34, she is being held to exactly this standard. She cannot say "I just knew." She cannot point at the answer and nod. She must say: first I separated the tens, then I separated the units, then I added the tens together, then I added the units together, then I combined the results. Each step must follow the previous one. Each step must be necessary. If she skips a step, the listener gets lost, and the listener getting lost is immediate, visible feedback that the explanation was incomplete.

This is pseudocode. Not in the technical sense, not yet. But in the cognitive sense, it is exactly that: a sequential, unambiguous, step-by-step description of a procedure, expressed in human language, verified by whether another person can follow it to the same result.

The child who has practised this enough times has built something more valuable than mathematical fluency. She has built the linguistic habit of thinking in procedures, of moving through a problem one clear step at a time, of not assuming that the listener already knows what she means. When that child eventually sits in front of a code editor and is asked to translate her thinking into a programming language, she will find that she has already done most of the hardest work. The structure was already there. The language simply becomes more precise.

Computational logic emerges from linguistic training when explaining a procedure becomes indistinguishable from writing it: when the child has learned that a step not stated is a step not done, and that clarity is not a courtesy but a requirement.

---

> **The Four Together**
>
> Logical-mathematical intelligence provides the if-then skeleton. Spatial-visual intelligence provides the ability to read that skeleton as a whole before executing any part of it. Intrapersonal intelligence provides the capacity to turn back when the skeleton breaks, and repair it without losing confidence. Linguistic intelligence provides the ability to make the skeleton visible to another mind, which is the only true test of whether it was ever really understood.
>
> No single one of these four produces computational logic alone. Together, through the accumulated pressure of GASING activities that develop all four simultaneously, they produce a child who thinks algorithmically: not because she was taught to, but because every condition for that thinking to emerge has been met.

---

#### The Algorithm Hidden in a Simple Subtraction

To see how completely this algorithmic thinking is embedded in every GASING operation, consider 64 minus 7. This apparently simple subtraction is, in fact, a complete algorithm: it has a condition to be checked, a decision to be made, a sequence of ordered steps to follow, and a determinate output.

*(See Figure 9.22: The 64−7 algorithm: step-by-step decomposition matching computational thinking.)*

The flow mirrors computer programming logic exactly. There is an if-then condition, a decision branch, an ordered execution sequence, and a result. GASING children practise this pattern dozens of times every day across every BAKALKUBAGI operation, without ever realising they are training themselves to think like programmers.

The computational logic skills built this way do not stay confined to subtraction. They become the cognitive infrastructure for everything that follows: multiplying three-digit numbers mentally, computing quadratic roots without a formula, and a range of operations that initially seem impossible to perform in one's head. But more importantly, they establish a *habit of mind* that transfers well beyond mathematics altogether. The child who has learned to decompose 64−7 into its logical steps has learned, without anyone using the word, what an algorithm feels like from the inside.

That habit does not arrive from a single operation. It accumulates across four distinct mechanisms, each recruiting a different intelligence, each adding a different layer to the same emerging capability. Table 9.19 maps these mechanisms against Wing's (2006) framework and shows precisely where the correspondence lies.

---

#### Alignment with Wing's Computational Thinking Framework (2006)

The table below makes the correspondence explicit. This is not an analogy being stretched to fit a theory. It is a point-for-point alignment between what Wing's framework identifies as the components of computational thinking and what GASING activities reliably produce.

**Table 9.19.** Computational Thinking Components (Wing, 2006) and Their Implementation in the GASING Method.

| Component (Wing, 2006) | Implementation in the GASING Method |
|---|---|
| Decomposition | Complex operations are broken into small sequential steps: 64−7, for instance, is decomposed into check condition, separate tens, subtract, recombine, state result. |
| Pattern Recognition | The multiplication sequence based on logical pattern (1→10→2→9→5→squares→3→4→8→7→6); square-number patterns; complement-of-ten pairs in addition. |
| Abstraction | Square-number patterns for 41–50 and 51–60 build generalisation capacity: finding the general rule from specific cases, then applying it to any number. |
| Algorithm Design | Every BAKALKUBAGI operation is a step-by-step procedure that can be replicated consistently, exactly like an algorithm executable by a computer. |

The policy implications of this mapping are direct. If the foundations of computational thinking can be built naturally through well-designed mathematics learning, then access to computers is not the precondition it has long been assumed to be. Children in the most remote areas of the archipelago, with no computers in their classrooms at all, can still develop the algorithmic thinking that the digital era demands.

---

#### From the Highlands of Papua to the International Stage: Field Evidence

The four videos below document how computational logic surfaces naturally across radically different locations in the Indonesian archipelago, from Central Papua to Bali, from the Papua Highlands to Morotai Island in North Maluku.

*(See Figure 9.23: Student, Puncak Regency, Central Papua, explains 39+34 using P and S notation.)*

In this first video, children in Puncak Regency, Central Papua, who previously could not perform addition, explain 39 + 34 with striking systematic clarity. The steps they articulate are decompose, group, add, combine. This is not merely arithmetic. This is coding expressed through mathematics. The algorithmic thinking pattern they are building here will serve them in the digital era.

*(See Figure 9.24: Students, Lanny Jaya, Papua Highlands, demonstrate 7+5 with collaborative algorithmic understanding.)*

In the second video, children from Lanny Jaya in the Papua Highlands demonstrate 7 + 5 using concrete manipulatives. They collaborate, correct one another, and build logical-mathematical thinking patterns together, all without any awareness that their computational logic is growing in the process. These children previously could not calculate 4 + 3. From unable to add 4 + 3 to demonstrating 7 + 5 with deep understanding: the transformation is extraordinary.

*(See Figure 9.25: Students, Badung Regency, Bali, understand the conceptual difference between 6×8 and 8×6.)*

Children in Badung Regency, Bali, in the third video do not merely memorise 6 × 8 = 48. They understand it. They know that 6 × 8, meaning six groups each containing eight objects, is conceptually different from 8 × 6, meaning eight groups each containing six, even though the products are equal. Their explanation follows the path from concrete objects to semi-abstract representation to pure abstraction: the exact progression that builds genuine computational logic, arising naturally from the right learning process.

*(See Figure 9.26: Esra, Morotai Island, North Maluku, advances from tally-mark counting to systematic multiplication.)*

In the fourth video, Esra, a student from Morotai Island, North Maluku, undergoes a remarkable transformation. She once calculated multiplication by counting tally marks one by one, a slow and mechanistic process. She now works with a logical, systematic, and far more efficient flow of thinking. This is precisely the kind of structured cognition that generates computational logic as a natural by-product: the foundation for facing a digital era with confidence.

Across all four videos, the same pattern holds: children from the historically most under-served regions of Indonesian education display remarkably strong algorithmic thinking. The evidence challenges a widespread assumption directly. Computational thinking is not the exclusive privilege of urban children with computers and coding tutors. With the right learning design, this foundation can be built anywhere in Indonesia, including the places that have been left furthest behind.

---

#### From Mental Arithmetic to the World Stage

The most compelling evidence that computational logic transfers directly into programming ability came in October 2024, at the *9th World Innovative Technology (WIT) Challenge* in South Korea.

---

**9th World Innovative Technology (WIT) Challenge**
*South Korea, October 2024*

- **Gold:** Felicia Dahayu, Banyuwangi, East Java
- **Silver:** Jose Norotouw, Jayapura City, Papua
- **Bronze:** Cressya Wianopa, Gunung Mas, Central Kalimantan
- **Bronze:** Esra Samuel Weyai, Biak Numfor, Papua
- **Bronze:** Uril Algifari, Central Halmahera, North Maluku

*All five primary school students, including those from 3T (frontier, outer, and disadvantaged) regions, had been learning coding from zero for only six months, every Saturday only.*

---

A contextual note is in order: the WIT Challenge is not the equivalent of the International Mathematical Olympiad, and it should not be presented as such. But that is precisely the point. For children to compete credibly on any international stage after only six months of Saturday-only lessons, technical coding skill alone is not sufficient. They need a firmly established foundation of algorithmic thinking built long before the first lesson. That is exactly what the GASING method had provided.

**Table 9.20.** The Transformation in Students' Computational Logic Capacity After GASING Training.

| Before Training | After Training |
|---|---|
| Memorises procedures without understanding the underlying pattern or logic | Algorithmic thinking: able to generalise patterns and transfer skills to new domains |
| Dependent on rote memorisation; easily lost under pressure | Possesses a strong thinking foundation, enabling very rapid absorption of coding |
| Cannot explain the procedure used; only knows "the answer is correct" | Able to articulate steps algorithmically, analogous to writing pseudocode |

*(See Figure 9.32: Transformation pathway: computational logic as emergent property leading to international achievement.)*

The transformation documented above is not merely a performance shift. It reflects something deeper: a move from memorisation toward genuine understanding, from procedure without meaning toward algorithms that can be explained and defended, and from dependence on the teacher toward independent thinking. That is precisely the cognitive profile most needed by the generation that will live and work in the era of artificial intelligence.

---

> **Key Principle: Computational Logic in GASING Is an Emergent Property**
>
> Computational logic emerges when logical-mathematical, spatial-visual, intrapersonal, and linguistic intelligences are developed simultaneously through multimodal activities.
>
> Not: *"Let us teach coding."*
>
> But: *"Let us build the foundation of algorithmic thinking through mathematics, and coding ability will emerge on its own."*
