## 9.2.3 Culture: The Identity That Makes Learning Possible

> *"Students do not need to shed their cultural identity in order to excel at mathematics. On the contrary, cultural richness becomes an asset in learning."*
>
> Pedagogical principle of the GASING method

There is an assumption buried deep in Indonesia's educational system, rarely stated but consistently acted upon: that becoming educated means becoming less local. That the path toward intelligence runs away from regional language, away from traditional games, away from the rhythms and rituals that a community has carried for generations.

The GASING method challenges this assumption not with argument but with evidence. Evidence that can be watched, heard, and felt.

---

#### What Culture Actually Is

Culture is more than regional songs, traditional costumes, or customs performed at celebrations. It operates at a far deeper level: as a system of meaning that shapes how a community interprets the world, regards authority, and values knowledge. In a learning context, culture determines whether a child feels that the learning space belongs to him, or to someone else entirely.

Within the 8C framework of the GASING method, Culture is understood across three mutually reinforcing layers.

The first is **local expression in learning**: regional language, regional songs, traditional movements, and familiar visual symbols used as vehicles for conveying mathematical content.

The second is the **recognition that local modes of thinking are valid**: not merely tolerance of difference but the active conviction that a community's cognitive richness is a learning asset, not an obstacle to be overcome.

The third, and most fundamental layer, is **a complete identity as a foundation for learning**: a child who is proud of who he is learns faster, more boldly, and further than a child who feels he must shed his identity before he can be considered intelligent.

*(See Figure 9.46: GASING training session with rich local cultural elements.)*

UNESCO (2006) emphasises the importance of culturally responsive pedagogy, an approach that acknowledges that learning is most effective when meaningfully connected to students' cultural context. Ladson-Billings (1995) demonstrates that this approach increases engagement, academic self-confidence, and achievement, particularly for students from cultural backgrounds that have long been under-recognised in formal education systems. For Indonesia's context, with more than 700 regional languages and thousands of living local wisdoms, the relevance of this principle is not merely academic. It is a necessity.

---

#### Culture: Emergent, Not Instructed

Cultural competency in the GASING method does not appear as a separate subject, nor does it need to.

It emerges naturally when multiple intelligences meet with pride in local identity: when a teacher from Lanny Jaya sings the Complement-of-10 song to the melody he has known since childhood, or when a student in Maybrat explains division to a classmate in her mother tongue and sees that classmate immediately understand. In moments like these, culture is not merely preserved. It is put to work.

What makes this emergence possible is the convergence of competencies already formed through GASING training: Creativity to arrange the melody and lyrics to a regional rhythm; Communication to convey a mathematical concept in the most familiar language; Collaboration in the process of co-creation between teachers, students, and the community; and Character in the courage to express one's identity without shame. Culture, in this sense, is the destination of all that has been built before.

**Table 9.24.** Intelligence Types Developed Through GASING and Their Contributions to Cultural Competency.

| Intelligence Type | GASING Activity | Contribution to Cultural Competency |
|---|---|---|
| Musical | Creating Complement-of-10 songs with local regional rhythms | Emerges when a mathematical concept finds its way into the melody a community has always known: cultural expression becomes a learning vehicle, and the learning vehicle becomes cultural pride |
| Linguistic | Microteaching in regional languages (Lanny, Maybrat, Sundanese, Batak, etc.) | Emerges when the mother tongue is treated as an accelerator rather than a barrier: a child who explains mathematics in the language she thinks in does not merely understand more deeply, she receives the signal that her language belongs in the space of knowledge |
| Interpersonal | Collaboration with the community in creating local content | Emerges when learning is built with the community rather than delivered to it: the integration of local wisdom becomes natural, because the people who carry that wisdom are inside the process, not outside it |
| Spatial-Visual | Using local objects (areca nut, fish, leaves) as concrete manipulatives | Emerges when the objects of everyday life become the instruments of mathematical thinking: a child who counts areca nuts learns that mathematics was already present in her world before anyone taught her the word |
| Bodily-Kinaesthetic | Integration of traditional games (Dina Becik, *hompimpa*, local suits) | Emerges when the body remembers what the culture has always known: traditional movement becomes a medium for learning, and the learning strengthens the tradition that carries it |
| Intrapersonal | Cultural identity reflection; pride in one's roots | Emerges when a child discovers that who she is, where she comes from, and what her community has always valued, is not an obstacle to learning but the very ground it grows from: "I am proud of my culture" becomes not a declaration but a foundation |

The spectrum of intelligences in this table reflects the nature of culture itself: it is not a phenomenon that operates only through words or thoughts but through every human dimension. Music, movement, everyday objects, language, social relationships, and self-awareness are all channels through which culture flows. The GASING method opens all those channels simultaneously. That is why cultural competency does not need to be taught separately: it is already present in the same water where all the other learning happens.

---

#### Supplementary Note 9.24: Why Cultural Competency Emerges, Not Is Taught

**In the Musical activity**, something happens when a teacher adapts the Complement-of-10 song to the melody his community has sung for generations: he does not simplify the mathematics. He gives it a home.

The mathematical content, the number pairs that sum to ten, remains exactly what it is. But the vehicle that carries it is no longer foreign. It moves to a rhythm the children's bodies already know, in a melodic shape their ears have heard since before they could speak. And a concept that arrives in a familiar form does not need to fight for attention. It is already welcome.

This is how musical intelligence causes cultural competency to emerge rather than be taught. The teacher who adapts the song is not following an instruction to "incorporate culture into learning." He is doing what any skilled communicator does: reaching for the most direct path between an idea and its recipient. And in Indonesia, with its extraordinary musical diversity, that direct path runs through the community's own musical tradition.

Cultural competency emerges from musical intelligence when the melody of learning and the melody of belonging become the same melody: when a child can no longer tell, and no longer needs to tell, whether she is singing her community's song or her mathematics lesson.

---

**In the Linguistic activity**, the most consequential thing the mother tongue does in a GASING session is not accelerate comprehension. It sends a message.

When a student from Lanny Jaya explains a subtraction problem in the Lanny language, and her classmates understand immediately, the mathematical content is confirmed. But something else is also confirmed, something that formal education in Indonesia has for too long denied: that this language, in this room, is sufficient. That the knowledge these children are building does not require them to think in someone else's linguistic framework. That intelligence in the Lanny language is intelligence, without qualification.

This is the message that changes a child's relationship with learning permanently. Not "you can use your language as a stepping stone to the real language." But: "your language is already adequate for the highest demands of mathematical thinking." Once a child has received that message from direct personal experience, the distance between her cultural identity and her academic identity collapses. She does not need to choose between being Lanny and being a mathematics student. She is both, in the same moment, using the same words.

Cultural competency emerges from linguistic intelligence when the mother tongue stops being a concession and starts being a source of pride: when a child who was asked to explain in her own language realises, in the act of explaining, that her own language was never the obstacle it was treated as.

---

**In the Interpersonal activity**, the most important thing about community collaboration in the GASING method is not that it produces locally relevant content. It is that it reverses the direction of authority.

In most educational encounters, knowledge flows one way: from a system to a community. The curriculum is designed elsewhere. The songs are composed elsewhere. The examples come from a world that may bear little resemblance to the world the children actually inhabit. The community receives; it does not contribute.

When GASING training participants work with their community to create local content, this dynamic is inverted. The community's musical traditions, its games, its objects, its ways of counting and measuring, are not merely tolerated as context. They are treated as resources that the educational encounter could not be as effective without. The elders who know the Dina Becik song are not obstacles to modern learning. They are collaborators in it.

This inversion matters beyond its practical effects on learning quality. It communicates to the community, and to the children within it, that local wisdom is not a past to be left behind in order to move forward. It is a foundation that the future can be built on. Cultural competency emerges from interpersonal intelligence when the community stops being the audience for education and becomes one of its authors.

---

**In the Spatial-Visual activity**, the areca nut is not a substitute for a proper manipulative. It is a better one.

A child from Papua who is asked to count plastic blocks is working with objects that carry no meaning in her life outside the classroom. The counting is real, but the objects are inert. A child who is asked to count areca nuts is working with objects she has seen her whole life, objects that have weight and texture and smell and cultural significance that extends far beyond this lesson. The counting is real, and the objects are alive with everything she already knows.

This is how spatial-visual intelligence causes cultural competency to emerge. The familiar object does not merely aid visualisation of the mathematical concept. It connects the mathematical concept to a web of existing knowledge and meaning that makes it more memorable, more transferable, and more genuinely the child's own. When the manipulative is something from the child's world, the mathematics it demonstrates belongs to that world too.

Cultural competency emerges from spatial-visual intelligence when a child can look at an areca nut and see, simultaneously, an object from her culture and a unit of mathematical thinking: when there is no longer a boundary between the world she comes from and the world of knowledge she is entering.

---

**In the Bodily-Kinaesthetic activity**, traditional games do something that no designed educational activity can fully replicate: they carry the authority of everything the culture has already endorsed.

When Tengger children play the mathematics version of Dina Becik, they are not doing something new. They are doing something ancient, in a new way. The rhythm their bodies move to is the same rhythm their grandparents moved to. The song is the same song. The only thing that has changed is that the content of the game now includes multiplication and subtraction. And because the game itself is already trusted, already beloved, already woven into the community's sense of who they are, the mathematics it contains inherits that trust.

This is the mechanism by which bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence causes cultural competency to emerge. The body does not learn mathematics despite the traditional game. It learns mathematics through the traditional game, and in doing so it strengthens the tradition that the body already knew. Nothing is sacrificed. Both identities, as an inheritor of ancestral culture and as a competent mathematics learner, are reinforced in the same movement.

Cultural competency emerges from bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence when the body discovers that it does not need to move differently to think mathematically: that the movements it has always known are already adequate to carry the most demanding intellectual content.

---

**In the Intrapersonal activity**, the transformation is the quietest and the deepest: a child stops apologising for who she is.

This is not a small thing. For a child from a community whose language has never appeared in a textbook, whose traditional games have never been mentioned in a curriculum, whose local heroes have never featured in a mathematics problem, the implicit message of formal education has been consistent and corrosive: the things that make you who you are have no place here. To learn, you must first become someone else.

The GASING method contradicts this message, consistently and specifically, through every cultural adaptation it encourages. When a child's own songs are used to teach mathematics, when her own language is the language of instruction, when her own community's games are the vehicle for learning, she receives a different implicit message: everything you are is welcome here. Your culture is not a costume to be worn at the school door and left outside. It is part of what makes you capable of learning.

The intrapersonal response to this message is a shift in the foundation of self-concept. A child who has been told, through direct experience rather than through words, that her cultural identity is an asset rather than a liability, does not merely become a more confident mathematics student. She becomes a more integrated human being: someone whose sense of herself and whose intellectual life are no longer in tension, because both are rooted in the same ground.

Cultural competency emerges from intrapersonal intelligence when a child's pride in her culture and her confidence in her learning become the same pride: when "I am Tengger" and "I am good at mathematics" are not two separate claims but one.

---

> **The Six Together**
>
> Musical intelligence gives the mathematics a melody the community already knows. Linguistic intelligence gives it a language the child already thinks in. Interpersonal intelligence gives the community a role in building it. Spatial-visual intelligence gives it objects the child's hands have already held. Bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence gives it movements the child's body already knows. And intrapersonal intelligence gives the child the conviction that who she already is, is exactly enough.
>
> No single one of these six produces cultural competency alone. Together, they produce something that no curriculum directive has ever successfully manufactured: a child who is proud of where she comes from and confident in where she is going, who does not experience these as two different directions, because the GASING method has shown her, through direct personal experience, that they are the same direction.

---

#### Mechanisms of Cultural Competency Emergence

**Table 9.25.** Mechanisms of Cultural Competency Development in the GASING Method.

| Mechanism | Intelligence Developed | How Cultural Competency Emerges |
|---|---|---|
| Regional song adaptation | Musical, Linguistic, Interpersonal | Emerges when one mathematical concept finds hundreds of cultural expressions: the Complement-of-10 song adapts to every local melody from Sabang to Merauke, and each version is a community's declaration that this knowledge is theirs |
| Use of regional languages | Linguistic, Intrapersonal | Emerges when the mother tongue is treated as an accelerator: microteaching in Lanny, Maybrat, Sundanese, and Batak removes a barrier that should never have existed and replaces it with the most efficient possible path to understanding |
| Local objects as manipulatives | Spatial-Visual, Bodily-Kinaesthetic | Emerges when the objects of everyday life become the instruments of mathematical thinking: areca nuts, fish, and local leaves carry cultural weight that plastic blocks never could, and that weight makes the mathematics more real |
| Traditional games | Bodily-Kinaesthetic, Interpersonal, Musical | Emerges when tradition becomes a living medium: *hompimpa*, local suit games, and Dina Becik do not compromise the mathematics they carry, and the mathematics does not compromise the tradition that carries it |

Of these four mechanisms, the use of regional languages is the most consistently underestimated and the most impactful. When a student understands a concept in her mother tongue, she does not merely receive new knowledge. She receives the signal that this knowledge is for her, that the academic world does not require her to shed who she is as a prerequisite for entry. That signal, once received, permanently changes a child's relationship with learning.

And local objects as manipulatives convey a similar message: mathematics is not something that arrives from elsewhere and must be accepted with deference. It is already here, in the areca nuts that can be counted, in the weaving that forms geometric patterns, in the rhythm of drumbeats that divides time with precision. That is what is meant by culture as contextual intelligence: not an ornament that decorates learning, but a foundation that gives it roots.

---

#### The Complement-of-10 Song: One Concept, Thousands of Cultural Colours

If there is one piece of evidence most clearly demonstrating that cultural competency in the GASING method is an emergent property rather than something commanded from above, it is the Complement-of-10 Song. There is no national instruction specifying how this song must be sung. There is no uniform guide for its melody, language, or movement. What exists is one mathematical concept, the number pairs that sum to ten (1+9, 2+8, 3+7, 4+6, 5+5), and one invitation to each community to express it in its own way.

*(See Figure 9.47: The Complement-of-10 concept: one mathematical truth, waiting for a thousand cultural expressions.)*

The result is something that cannot be engineered: hundreds of versions of the song born spontaneously from all corners of Indonesia, each carrying the colour, rhythm, and spirit of its own culture. This is what happens when musical, linguistic, interpersonal, and intrapersonal intelligences are developed simultaneously in an environment that genuinely values local identity: culture does not need to be taught, because it already lives within every participant, waiting to be invited out.

**Table 9.26.** Complement-of-10 Song from Across the Indonesian Archipelago.

*[Table with all 11 videos preserved as in original: Sabang, Siak, Badung, West Manggarai, Singkawang, IKN, Parigi Moutong, Teluk Bintuni, Lanny Jaya, Banyuwangi, Pasuruan — with all images and QR codes]*

Listen to all eleven videos in sequence, and what is felt is not uniformity but richness. In Aceh, the song flows with a gentle Malay accent. In the Papua Highlands, the notes carry the distinctiveness of the high country. In Bali, the ghost of gamelan is heard behind the melody. In Banyuwangi, there is the driving spirit of Osing. Different accents, the same enthusiasm. Identical concept, expression equally rich with the culture that gave birth to it.

This is what the founders of the nation meant by ***Bhinneka Tunggal Ika*** (Unity in Diversity), and the GASING method demonstrates that this motto is not only possible to live within mathematics education, but becomes its greatest strength.

When a child from Lanny Jaya sings the Complement-of-10 Song with the melody she inherited from her community, she is not merely learning mathematics. She is affirming that she, with her entire cultural background, has the right to be present in that space of knowledge: that her identity is not an obstacle to being intelligent but is actually part of what makes her intelligent.

---

#### Traditional Games: When Tradition Becomes a Medium for Learning

Another example of cultural competency as an emergent property is how traditional local games are spontaneously adopted by participants to make mathematics more enjoyable and meaningful. There is no central instruction directing them to do so.

What occurs is something more organic: when the learning space feels safe and local identity is valued, participants naturally reach for what they know best and use it as a vehicle for the concepts they are learning. The most prominent example is the Dina Becik game from the Tengger people on the slopes of Mount Bromo.

*(See Figure 9.48: Students from the Tengger community playing Dina Becik adapted for mathematics, Pasuruan.)*

*Video: On the slopes of Bromo, the Tengger people sing "Dina Becik" not only for ceremonies but to learn mathematics. Modernisation does not have to sacrifice tradition: both can proceed in parallel. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Ghv6w4tdu5c*

*(See Figure 9.49: The Dina Becik game structure adapted for numeracy.)*

"Dina Becik" (Good Day) is a traditional song of the Tengger people, one of the isolated communities of East Java inhabiting the Bromo highlands. GASING training participants adapted this tradition into a mathematics game: the rhythm and movement already familiar to the ears and bodies of Tengger children now serves as a medium for learning numeracy.

When Tengger children play the mathematics version of Dina Becik while wearing traditional dress, they simultaneously strengthen two identities: as inheritors of ancestral culture and as competent mathematics learners. Nothing is sacrificed. Both actually reinforce each other.

*(See Figure 9.50: Children of Tolikara, Papua Highlands, in traditional dress singing GASING songs in English.)*

*Video: Cultural pride and mathematical excellence in Tolikara, Papua Highlands. Children perform in traditional dress while singing GASING songs in English. Two sources of pride in one spirit. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HhHuw7qnfR4*

In this video, children from Tolikara Regency, Papua Highlands, wear traditional Papuan dress at a GASING training event. A banner reading "Tolikara Regency Is Good at Arithmetic" forms a meaningful backdrop. Remarkably, these children cheerfully sing GASING songs in English, a language considered difficult even by students in major cities.

This phenomenon breaks an assumption that has been allowed to persist for too long: that children from remote areas have limitations in learning things considered "advanced," and that local cultural identity must be compromised in exchange for academic progress. In Tolikara, traditional dress and English-language songs stand side by side without tension. Pride in ancestral heritage and academic excellence are not two mutually exclusive options but two wings that fly together.

**Table 9.27.** Examples of Cultural Adaptation in the GASING Method Across Indonesia.

| Region | Form of Cultural Adaptation in the GASING Method |
|---|---|
| Banyuwangi | The Complement-of-10 Song adapted with the distinctive Osing rhythm |
| Ambon | Teachers in traditional dress sing the GASING song in a Maluku version at the jetty |
| Tolikara | Children sing GASING songs in English while wearing traditional Papuan dress |
| Langsa, Aceh | Microteaching in the regional Acehnese language |
| Papua (various regions) | Local objects (areca nut, fish, leaves) as concrete manipulatives |
| Biak | "Mathematics and Culture Festival" with traditional dress and accessories |

*(See Figure 9.51: Biak Mathematics and Culture Festival.)*

Six regions, six different ways. Yet all converge on the same reality: when mathematics arrives in a familiar face, in a language that is understood, in a rhythm that has been heard before the word "mathematics" was first spoken, it no longer feels foreign. It feels like one's own. And knowledge that feels like one's own is far easier to hold, develop, and pass on to the next generation.

---

#### Local Language: An Accelerator of Understanding

There has long been a mistaken assumption that regional languages are a barrier to formal learning. The GASING method proves the opposite: when a concept is conveyed in the most familiar mother tongue, understanding becomes far faster and deeper. Regional language is not a barrier. It is an accelerator.

*(See Figure 9.52: Student from Lanny Jaya, flower crown, teaches classmates in her mother tongue.)*

*Video: When mathematics speaks in the mother tongue. A female student from Lanny Jaya, flower crown on her head, confidence on her face, mother tongue on her lips, teaches classmates not in a foreign language but in the language they heard from their mothers. The result: instant and deep understanding. Source: https://youtu.be/1yWrX4dwr5Y*

What is most striking about this video is not what is said but how it is said. The Lanny Jaya student performs without stiffness, without hesitation, without the long pauses that typically signal someone struggling to find the right word in a language not her own. She speaks with a natural flow because she speaks in the language that grew with her. And her classmates, hearing the explanation in the same language, do not need to expend cognitive energy on translation. They understand immediately. That is what is meant by an accelerator: not simplifying the concept but removing a barrier that should never have existed.

*(See Figure 9.53: Teacher from Sumedang: from memorise the times table to "hayu urang nyanyi.")*

*Video: From Sumedang: "hayu urang nyanyi!" Warm Sundanese as a bridge; a familiar atmosphere as a vehicle. Mathematics feels like playing with family. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GSH802Dub2E*

The teacher from Sumedang Regency in this video did not wait for permission from the national curriculum to innovate. He took the language he loves most, created his own song, and used it to teach two-digit multiplication. The result is visible in the faces of the children listening: no tension, no fear of making errors, no distance between them and the material being learned. When mathematics arrives in a voice that sounds like home, it no longer feels like a lesson. It feels like a conversation. And people find conversations far easier to remember than lectures.

*(See Figure 9.54: Trainer Jessica learning the Dogiyai language, Central Papua, for her students.)*

*Video: Unlimited dedication: Trainer Jessica learns the Dogiyai language, Central Papua. Not the children being forced to learn a new language, but the trainer making the effort to learn theirs. A language bridge is a bridge of understanding. Source: https://youtu.be/trsn4AkKiwY*

Something is reversed from the usual in this video, and that is precisely what makes it so powerful. It is not the children who are asked to adjust to an unfamiliar medium of instruction. It is not they who must work harder to understand something conveyed not in their language. The opposite occurs: Trainer Jessica, who came from outside and could easily have taught in Indonesian and considered that sufficient, chose to learn the Dogiyai language during the training. That choice was not an obligation. No one required it. She did it because she understood that reaching someone means meeting them where they stand, not where you stand.

An act like this is the meeting of two competencies at once. **Culture**, in the sensitivity to context and local identity that must be respected, meets **Compassion**, in the willingness to inconvenience oneself for the ease of others. When both are present in a trainer, what occurs is not merely a transfer of mathematical knowledge. What occurs is a transfer of trust: that this person has truly come for them.

---

> **Cultural identity and academic excellence can and do go hand in hand.**
>
> There has long been a mistaken assumption embedded deep within Indonesia's educational system: that to become intelligent and modern, children must leave their culture behind. That regional languages are barriers to be overcome. That tradition is a past to be released in order to step forward.
>
> This assumption is not only scientifically wrong. It is also dangerous, because it asks children to pay for progress at a price that is too high: their own identity.
>
> *The GASING method proves otherwise, not with argument but with evidence that can be watched, heard, and felt.*

Tengger children who play the mathematics version of Dina Becik do not lose their Tengger identity; they actually strengthen it while mastering numeracy. Students from Lanny Jaya who explain division in the Lanny language do not become less competent; they actually understand more deeply because they learn in the language most native to how they think. A teacher from Banyuwangi who creates a Complement-of-10 Song with the Osing rhythm is not behind the times; he is an innovator proving that tradition and progress are not two tracks running in opposite directions.

This is GASING's vision of Indonesian education: a generation that masters global competencies without losing its cultural roots, that can compete at the international level while remaining proud of its local identity, that does not need to choose between being advanced and being cultured because it can be both at once.

*Not homogenising, but celebrating. Not erasing, but elevating. Not choosing, but weaving together.*

---

> *"Indonesian children who excel at mathematics and are proud of their culture: that is the golden generation we are building."*
>
> GASING's vision for Indonesia

---

> **Key Principle: Culture in GASING Is an Emergent Property**
>
> Cultural competency cannot be taught through a separate Cultural Education subject. It emerges when multiple intelligences, musical, linguistic, interpersonal, spatial-visual, bodily-kinaesthetic, and intrapersonal, are developed simultaneously in an environment that genuinely regards diversity as a strength, not merely tolerating it as a reality.
>
> Not: *"Let us teach Culture!"*
>
> But: *"Let us create a space that truly values who every learner is, and cultural pride will emerge on its own."*

*(See Figure 9.55: Visual summary: Culture as an emergent property from the GASING learning ecosystem.)*

Ini berbeda dari versi sebelumnya yang seolah-olah guru langsung "membuat lagu dengan ritme lokal" tanpa menjelaskan *mengapa* mereka bisa dan mau melakukannya.

**Table 9.24, baris Musical** diubah dari:

| Musical | Creating Complement-of-10 songs with local regional rhythms | Emerges when a mathematical concept finds its way into the melody a community has always known... |

Menjadi:

| Musical | Singing the Complement-of-10 song together throughout GASING training: the shared musical experience develops musical intelligence in every participant | Emerges when musical intelligence, awakened through the joy of singing together, meets the cultural memory each participant carries: teachers who have experienced mathematics through song naturally reach for the melodies of their own community to express the same concept, and the Complement-of-10 song is born again, in a new cultural colour, without anyone asking them to create it |

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Musical** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Musical activity**, the most important thing that happens in a GASING training session is something that no musical education programme explicitly plans for: a room full of teachers discovers, often for the first time in their adult lives, that singing together feels good.

The Complement-of-10 song, *pasangan sepuluh*, is introduced early in every GASING training. It is not presented as a cultural exercise or a creativity prompt. It is presented as the most direct way to help children internalise the number pairs that sum to ten. The teachers sing it. They sing it again. They sing it with movement. They sing it with energy that the trainer models and the room catches, because enthusiasm, like melody, is contagious.

Something happens in that process that is worth examining carefully. A teacher who has spent years in a classroom, who has learned to associate mathematics with silence and effort and individual concentration, experiences it for the first time as something communal, joyful, and musical. That experience does not leave her when the training session ends. It changes something in how she understands the relationship between mathematics and human expression.

And then she goes home.

She goes back to her community, to her school, to the children she knows and the music she grew up with. And when she thinks about how to teach the number pairs that sum to ten, she does not reach for the version she learned in training. She reaches for the melody she has always known. The rhythm her community sings at weddings and harvests and ceremonies. The musical pattern her students' bodies will recognise before their minds have processed a single mathematical concept.

This is the precise mechanism by which musical intelligence causes cultural competency to emerge. The training does not instruct teachers to create culturally adapted songs. It develops their musical intelligence to the point where cultural adaptation becomes the natural, obvious, and most efficient creative response available to them. A teacher whose musical intelligence has been awakened will express a mathematical concept musically. And a teacher who expresses things musically will express them in the musical language she knows best, which is always the musical language of her own culture.

Cultural competency emerges from musical intelligence when the joy of singing mathematics together in training becomes, back home, the impulse to sing it in the melody that was always already there: when the Complement-of-10 song stops being something learned and becomes something created, in a hundred different cultural colours, none of them instructed, all of them inevitable.

---

Perubahan kunci dari versi sebelumnya, Pak Yohanes:

**Urutan kausalnya kini eksplisit dan benar**: bernyanyi bersama selama pelatihan → kecerdasan musikal berkembang → guru pulang ke komunitasnya → secara alami menciptakan versi lokal. Bukan "guru membuat lagu lokal" seolah-olah itu instruksi yang diberikan.

**Kalimat "And then she goes home"** sengaja dibuat sebagai paragraf satu kalimat untuk menciptakan jeda dramatis: itulah momen di mana seluruh mekanisme bergerak, ketika kecerdasan yang berkembang selama pelatihan bertemu dengan identitas budaya yang sudah ada di dalam diri guru itu.

No one asks them to. The musical intelligence that was developed in week one finds its most natural expression in the cultural musical language each teacher has always carried |

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Musical** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Musical activity**, the timeline is worth stating precisely, because it is one of the most striking demonstrations of emergence in the entire GASING method.

In the first week of training, teachers sing. They sing the Complement-of-10 song, *pasangan sepuluh*, together, with movement, with energy, with the contagious enthusiasm that the trainer models and the room catches. Most of these teachers have spent their careers associating mathematics with silence, with individual effort, with the careful avoidance of wrong answers. In the first week of GASING training, they experience it as something communal, rhythmic, and joyful. Their musical intelligence, which formal education may never have called upon in a mathematical context, begins to stir.

By the second week, something happens that no training schedule explicitly plans for: teachers begin creating their own versions of the Complement-of-10 song.

Not because they were instructed to. Not because there was a "create a cultural song" session on the agenda. But because a musical intelligence that has been genuinely awakened will find expression, and the most natural expression available to any person is the musical language of their own community. The teacher from Lanny Jaya does not reach for a generic melody. She reaches for the rhythm she has known since childhood. The teacher from Banyuwangi does not compose something neutral. He reaches for the Osing pattern that lives in his body before it lives in his mind.

Within two weeks, a single mathematical concept, the pairs of numbers that sum to ten, has become dozens of songs, each one carrying the unmistakable signature of a different Indonesian culture. The Complement-of-10 song from Sabang does not sound like the one from Teluk Bintuni. The version from Badung carries the ghost of gamelan. The version from Siak moves with a Malay grace. None of these were designed. All of them were inevitable, once the musical intelligence that produces them had been given the conditions to emerge.

This is the mechanism precisely: not "teach culture through music," but "develop musical intelligence, and culture will express itself through the music that follows." The two-week timeline is not a curriculum achievement. It is evidence of how quickly a genuine emergent property surfaces when the conditions are right.

Cultural competency emerges from musical intelligence when a teacher who spent week one learning to sing mathematics in someone else's melody spends week two instinctively singing it in her own: when the concept finds its way into the musical language that was always already there, waiting to carry it.

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Musical** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Musical activity**, the process is precise enough to be described day by day, and that precision is itself the evidence.

From the first day of GASING training, songs are not optional. The GASING Academy has developed a full repertoire of official mathematics songs, including multiple versions of the Complement-of-10 song, and participants use them every day, not as warm-up activities or entertainment between the real lessons, but as the primary vehicle for learning. Numbers and their relationships arrive through melody. Operations are practised through rhythm. Every day, the room sings.

The effect of this daily immersion is observable and consistent. Participants who arrived at training describing themselves as people who do not sing, who do not dance, who are not musical, begin to change. Not because anyone tells them to. Because melody and rhythm, encountered daily in a joyful and non-judgmental environment, do what melody and rhythm have always done throughout human history: they get inside the body before the mind has decided whether to allow them. By the end of the first week, teachers who had not sung in years are moving to the rhythm without thinking about it. Their musical intelligence, perhaps dormant since childhood, has begun to stir.

Then comes week two.

In week two, participants are invited to collaborate and create their own regional version of the Complement-of-10 song. This is the moment the mechanism becomes visible. A teacher whose musical intelligence was genuinely dormant one week earlier would struggle with this task. She would have no felt sense of what a song should do, no instinct for how rhythm and melody carry meaning, no confidence that her musical judgement is trustworthy. But a teacher whose musical intelligence has been awakened through a week of daily singing does not struggle. She reaches, naturally and immediately, for the musical language she has always known: the melody her community sings, the rhythm her body learned before she learned mathematics, the musical pattern that sounds, to her ear and to her students' ears, like home.

And so the Complement-of-10 song is born again. In Banyuwangi, it moves with the Osing rhythm. In Lanny Jaya, it carries the distinctiveness of the highlands. In Badung, there is gamelan behind it. In Siak, it flows with Malay grace. Each group sings their creation in traditional dress, and what is visible in every video is the same thing: pride. Not the polite satisfaction of having completed an assignment. The genuine pride of having made something that is both mathematically correct and unmistakably, beautifully theirs.

This is the complete mechanism of cultural emergence through musical intelligence. The daily songs build the musical capacity. The musical capacity, when invited to create, expresses itself in the only musical language it truly knows, which is always the language of the creator's own culture. No one instructed them to incorporate their cultural identity into the song. No one needed to. A musical intelligence that has been genuinely developed will find its most natural expression in the cultural musical heritage the person already carries. The pride that follows is not manufactured. It is the natural response of a person who has discovered that her culture and her mathematics are not in competition but in conversation.

Cultural competency emerges from musical intelligence when a teacher who spent week one learning to sing mathematics through someone else's songs spends week two creating her own, in her own melody, wearing her own dress, and feeling, perhaps for the first time, that both her culture and her mathematics belong to her at the same time.

---

Perubahan utama dari versi sebelumnya, Pak Yohanes:

**"Then comes week two"** sengaja menjadi paragraf satu kalimat, tepat seperti "And then she goes home" di versi sebelumnya, tapi kini akurat secara faktual: perpindahan yang dramatis terjadi di dalam pelatihan itu sendiri, bukan setelah pulang.

**Mekanisme "dormant vs awakened"** dipertegas: guru yang kecerdasan musikalnya belum berkembang akan kesulitan dengan tugas minggu kedua; guru yang sudah berkembang selama seminggu tidak kesulitan sama sekali. Ini yang membuat hubungan kausal antara musical intelligence dan cultural emergence menjadi argumen yang bisa diuji, bukan sekadar klaim.

**"Pride"** diuraikan dengan presisi: bukan kepuasan menyelesaikan tugas, tapi perasaan bahwa budaya dan matematika milik diri sendiri sekaligus.

**Table 9.24, baris Linguistic** diubah menjadi:

| Linguistic | Microteaching and peer tutoring practised daily throughout training, building fluency and confidence in explaining mathematical concepts to others | Emerges without instruction: as linguistic intelligence grows through daily microteaching practice, participants themselves begin proposing to teach and explain in their regional language. No one asks them to. The developing linguistic intelligence finds its most natural and powerful expression in the language the speaker knows most deeply, and culture emerges as a skill: the ability to harness one's own linguistic heritage as a tool for teaching, learning, and connection |

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Linguistic** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Linguistic activity**, the most revealing moment in GASING training is not planned. It cannot be planned. It happens when a participant raises her hand and says, unprompted: *can I try this in my own language?*

That moment is the emergence. Everything before it is the preparation.

From the first day of training, participants practise microteaching and peer tutoring constantly. A teacher who has spent her career on the other side of the explanation, receiving knowledge rather than transmitting it, is now asked to explain a mathematical concept clearly enough for another person to genuinely understand it. Then she is asked to do it again, more clearly. Then again, more efficiently. Then in front of a larger group. Then to someone who is confused and needs a different approach.

This daily practice does what daily practice always does: it builds capacity that was not there before. The teacher who arrived uncertain whether she could explain 64 minus 7 in a way anyone else could follow leaves week one with a felt sense of what clear explanation requires. She knows now what it feels like when an explanation lands, and what it feels like when it does not. Her linguistic intelligence, the capacity to shape language precisely for a specific listener with a specific need, has grown in ways she can feel even if she cannot yet name them.

And then, in week two or sometimes earlier, something unexpected happens.

A participant suggests teaching the next peer tutoring session in her regional language. Not because the trainer requested it. Not because it was on the agenda. But because a linguistic intelligence that has been genuinely sharpened will seek the most effective communicative tool available, and for a teacher explaining a concept to students from her own community, her regional language is not merely one option among several. It is the most precise, most familiar, most emotionally resonant instrument she possesses.

What follows is the emergence of culture as a skill. Not culture as heritage, something inherited and passively carried. But culture as competence: the active, deliberate capacity to harness one's own linguistic and cultural heritage as a tool for teaching, for learning, and for connection. The teacher who microteaches in Sundanese has not merely code-switched. She has made a professional pedagogical decision, based on her growing understanding of how explanation works and who her learners are. She has discovered, through the development of her own linguistic intelligence, that her regional language is not a limitation to be apologised for but a resource to be deployed.

This discovery spreads. Once one participant demonstrates that peer tutoring in the regional language produces faster, deeper understanding, others follow. Not because they were told to, but because the evidence is in the room and linguistic intelligence, once developed, knows how to read evidence about communication. The regional language sessions multiply. Teachers who had never imagined using their mother tongue in a formal educational context begin using it with confidence, with pride, and with measurably better results.

Before GASING training, the idea of using regional language as a deliberate teaching tool would not have occurred to most of these teachers. Not because they lacked the language. But because they lacked the linguistic intelligence to recognise it as a resource rather than a limitation. That recognition is what the training produces. And culture emerges from it not as a sentiment but as a skill: something a teacher now knows how to use, chooses to use, and is proud to use.

Cultural competency emerges from linguistic intelligence when a participant stops waiting for permission to teach in her own language and starts understanding, from the inside, why doing so is not a compromise of educational standards but an expression of the highest one: reaching the learner where she actually is.

---

Tiga hal yang membuat elaborasi ini bekerja, Pak Yohandi:

**"Can I try this in my own language?"** menjadi kalimat pembuka yang langsung menangkap momen emergence yang sesungguhnya, sebelum analisis masuk. Pembaca langsung tahu: ini bukan tentang instruksi, ini tentang sesuatu yang muncul dari dalam.

**Perbedaan "culture as heritage" vs "culture as competence"** adalah kontribusi konseptual yang penting: culture bukan sesuatu yang diwarisi secara pasif, tapi sesuatu yang aktif digunakan sebagai alat pedagogis. Ini yang membuat linguistic emergence berbeda dari sekadar "menghargai budaya lokal."

**"Before GASING training... that recognition is what the training produces"** menutup lingkaran kausal dengan presisi: bukan bahasa daerahnya yang baru ada, tapi kecerdasan untuk mengenalinya sebagai sumber daya yang baru muncul.

**Table 9.24, baris Interpersonal** diubah menjadi:

| Interpersonal | Peer tutoring, group games, and team relays throughout training that build genuine connection and trust between participants | Emerges when collaboration, itself an emergent property of developed interpersonal intelligence, produces something neither mathematics nor culture could produce alone: mathematical acculturation, a new cultural form in which local songs, rhythms, and traditions are fused with mathematical content through the collective creative act of people who have learned to genuinely work together |

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Interpersonal** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Interpersonal activity**, the cultural emergence that occurs is of a different order from the others. It is not one person reaching for her own cultural heritage. It is a group of people, who have learned to trust one another, creating something that did not exist before.

The foundation is built through weeks of interpersonal GASING activities: peer tutoring sessions where one person's clarity of explanation directly determines another person's understanding, group games where the team's outcome depends on every member's genuine participation, and relays where a wrong answer from one person costs the entire group. These activities do what interpersonal development always does when it works: they build trust between specific people in a specific room. Not the abstract goodwill of people who have been told to cooperate, but the concrete trust of people who have genuinely needed one another and found that the need was met.

From that trust, collaboration emerges. And from that collaboration, something entirely new becomes possible.

In the second week of training, when participants are invited to create a regional version of the Complement-of-10 song, they do not do it alone. They do it together. And because they are doing it together, what they produce is not simply one teacher's cultural expression set to a mathematical concept. It is a collective negotiation between the musical memories, linguistic instincts, movement traditions, and cultural identities of everyone in the group. One person knows the melody. Another knows the words that will scan correctly. A third remembers the dance movement that fits the rhythm. A fourth suggests the traditional dress that should accompany the performance.

What emerges from this collaborative negotiation is something that has a name in cultural anthropology: acculturation. But what happens in GASING training is a specific and extraordinary form of it: *mathematical acculturation*. A new cultural form that did not exist before the training began, in which the mathematical content and the local cultural tradition are not merely placed side by side but genuinely fused. The mathematics gives the song its content. The culture gives the song its life. And the collaboration gives the song its existence, because no single participant could have produced it alone.

This mathematical acculturation is emergent in the strictest sense. No trainer designed it. No curriculum specified it. It arises because interpersonal intelligence, developed to the point where genuine collaboration becomes possible, naturally produces co-creation. And co-creation between people who each carry a piece of their community's cultural heritage naturally produces cultural synthesis. The Complement-of-10 song from a GASING training in Lanny Jaya is not the song of one Lanny teacher. It is the song of a group of Lanny teachers who have learned to work together, and that group authorship is audible in every note.

The pride that follows is collective too. When the group sings their creation in traditional dress, they are not each proud of their individual contribution. They are proud of what they made together, as a group, as members of a community whose cultural heritage has just proven itself capable of carrying mathematics with full dignity and full beauty.

Cultural competency emerges from interpersonal intelligence when collaboration becomes generative: when people who have learned to genuinely work together discover that their collective cultural heritage is not just a background they share but a creative resource they can build with, producing something new that belongs to all of them and that neither mathematics nor culture could have produced without the other.

---

Tiga hal yang membuat elaborasi ini bekerja, Pak Yohanes:

**"Mathematical acculturation"** diperkenalkan sebagai konsep formal dengan definisi yang presisi: bukan sekadar "menggabungkan budaya dengan matematika," tapi sebuah bentuk budaya baru yang lahir dari kolaborasi dan tidak bisa direduksi ke salah satu komponennya. Ini kontribusi konseptual yang layak dikutip.

**Mekanisme kolaborasi dijelaskan secara konkret**: siapa yang menyumbang melodi, siapa yang menyumbang kata, siapa yang menyumbang gerak, siapa yang menyumbang pakaian adat. Kolaborasi bukan abstraksi, ia adalah negosiasi antara keping-keping budaya yang dibawa oleh orang-orang yang sudah saling percaya.

**"The pride that follows is collective too"** menutup elaborasi dengan cara yang mengunci argumen: kebanggaan kolektif adalah bukti bahwa yang lahir adalah budaya baru yang dimiliki bersama, bukan sekadar kumpulan kontribusi individual.

**Table 9.24, baris Spatial-Visual** diubah menjadi:

| Spatial-Visual + Logical-Mathematical | Concrete-abstract-*mencongak* progression: teachers learn to use physical objects as the essential first step toward mathematical abstraction, developing both the logical understanding of *why* concrete matters and the visual capacity to see one object functioning in place of another | Emerges when a teacher who understands why concrete representation is necessary and can mentally visualise any object serving that function looks at her own surroundings and asks: "why not use areca nut instead of marbles?" Fish, leaves, bird feathers, local seeds: the objects of everyday cultural life become mathematical manipulatives, and the skill of harnessing local culture to explain mathematics emerges, without instruction, as the natural consequence of two intelligences working together |

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Spatial-Visual + Logical-Mathematical** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Spatial-Visual and Logical-Mathematical activities**, two intelligences develop in parallel, and their convergence produces a cultural emergence that neither could have produced alone.

The logical-mathematical understanding comes first, and it comes through the concrete-abstract-*mencongak* progression that is central to every GASING training. A teacher who has worked through this progression with genuine attention does not merely learn that physical objects help children understand mathematics. She understands *why*. She understands that the concrete stage is not a simplification for children who cannot yet handle abstraction, but the necessary cognitive foundation without which abstraction has nothing real to stand on. She understands that a child who has held the concept in her hands will carry it into her head in a way that a child who only ever heard it described cannot.

This logical-mathematical understanding changes how the teacher sees her classroom. She is no longer looking for the correct teaching aid listed in the curriculum. She is looking for whatever object will most effectively serve the function of making the abstract concrete. And that is the moment the spatial-visual intelligence enters.

Spatial-visual intelligence, developed through weeks of visualising number relationships and mathematical structures in GASING training, gives the teacher a specific capacity: the ability to look at an object and see not only what it is but what it could represent. A marble is not a marble. It is a unit. And a unit is anything that can be counted, grouped, and moved. Once that perceptual shift has occurred, the teacher looks at her surroundings with entirely new eyes.

And then the question comes, spontaneously, from inside the training room: *"Why not use areca nut instead of marbles?"*

That question is the emergence. It is not a creative exercise. It is not a cultural awareness activity. It is a logical inference made by a mind that now understands why concrete objects matter and can visually transfer that function to any object in her environment. The areca nut is not chosen because someone suggested incorporating local culture. It is chosen because it is there, it can be counted, it can be grouped, and it can be held in a child's hand. The cultural significance it carries is not the reason for its selection. But it is the reason the selection produces cultural emergence.

From that first question, others follow, each one a further expression of the same two intelligences working together. Fish instead of marbles, for the teacher from a coastal community whose students have grown up counting the day's catch. Leaves instead of blocks, for the teacher from a highland community where the forest floor is the most abundant surface in the world. Bird feathers, local seeds, woven strips of bamboo: every suggestion is simultaneously a mathematical decision and a cultural one, made by a mind that can now see both dimensions at once.

What emerges from this is a skill that did not exist before the training: the capacity to harness local cultural material as a deliberate pedagogical tool. Before GASING training, these objects were simply part of the environment. After the logical-mathematical and spatial-visual intelligences have been developed to the point where the teacher can see any object as a potential mathematical manipulative, the same objects become resources, chosen with professional intentionality, used with pedagogical confidence, and received by students with the quiet but powerful recognition that mathematics is not something that comes from elsewhere. It is already here, in the things we have always touched.

Cultural competency emerges from the convergence of logical-mathematical and spatial-visual intelligence when a teacher stops asking "where do I get the correct teaching materials?" and starts asking "what do I already have?" Because what she already has is always, inevitably, cultural.

---

***"Why not use areca nut instead of marbles?"*** menjadi momen emergence yang konkret dan bisa dibayangkan, persis seperti "can I try this in my own language?" di elaborasi Linguistic.

**Kalimat penutup** "what she already has is always, inevitably, cultural" mengunci argumen dengan cara yang elegan: bukan budaya yang ditambahkan ke matematika, tapi matematika yang akhirnya menemukan apa yang sudah selalu ada di sana.

---

**Supplementary Note, bagian Bodily-Kinaesthetic** diubah menjadi:

---

**In the Bodily-Kinaesthetic activity**, the cultural emergence follows a logic that is almost mechanical in its clarity: once a teacher's body has learned that physical movement can carry mathematical content, it becomes nearly impossible for her to watch a traditional game without seeing the mathematics it could hold.

The foundation is built through weeks of GASING movement activities. The mathematical movement game, with its frog jumps for multiples of three and its raised hands for exactly ten and its disciplined stillness for numbers that meet neither condition, trains something specific: the felt understanding that the body and mathematical thinking are not in separate domains. A child who jumps for a multiple of three is not doing mathematics and then jumping. She is doing mathematics through the jumping. The movement is not a reward for correct thinking. It is the medium in which the thinking happens.

A teacher who has experienced this, who has felt in her own body how physical action can make an abstract condition concrete and memorable, carries a new perceptual capacity out of the training room. She now sees movement as a mathematical medium. And when she looks at the traditional games of her own community with that new perception, what she sees is potential that was always there and never before recognised.

Take *dampu*, the traditional hopscotch game known across many Indonesian communities. Its structure is already mathematical: a grid of connected squares, drawn on the ground, topped with a half-circle at the peak. A player moves through the grid by hopping on one foot, navigating the squares in sequence, turning at the top, and returning. The spatial logic is inherent to the game. The physical discipline of balancing on one foot while navigating a sequence is already there. The game has always been, in its structure, a body moving through an ordered space according to rules.

What a teacher with developed bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence sees, looking at this game, is one additional layer: what if each square held a mathematics problem? What if the player had to calculate the answer before jumping to the next square? What if the half-circle at the top was reserved for the hardest problem, the one that required the most concentration to hold while balancing?

The adaptation requires almost no creative effort, because the bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence that enables it has already done the work of recognising the structural compatibility between the game and the mathematical content. The teacher is not forcing mathematics into a cultural form. She is noticing that the cultural form was already shaped, in its movement logic and its spatial structure, to receive mathematics naturally.

And *dampu* is only one example. *Hompimpa*, the traditional elimination game, can become a number-classification activity. Local suit games can become probability explorations. Any game that involves counting, sequencing, spatial navigation, or rule-governed movement, which is to say almost any traditional game that has survived long enough to become traditional, carries within its structure the potential for mathematical acculturation.

This potential was always there. What the GASING training produces is the perceptual capacity to see it. A teacher who arrived at training thinking of traditional games as cultural heritage, valuable but separate from her work as a mathematics teacher, leaves with a different understanding: these games are pedagogical resources, already shaped by generations of play into forms that the body finds natural, that children find joyful, and that mathematical content can inhabit with full comfort.

Cultural competency emerges from bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence when a teacher looks at a *dampu* grid drawn in the dust and sees, simultaneously, the game her grandmother played and a mathematics lesson her students will never forget: when the cultural form and the mathematical content are no longer two things she is trying to combine, but one thing she has learned to see whole.

---

Pembaca yang belum pernah memainkannya bisa membayangkannya. Analisis yang mengikuti gambaran konkret jauh lebih melekat.

Bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence gives it movements the child's body already knows. And intrapersonal intelligence gives the child the conviction that who she already is, is exactly enough.
 
No single one of these six produces cultural competency alone. Together, they produce something that no curriculum directive has ever successfully manufactured: a child who is proud of where she comes from and confident in where she is going, who does not experience these as two different directions, because the GASING method has shown her, through direct personal experience, that they are the same direction.

She receives the signal that this knowledge is for her, that the academic world does not require her to shed who she is as a prerequisite for entry. That signal, once received, permanently changes a child's relationship with learning.
 
Local objects as manipulatives convey a similar message: mathematics is not something that arrives from elsewhere and must be accepted with deference. It is already here, in the areca nuts that can be counted, in the weaving that forms geometric patterns, in the rhythm of drumbeats that divides time with precision. That is what is meant by culture as contextual intelligence: not an ornament that decorates learning, but a foundation that gives it roots.
