## 9.4 The Evidence That Cannot Be Argued With

There is a kind of proof that statistics cannot provide and theoretical frameworks cannot produce. It is the proof of a face.

A child's face before she could add. And the same face, weeks later, after she can.

The thirty student before-and-after videos in this catalogue document exactly that: the moment when "cannot" becomes "can," captured on camera, unrepeatable, undeniable. They were not filmed to make a point. They were filmed because something was happening that deserved to be witnessed, and because the people in those rooms understood, perhaps instinctively, that what they were watching should not be allowed to disappear.

Alongside them are videos of teachers whose classrooms changed because they changed first, testimonials from students and parents and heads of education departments and a President, songs composed in the languages of communities that had never before seen their own culture carrying mathematics, and farewells so full of tears that they require no caption.

These videos are not claims. They are documentation. Not of a programme, but of what becomes possible when the right conditions are created and every child is given the opportunity they have never before received.

From Sabang to Merauke. From the coast to the highlands. From children who trembled when asked what four plus three equals, to children who demonstrate triple-digit multiplication before a head of state.

Watch them in sequence, and what accumulates is not data. It is conviction.

---

### B. Teacher Teaching Before-After Videos (3 Videos)

### C. Testimonial Videos (30 Videos)

*Inspirational stories from teachers, students, parents, education officials, and a President.*

Testimonials are the least controllable form of evidence. A score can be coached. A performance can be rehearsed. But the specific way a person's voice changes when she is describing something that genuinely altered the course of her life, the pause before she finds the word, the moment she laughs because she still cannot quite believe it, cannot be manufactured on instruction.

These thirty voices come from every corner of the archipelago. They speak different languages with different accents and describe different classrooms and different children. But they converge, consistently and without coordination, on the same thing: something happened here that was not supposed to be possible, and it happened anyway.

## 9.5 Closing of Chapter 9: The 8C Symphony, from Potential to Reality

This chapter began with a simple question: what actually grows from 23,633 learning journeys?

The answer has been eight competencies. Not eight competencies listed in a curriculum and delivered in eight dedicated sessions, but eight competencies that emerge simultaneously, naturally, and inevitably from the right conditions. Critical Thinking and Creativity and Communication and Collaboration from the P21 framework, yes. But also Computational Logic, which emerges from mathematical training long before a keyboard is ever touched. Character, which emerges when a child is surprised by her own capability. Culture, which emerges when a teacher whose musical intelligence has been awakened reaches instinctively for the melody she has always known. And Compassion, which emerges when a person witnesses someone giving everything, without complaint, and quietly decides to become like that.

In Section 9.1, we saw the four P21 competencies growing organically from GASING activities that carry no label of "competency class" whatsoever. In Section 9.2, we understood why the four additional competencies are not a quantitative addition to the framework but a recognition of dimensions of humanity that P21, designed in a different context for different purposes, did not need to address and Indonesia cannot afford to ignore. In Section 9.3, we saw that the eight competencies do not operate in isolation but as an ecosystem of mutual reinforcement, where a single twelve-second activity can develop all eight simultaneously. And in Section 9.4, we found that behind the entire framework lies one conviction, simple enough to state in a single sentence, radical enough to change everything it touches: there are no unintelligent children, only children who have not yet had the opportunity to learn from a good teacher using the right method.

The videos in the catalogue above are the living evidence of that conviction put into practice. From Aceh Barat Daya to Nduga. From a police training in Dairi to a mathematics demonstration before a President. From a child who trembled when asked what eight plus four equals, to five children standing on an international stage in Korea with medals around their necks.

But evidence of what, precisely?

Not of a method that works in ideal conditions. Not of results achievable only by exceptional teachers or unusually gifted students or well-resourced schools. The 127 regencies represented in this data include some of the most isolated, most under-resourced, most geographically challenging educational environments in the world. The children in these videos are not the selected ones. They are the ones who were there.

That is the argument this chapter has been building. And it is an argument that now requires the most rigorous test available.

---

Chapter 10 submits that argument to the only authority that does not bend to narrative: numbers.

From 23,633 participants. Across 127 regencies. In 33 provinces, from Aceh to Papua. Pre-test scores and post-test scores, effect sizes and statistical significance, distributions and outliers and the specific weight of what a Cohen's d of 5.05 means when the benchmark for a "large" effect is 0.8.

The faces in this chapter were real. Chapter 10 will show how many of them there were, and by exactly how much they changed.

---

> *"This is what happens when educational theories stop arguing and begin to work together: an extraordinary transformation that proves every child, wherever they may be, possesses unlimited potential waiting to be actualised."*
>
> Principle of the GASING method

*(See Figure 9.63: GASING participants: 23,633 journeys, one conviction.)*

# Chapter 10: Empirical Evidence of the Effectiveness of the GASING Method

*A Comprehensive Analysis of 23,633 Participants Across 33 Indonesian Provinces*

---

| **23,633** | **33** | **d = 5.06** | **24×** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Total Participants** | **Indonesian Provinces** | **Student Effect Size** | **Faster than Conventional Education** |

---

### Introduction

Chapter 9 answered the question of *why*: why the GASING method works, where its power comes from, and which theories converge within a single learning session. This chapter answers a different and unavoidable question: how large, how consistent, and how real is its impact in the actual world?

Not in a laboratory. Not under conditions designed to produce the most flattering results. But in real classrooms, with earthen floors and electricity that sometimes failed, in highlands accessible only by small aircraft, with teachers encountering this method for the very first time and children who arrived, in their majority, without adequate numeracy foundations.

The answer lies in data from **23,633 participants**: 7,983 teachers and 15,650 students, distributed across 127 districts and cities in 33 provinces throughout the Indonesian archipelago. This is not a sample drawn from the easiest locations. It is a dataset built from a deliberate choice to go to the hardest ones first.

Before reading the numbers, one context is essential. Indonesia faces an acute numeracy crisis. PISA 2022 placed Indonesia at rank 68 out of 81 countries in mathematical literacy, with 70% of students falling below the minimum competency level. In *3T regions* (Frontier, Outermost, and Underdeveloped areas), the situation is even more severe: many students complete primary school without ever truly mastering basic arithmetic, not because they lack capability, but because the right opportunity has never reached them.

The GASING method arrived in that context. And this is what happened.

---

> *"There is no such thing as a dumb child. There are only children who have not yet found the right teacher and the right method."*
>
> Prof. Yohanes Surya, Ph.D.

---

### Four Principal Findings

Before proceeding to the full analysis, it is useful to have an overview of what this chapter documents. The following four findings are the most scientifically and humanly significant. Read them once here, and then encounter them again in the data that follows. The second reading will feel different from the first.

**First: an effect size that exceeds the global literature.** Cohen's d = 5.06 for students and 3.04 for teachers. This figure is not merely "large" by the standards of educational science. It surpasses the "large" threshold (d = 0.8) by more than six times, and ranks among the highest ever documented in the educational intervention literature anywhere in the world. The benchmark for what is considered "extraordinary" in educational research is d > 2.0. The GASING method produces d = 5.06.

**Second: a transformation speed 24 times faster.** The mastery of numeracy that conventionally requires approximately 48 months of formal schooling was achieved within 2 weeks of intensive GASING training. This is not a simplification of the material, nor a reduction in standards. It is learning that is more efficient precisely because it is more humane: designed for how the human brain actually works, rather than for how it has been assumed to work.

**Third: an inverse effectiveness gradient.** A strong negative correlation (r = −0.620, ρ = −0.937) indicates that students and teachers with the lowest initial scores experienced the greatest improvements. The GASING method is not only effective for those who already have a foundation. It is most effective precisely for those who need it most. In a country with Indonesia's geographic and historical inequalities, this property is not merely academically interesting. It is a policy instrument.

**Fourth: Papua as the national leader.** The region most frequently described in education reports as "difficult to intervene in" recorded an average improvement of 10.04 times and emerged as the national leader in responsiveness to the GASING method. This finding does not merely challenge the assumptions that have been allowed to persist about the capabilities of children in eastern Indonesia. It overturns them, by a margin too large to attribute to coincidence.

---

**Table 10.1.** Structure of Chapter 10: Empirical Evidence of the Effectiveness of the GASING Method.

| Section | Title | Description |
|---|---|---|
| 10.1 | Implementation of the GASING Method in Indonesia | Scale, scope, and geographic distribution of implementation |
| 10.2 | Training Results: National Quantitative Data | Comparison of pre-training and post-training scores |
| 10.3 | Effect Size Analysis | Measurement of impact using Cohen's d |
| 10.4 | Inverse Effectiveness Gradient | Correlation analysis and evidence of an equalization effect |
| 10.5 | Per-Operation Analysis (BAKALKUBAGI) | Results by the four arithmetic operations |
| 10.6 | Regional Analysis: Papua and 3T Areas | Focus on priority regions |
| 10.7 | Speed of Transformation | Comparison of speed versus traditional education |
| 10.8 | Additional Findings: Eight Advanced Analyses | Deeper patterns, predictors, and implications |

---

### A Note on Data Validity

One point must be established at the outset: all analyses in this chapter are based on actual field implementation, not on controlled experimental conditions. This means the results carry high *ecological validity*. What is documented here is what can genuinely happen in Indonesian schools, not merely what is possible in a laboratory with every available facility.

The geographic reach reinforces this point. From Aceh to Papua. From urban centers to remote interiors accessible only by small aircraft. From madrasahs in Jakarta to classrooms without walls in the highlands of Central Papua. The GASING method is not an intervention that succeeds only under ideal conditions. It is a methodology proven effective precisely in the most challenging conditions, among participants who need it most, in the most marginalised regions.

A method that produces these results under these conditions is not a method that got lucky. It is a method that works.

## 10.1 Implementation of the GASING Method in Indonesia

### Scale and Scope: A Map of Need, Not Convenience

There is a wrong way to read the numbers in this section: to view them as program statistics. There is a right way: to see them as a measure of how far a conviction has travelled, from the belief that every child can learn mathematics, to every corner of the widest archipelago on earth.

The GASING method has been implemented across **33 provinces**, with teacher training reaching **127 districts and cities**, and student training reaching **125 districts and cities**. The total of **23,633 participants**, comprising 7,983 teachers and 15,650 students, was not accumulated gradually through a slowly expanding programme. It is evidence of a movement operating with urgency, because there is a crisis that cannot wait.

*(See Figure 10.1: Map of Indonesia with red dots marking each district and city that has participated in GASING method training.)*

What must be understood from the outset about this map: **these 127 districts and cities are present because of a need they recognised themselves.** Local governments in these 127 locations saw the state of numeracy in their regions, recognised that their children required a real intervention, and made the decision to act. The red dots on this map are not a map of top-down programme distribution. They are a map of local awareness about a need that has waited too long.

The distribution spans the entire Indonesian archipelago without significant gaps: from Aceh at the western tip of Sumatra to districts in Papua accessible only by small aircraft. Sumatra, Java, Kalimantan, Sulawesi, Maluku, Nusa Tenggara, and all Papua provinces are represented. The densest clusters appear in Sulawesi and Papua, reflecting the heightened awareness of local governments in those regions about the urgency of improving numeracy outcomes for their children.

And yet these 127 locations are only the beginning. Indonesia has **514 districts and cities**. This map shows only a quarter of all existing administrative territories. In every district not yet present on this map, there are thousands of students still struggling with basic arithmetic, and thousands of teachers instructing every day with knowledge that has never been systematically strengthened. The data in this chapter was produced by 127 regions that recognised their need and acted. The most meaningful question is not what has already happened to them.

The question is: **what will happen when all 514 districts and cities in Indonesia are given the same opportunity?**

---

**Table 10.2.** National Implementation Metrics of the GASING Method.

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Total Participants | 23,633 |
| Students | 15,650 |
| Teachers | 7,983 |
| Provinces | 33 |
| Districts/Cities (students) | 125 |
| Districts/Cities (teachers) | 127 |

---

### Geographic Distribution: A Deliberate Choice

*(See Figure 10.2: Distribution of student participants in the GASING method across Indonesia. Papua n = 5,707 (36.5%), Sumatra n = 3,291 (21.0%), Sulawesi n = 2,408 (15.4%), Java & Bali n = 2,048 (13.1%), Maluku n = 860 (5.5%), NTT n = 708 (4.5%), Kalimantan n = 628 (4.0%). Total student participants: 15,650.)*

The distribution map in Figure 10.2 tells something more than mere geographic spread. It tells a story of choice. A programme seeking to appear successful would select large cities, schools with adequate infrastructure, and participants who already have a foundation. The GASING method chose otherwise.

Papua became the region with the largest concentration of participants: **5,707 students, or 36.5% of all national participants**. Within this figure lie thousands of journeys that were not easy, whether the physical journeys of trainers toward districts accessible only by small aircraft, or the learning journeys of children who arrived with the lowest initial scores and left with the highest improvements. Papua Province proper contributed 1,933 students, Central Papua 1,752, and Highland Papua 881. These three provinces alone reflect the depth of the commitment to reach the areas least touched by any national education programme.

Sumatra became the second-largest regional cluster with 3,291 students (21.0%), led by North Sumatra with 1,459 students. Sulawesi followed with 2,408 students (15.4%), with North Sulawesi and Southeast Sulawesi as the largest contributors. Java and Bali recorded 2,048 students (13.1%), Maluku 860 students (5.5%), NTT 708 students (4.5%), and Kalimantan 628 students (4.0%).

This distribution did not form organically because of ease of access or lower costs. On the contrary: Papua and Kalimantan are the most expensive and most logistically complex regions to reach, requiring small aircraft, multi-day journeys, and coordination far more demanding than opening a training programme in a large city. More than half of all national participants came from Papua (36.5%), Sulawesi (15.4%), and Maluku (5.5%), together reaching **57.4% of the total**. This is a distribution shaped by a deliberate priority to reach those who need it most, not those easiest to serve.

As will become evident in the following sections, that choice proved correct not only ethically, but empirically: the regions with the lowest starting points demonstrated the highest responsiveness to the GASING method.

---

**Table 10.3.** Distribution of Student Participants and Average Improvement per Region.

| Region | Students | Percentage | Avg. Ratio | Avg. Post-Test |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Papua | 5,707 | 36.5% | 10.04× | 71.3 |
| Sumatra | 3,291 | 21.0% | 3.5× | 81.1 |
| Sulawesi | 2,408 | 15.4% | 4.9× | 76.9 |
| Java & Bali | 2,048 | 13.1% | 3.1× | 85.1 |
| Maluku | 860 | 5.5% | 4.6× | 74.2 |
| NTT | 708 | 4.5% | 4.0× | 78.6 |
| Kalimantan | 628 | 4.0% | 7.2× | 76.4 |

The third column of this table carries a message that cannot be ignored. Read the improvement ratios from top to bottom and let the pattern speak for itself.

Java and Bali, with the best educational infrastructure and the highest initial scores among all regions, recorded an improvement of **3.1 times**. That figure is already extraordinary by the standards of any educational intervention. But then compare it with what happened at the other end of the spectrum. NTT: 4.0 times. Maluku: 4.6 times. Sulawesi: 4.9 times. Kalimantan: 7.2 times. Papua: 10.04 times.

The further from the centre, the greater the leap. Not slightly greater. More than twice as great. This pattern holds without exception across all seven regions, and it can only be explained by one thing: the GASING method does not merely work across all contexts. It becomes *increasingly* effective precisely where initial conditions are most challenging.

This is what statistical analysis terms an inverse effectiveness gradient, and this regional distribution table represents its first evidence, before we have even entered the formal correlation analysis in Section 10.4.

---

### The Baseline: A Portrait of Historical Inequality

*(See Figure 10.3: Distribution of student pre-test scores by province, sorted from lowest to highest. Dark teal = Papua provinces, medium teal = Other 3T regions, light teal = Non-3T regions.)*

Figure 10.3 visualises average student pre-test scores across all 33 provinces, sorted from lowest on the left to highest on the right. Before reading a single number, the colour pattern is immediately visible: dark teal dominates the left side. Papua provinces cluster at the far left, carrying the lowest baselines in the entire dataset.

This is not a geographic coincidence. It is a reflection of historically structured inequality in access to quality education. The range between the leftmost and rightmost points of the chart is **34.6 points**: from 3.9 (the lowest) to 38.5 for DKI Jakarta at the far right. On a scale of 0 to 100, this means students in the province with the highest baseline began training with scores nearly ten times those of students in the province with the lowest baseline.

That disparity does not reflect a difference in learning capacity. It is an accumulation of decades of differences in access, teacher quality, infrastructure, and policy attention. A child in DKI Jakarta and a child in Central Papua enter training with a 35-point gap between them that neither of them created.

The right side of Figure 10.3 is dominated by light teal bars: DKI Jakarta (38.5), East Java, Central Java, Bali, Yogyakarta, and Bangka Belitung all rank in the top quartile. But precisely here, this chart prepares the reader for the most important finding in the sections that follow: these provinces with the highest baselines will produce the lowest improvement ratios, while the provinces on the far left that appear most disadvantaged will record the most dramatic transformations.

The map of disadvantage and the map of opportunity, in this dataset, are the same map.

---

### Field Evidence: Lanny Jaya and Dogiyai

*(See Figure 10.4: GASING method training in an open space in Lanny Jaya. Participants seated in groups on the grass, completing mathematics exercises.)*

In Lanny Jaya there is a school, and there are classrooms, and there are chairs. The decision to hold training in an open space was a pedagogical choice, not a necessity. The GASING method does not require a specially equipped room as a prerequisite for learning to take place. In the highlands of Highland Papua, a wide lawn became a more vibrant arena than neatly arranged desks facing a chalkboard.

But what speaks most powerfully is not the image. It is the data behind it.

In Lanny Jaya, 296 students began training with an average BAKALKUBAGI score of **7.5 out of 100**. Their average subtraction score was 11.18. Their multiplication score was 9.63. Their division score was 3.4, a figure that in practice means almost no measurable ability whatsoever, even though most of these children had already been attending school for years.

After two weeks of training, their average BAKALKUBAGI score rose to **72.99**, an improvement of **9.73 times**. The division score that had been 3.4 rose to 71.17. The subtraction score from 11.18 to 79.05. One hundred and forty-eight teachers in the same district moved from an average score of 21.12 to 82.66, an improvement of 3.91 times.

The same children. The same teachers. The same place. In the same two weeks. The only thing that changed was the method.

*(See Figure 10.5: GASING method teacher training in Dogiyai District, Central Papua. Community members in traditional Papuan attire alongside other participants.)*

One hundred kilometres from Lanny Jaya, in Dogiyai District, Figure 10.5 captures a different but equally significant scene. Dogiyai is one of the most isolated districts in Indonesia, accessible only by small aircraft through challenging terrain. Yet precisely here, GASING arrived, not as a passing guest but as something to be rooted.

What is most striking about this image is not the location. It is who is present. Community members wearing traditional Papuan attire stand alongside other participants in everyday dress, all raising their hands, following the trainer's instructions with the same enthusiasm. No one was excluded based on background. No one was asked to shed their cultural identity as a condition for learning. Traditional dress and mathematical thinking stand side by side without a hint of tension.

The data: 96 students in Dogiyai began training with an average BAKALKUBAGI score of **3.2**, the lowest of the two districts. Their average multiplication score was 3.97. Their division score was 4.0. Figures that would lead many education systems to conclude that years of foundational work would be needed before meaningful progress was possible.

After two weeks, their average score reached **68.46**, an improvement of **21.39 times**, more than three times the already-high regional Papua average. The division score that had been 4.0 rose to 65.13. The multiplication score from 3.97 to 67.04. Forty-eight teachers in Dogiyai moved from an average of 15.96 to 77.38, an improvement of 4.85 times.

These teachers are now multipliers of the methodology: they carry the GASING method forward to students and fellow teachers in their communities, in a language they know, within a cultural context they live every day, long after the external training team has returned home.

Lanny Jaya with an improvement of 9.73 times. Dogiyai with an improvement of 21.39 times. Two districts among the most remote in Indonesia, with initial conditions among the most challenging nationally, producing two figures that cannot be explained by luck or coincidence. They can only be explained by one thing: **when the right method finally arrives in a place that has never had it, what happens is not merely improvement. What happens is a demonstration that the potential was always there, and what had been absent was only the opportunity to actualise it.**

---

## 10.2 Training Results: National Quantitative Data

### The Two Stories in One Table

Section 10.1 showed where the GASING method went. This section shows what happened when it arrived.

The most direct way to answer that question is a single table. But tables, read without context, are just numbers. The two stories inside Table 10.4 deserve to be told before the numbers are encountered, because they are not the same story, and understanding why they are different is essential to understanding what the data means.

*(See Figure 10.6: National aggregate pre-test and post-test BAKALKUBAGI scores for students and teachers. Both groups exceed the 70-point competency threshold following training.)*

**Table 10.4.** National Aggregate Pre-Test and Post-Test BAKALKUBAGI Results.

| Group | Participants | Pre-Test | Post-Test | Gain | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Students (n = 15,650) | 15,650 | 17.92 | 76.40 | +58.48 | +326% |
| Teachers (n = 7,983) | 7,983 | 46.42 | 88.41 | +41.99 | +91% |

**The first story concerns teachers.** Seven thousand nine hundred and eighty-three teachers entered training with an average BAKALKUBAGI score of **46.42**, a figure below the 70-point competency threshold. This is an uncomfortable finding, but one that cannot be looked away from: many of the teachers who teach mathematics daily in Indonesian elementary schools entered every classroom to teach material they themselves had not fully mastered.

Not because they did not try. Not because they did not care. But because the system that prepared them never equipped them with sufficiently solid understanding from the start. For years, those gaps were transmitted unknowingly, from teacher to student, from generation to generation, in a quiet cycle that no examination result ever captured because the examinations themselves were calibrated to the same incomplete understanding.

After two weeks of GASING training, the average teacher score rose to **88.41**, an improvement of 91%, placing them well above the competency threshold, not merely passing it by a narrow margin. This is not merely an improvement in teaching technique. It is a reconstruction of understanding: teachers who for years had been teaching with gaps in their own knowledge now possess, for the first time, a genuinely solid foundation for the content they will spend the rest of their careers delivering.

**The second story concerns students, and it is more dramatic still.** Fifteen thousand six hundred and fifty students began training with an average score of **17.92**. To understand what that figure means in real life: a child with a score of 17.92 on the BAKALKUBAGI scale has not yet mastered the basic arithmetic operations they should have had since second or third grade of primary school. Such a child might be able to count single digits, but adding two-digit numbers, subtracting with borrowing, multiplying, or dividing is already beyond their reach.

And this child is not the exception. A score of 17.92 is the national *average*, meaning half of all students scored below this figure.

After two weeks of training, their average score rose to **76.40**. An improvement of 326%, from well below the competency threshold to comfortably above it. A child who two weeks earlier could not complete a simple subtraction can now work all four BAKALKUBAGI operations with an average score exceeding the national competency standard.

*(See Figure 10.7: Real change before and after GASING training for a student from Timor Tengah Selatan District, NTT, solving the problem 56 − 32÷8.)*

What is most significant across these results is what the two rows of Table 10.4 share. Both groups, beginning from very different starting points, one at 46.42 and one at 17.92, succeeded in surpassing the same competency threshold. The same method, the same two weeks, results that crossed the same line. This is the first evidence that the GASING method is not calibrated for a particular ability level. It works from any starting point, as long as the method is right and the opportunity is genuinely provided.

---

### Consistency Across 125 Locations: The Evidence That a Method Truly Works

A result from one location can be impressive. A result from ten locations can be remarkable. A result from **125 locations spanning the entire Indonesian archipelago** is something different: it is a proof of reliability that a method either has or does not have, and cannot be manufactured through selective reporting.

*(See Figure 10.8: Distribution of student achievement gains (pre-test to post-test) across 125 districts/cities, grouped by gain magnitude.)*

**Table 10.5.** Distribution of Student Achievement Gain Categories Across 125 Locations.

| Gain Category | No. Districts | Percentage | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gain > 70 points | 13 | 10% | Extraordinary transformation |
| Gain > 60 points | 70 | 56% | Very large transformation |
| Gain > 50 points | 97 | 78% | Substantial transformation |
| Post-Test ≥70 | 99 | 79% | Reached competency threshold |
| Gain > 100% (Ratio > 2×) | 120 | 96% | More than doubled initial competency |

The 96% figure in this table must be read in its proper context. This is not 96% of a sample selectively drawn from locations with favourable conditions. This is 96% of 125 districts and cities spread from Aceh to Papua, from Javanese cities with complete infrastructure to the highlands of Papua accessible only by small aircraft. Only 5 of 125 locations failed to achieve at least a doubling of scores, and even those five still recorded significant positive improvement. Not a single location declined.

Consistency of this kind is not the hallmark of a method that happened to succeed under certain conditions. It is the hallmark of a method that fundamentally works, everywhere, under any conditions.

**At the lower end of the spectrum**, four locations recorded gains below 30 points: Makassar (South Sulawesi, +24.6 points), Payakumbuh (West Sumatra, +26.8 points), Kampar (Riau, +27.5 points), and Dairi (North Sumatra, +29.3 points). The first three locations started with low initial scores yet ended below the competency threshold of 70, reflecting implementation conditions that warrant further attention.

Dairi, however, is a different story: with an initial score of 58.2, well above the national average of 17.92, a gain of 29.3 points brought it to 87.6, an excellent result. The relatively modest 29-point improvement in Dairi is not a sign of methodological weakness. It is the mathematical consequence of a high starting point: the closer one is to the ceiling, the smaller the room available for measured growth.

**At the upper end**, 13 locations recorded gains above 70 points: Tebo (Jambi, +82.0), Pringsewu (Lampung, +75.2), Hulu Sungai Tengah (South Kalimantan, +74.2), Parigi Moutong (Central Sulawesi, +72.8), West Manggarai (NTT, +72.5), Karanganyar (Central Java, +72.3), Landak (West Kalimantan, +72.1), Buru (Maluku, +71.3), Mentawai (West Sumatra, +71.2), Nagekeo (NTT, +71.2), Bombana (Southeast Sulawesi, +70.6), Central Buton (Southeast Sulawesi, +70.5), and North Penajam Paser (East Kalimantan, +70.2).

The most dramatic is Tebo, a district in the interior of Jambi that began at a score of 10.4 and reached 92.4, surpassing the DKI Jakarta average in two weeks. What is equally significant is that these 13 locations are distributed across eight different provinces, from Sumatra to Sulawesi, from Kalimantan to Nusa Tenggara. The highest gains are not concentrated in one favoured region. They are a pattern that emerges wherever implementation conditions are strong.

*(See Figure 10.9: Before and after GASING training for a student from Tebo District, Jambi. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aQW_Ol27WxA)*

*(See Figure 10.10: Before and after GASING training for a student from Nagekeo District, NTT. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N6GIJWDKPCM)*

---

### The Distribution of Improvement Ratios: What the Shape of the Data Reveals

A mean tells the story of a typical outcome. But the shape of the entire distribution tells a different and richer story: about the range of what is possible, about where the method is most powerful, and about whether the impressive results are concentrated at one unusual extreme or spread consistently across the full spectrum of locations.

*(See Figure 10.11: Histogram of student improvement ratios across all 125 districts/cities. Teal line = mean (6.61×); dark blue dashed line = median (4.79×). The extended right tail reaching 46.86× represents districts with very low baselines.)*

The histogram in Figure 10.11 contains more information than a single average can capture. The first two figures to note are a **mean of 6.61×** and a **median of 4.79×**. In statistics, when the mean substantially exceeds the median, the distribution has a longer tail on the right side, meaning some locations show far higher improvements than the typical case. That is precisely what the histogram shows: most locations cluster in the range of 2 to 8 times improvement, but a number of locations show far higher improvements, even reaching 46.9 times in Puncak, Central Papua, pulling the mean value significantly upward.

This extended right tail is not an anomaly that distorts the overall picture. It is a signal about the maximum capacity of this method when applied to the most challenging conditions.

In educational programme evaluation, a positively skewed distribution in field data typically has two causes: either an inconsistent method that succeeds only in some locations, generating artificial positive outliers, or a method that systematically provides the greatest benefit to participants with the greatest deficits. The GASING method data clearly demonstrates the latter. The locations in the right tail, **Puncak** (46.9×, pre = 1.0), **Kotabaru** (33.69×, pre = 1.9), **West Seram** (24.74×, pre = 2.9), **Dogiyai** (21.39×, pre = 3.2), and **Arfak Mountains** (21.23×, pre = 2.6), are without exception the locations with the lowest initial scores in the entire dataset. Their extreme ratios are not statistical luck. They are the first manifestation of the inverse effectiveness gradient that Section 10.4 will examine in full.

Equally important is the shape of the main body of the distribution. The majority of locations are concentrated in the 2 to 8 times improvement range, forming a clear and consistent central peak. This is what statistical theory calls a strong *central tendency*: there is a typical value that can be anticipated, not a wide and unpredictable scatter. The **median of 4.79×** is a figure of direct practical significance: a programme planner considering GASING implementation in a new district can realistically anticipate that student scores will improve by nearly five times in two weeks of training, based on evidence already documented from 125 locations.

The *worst-case downside* documented, even in the five locations with the lowest improvement ratios, **Dairi** (1.50×), **Banyuwangi** (1.62×), **Lembata** (1.87×), **Surabaya Kemenag** (1.90×), and **Pekanbaru** (1.99×), remains a positive and meaningful improvement. Banyuwangi, for instance, with "only" 1.62×, achieved a post-test of 92.8 from a baseline of 57.4. Not a single one of 125 locations recorded a decline or stagnation.

---

### The Transformation in Mamberamo Tengah: What the Numbers Look Like in a Life

Before proceeding to the formal analysis of effect size, one case from the dataset deserves to be examined in full. It is the most visually and humanly immediate document in this entire chapter.

*(See Figure 10.12: Transformation of division ability before and after training in Kelila, Mamberamo Tengah, Papua. Left panel: pre-intervention, consisting only of tally marks. Right panel: post-intervention, showing complex division calculations — 84÷2=42; 8,484÷6=1,090.5; 9,368÷8=1,170. Both tasks completed within the same timeframe: 1 minute 41 seconds. Transformation occurred within two weeks.)*

Figure 10.12 is one of the most powerful documents in this entire chapter. Not because it is visually dramatic, although it undeniably is, but because it makes concrete something that no statistical table can convey: the distance between two worlds that a single child traversed in fourteen days.

The left panel records the starting point. A student from Kelila District, Mamberamo Tengah, Highland Papua Province, faced a division problem with the only tool available: tally marks. He scratched line after line, attempting to count using the most basic method he had ever known. In 1 minute 41 seconds, he did not manage to complete a single problem. Not because he was not trying. Not because he was not intelligent. But because tally marks, which can only represent numbers as collections of scratches, have no mechanism for performing division. He was attempting to construct a building with a tool that can only mark the ground.

The right panel records what happened two weeks later. The photograph was taken at the 88-second mark, and the third problem was still being worked on. In 1 minute 41 seconds, the same student completed three four-digit division problems: 84 ÷ 2 = 42, 8,484 ÷ 6 = 1,090.5, and 9,368 ÷ 8 = 1,170. The third problem involves a decimal result, which means understanding of place value, algorithmic procedure, and fraction representation, all formed within two weeks. The time that previously was not enough to complete even one tally-based attempt was now sufficient to correctly solve three complex division problems.

Data from 192 Mamberamo Tengah students confirms that this image is not a special case selected for dramatic effect. The average score of Mamberamo Tengah students before training was **5.57 out of 100**. After two weeks, their average reached **73.10**, an improvement of **13.12 times**, almost twice the national average of 6.64 times. Their division score moved from 4.36 to 66.47, with an improvement ratio of 15.25 times.

Among the 31 Papua districts in this dataset, Mamberamo Tengah ranks **eighth**. Above it are districts with even more challenging initial conditions: Puncak (46.86×), Dogiyai (21.39×), Arfak Mountains (21.23×), Puncak Jaya (18.53×), Teluk Wondama (16.90×), Supiori (14.48×), and South Manokwari (14.22×). Mamberamo Tengah is not an exceptional case. It is part of a consistent and predictable pattern.

---

> *The leap visible in Figure 10.12 is not about intelligence suddenly ignited. It is about potential that was always there, and a tool that finally matched that potential.*

---

## 10.3 Analysis of Effect Size and Statistical Significance

### Two Questions, Two Tools

The improvement in scores from pre-test to post-test is necessary information, but not sufficient. The figure 17.92 becoming 76.40 sounds dramatic, and indeed it is, but without proper statistical context it cannot answer the most important question for policy: how large is this impact compared with other interventions ever attempted anywhere in the world?

To answer that question, two statistical tools are needed that work together but answer different questions. The p-value answers: *is there a real difference, or could this have happened by chance?* The d-value answers: *how large is that difference, and how does it compare with everything else ever tried?*

Both questions matter. But they are not equal. A programme can be statistically significant while being practically trivial, producing a real but tiny improvement that policy cannot usefully build on. The d-value is where the honest reckoning happens.

---

### The p-Value: Is This Real?

The p-value falls between 0 and 1, and measures the probability that an observed difference occurred by chance. The smaller the p-value, the more convincingly the difference is genuine rather than coincidental. International convention establishes that a result is considered statistically significant when p < 0.05, meaning the probability of chance is below 5%.

From the GASING method data, comprising 15,650 students across 125 districts and 7,983 teachers across 127 districts, all statistical tests produced values of **p < 0.001**. The probability of these results occurring by chance is less than one in a thousand. This is the strongest level of statistical evidence routinely reported in academic research.

The p-value, in this dataset, is settled. The results are real. The more interesting question is how large they are.

---

### The d-Value: How Large Is This?

Cohen's d, developed by statistician Jacob Cohen in 1988 and now the international standard in educational research, measures the difference between two means in units of standard deviation:

> **d = (Mean Post-Test − Mean Pre-Test) / Pooled Standard Deviation**

To understand what "standard deviation" means as a unit of measurement, consider a simple example. In a class, the average mathematics score is 45 with a standard deviation of 10. A student who scores 55 is exactly one standard deviation above the average, meaning she is higher than approximately 84% of her classmates. In Cohen's notation, her position relative to the class average is equivalent to d = 1.0.

Applied to a training context: d = 1.0 means a participant who was initially at the 50th percentile moves to the 84th percentile after training, surpassing 34% of peers who were previously above them.

The greatest advantage of the d-value is its universality. A d-value of 1.0 means exactly the same thing whether it was generated from a 0-to-100 scale or a 0-to-500 scale, from research in Highland Papua or in Finland, from a sample of 50 or 50,000. It is the common language that makes honest cross-study comparison possible.

**Table 10.6.** Interpretation of Cohen's d Effect Size in Educational Research.

| Effect Size | Category | Practical Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| d = 0.2 | Small | Difference exists but is difficult to detect without measurement |
| d = 0.5 | Medium | Difference is fairly visible and practically meaningful |
| d = 0.8 | Large | Difference is clearly visible and highly meaningful |
| d > 1.0 | Very Large | Dramatic difference, rarely found in the literature |
| d > 2.0 | Extraordinary | Fundamental transformation, extremely rare in educational research |

To place this table in global context: Hattie (2009), in his landmark synthesis of more than 800 meta-analyses covering millions of students across dozens of countries, found that the typical effect size of successful educational interventions ranged between d = 0.2 and d = 0.4. Intensive one-on-one tutoring programmes, among the most expensive and most effective interventions known, average around d = 0.8 to 1.0. Kraft (2020) established that effect sizes above 1.0 are already rare and merit serious attention, while those above 2.0 are very rarely documented with verifiable methodology.

---

> **Reference points:**
> - Typical effect size of educational interventions: d = 0.2 to 0.4
> - Effect size > 1.0 is rare
> - Effect size > 2.0 is extraordinary and requires verification

---

### Why Effect Size Matters More Than Test Scores

There are four reasons why the d-value is more useful than raw score comparisons in evaluating and comparing educational interventions.

First, it is independent of sample size. A p-value from research with 100 participants and one with 100,000 cannot be directly compared. The d-values from both can be.

Second, it enables comparison across studies and countries. A d-value of 1.0 from research in Jayapura and a d-value of 1.0 from research in Seoul speak the same language. This allows the GASING method's effectiveness to be placed honestly on the map of global literature, not merely celebrated in isolation.

Third, it is practically meaningful. A policymaker does not need to understand inferential statistics to grasp that d = 5.06 represents a fundamental transformation, while d = 0.2 represents a change barely perceptible without measurement.

Fourth, it assists rational allocation of public resources. When budgets are limited and many programmes compete for funding, effect size provides an objective basis for comparison: how much real change is produced per rupiah invested?

---

### The GASING Method Effect Sizes: The Numbers

*(See Figure 10.13: Effect size analysis (Cohen's d) per arithmetic operation for the GASING method. Dark blue bars = teachers, teal bars = students. All values massively exceed the "large" threshold of d = 0.8. Division shows the highest values: Teacher d = 5.96; Student d = 6.35.)*

Figure 10.13 presents the Cohen's d effect size for each arithmetic operation. The red dashed line at the bottom of the chart marks the d = 0.8 threshold, which by international standards is already classified as a "large" effect. Every bar in this chart towers far above that line, making the reference line appear like a very low floor compared with what was actually achieved.

The chart tells one very consistent trend: the bars grow taller from left to right. Addition yields the shortest bars, division the tallest, and the composite BAKALKUBAGI falls between them. This trend holds for both teachers and students without exception, and it holds for a reason: as will be discussed, the pattern is not accidental. It is the same inverse effectiveness gradient operating at the level of individual operations.

**Table 10.7.** Summary of Cohen's d Effect Sizes and p-Values per Arithmetic Operation.

| Metric / Operation | Teacher (d) | Student (d) | p-Value | Context |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| BAKALKUBAGI (Overall) | 3.04 | 5.06 | < 0.001 | 4 to 6× the "large" threshold |
| Addition | 1.85 | 3.41 | < 0.001 | 2 to 4× the "large" threshold |
| Subtraction | 2.41 | 4.56 | < 0.001 | 3 to 6× the "large" threshold |
| Multiplication | 3.16 | 5.30 | < 0.001 | 4 to 7× the "large" threshold |
| Division (highest) | 5.96 | 6.35 | < 0.001 | 7 to 8× the "large" threshold |

Read the Context column carefully. Division with d = 6.35 for students is seven to eight times what is already considered highly meaningful by international standards. Even addition, the lowest d in this table at 3.41 for students, is still two to four times the "large" threshold. There is no operation in this table, for either teachers or students, that falls below the "extraordinary" category by global standards.

The uniform p < 0.001 in the p-Value column settles the first question with certainty: the GASING method produced a real difference in every operation, for both participant groups, without exception.

---

### The Pattern: The More Difficult, the Greater the Impact

The progression in Table 10.7 is not random. It is consistent and explicable.

Addition, as the most fundamental operation and the one with the highest pre-test scores (national average 39.3 for students), produces the lowest effect size: d = 3.41. Subtraction: d = 4.56. Multiplication: d = 5.30. Division, the most complex operation with the lowest initial scores (national average 7.5 for students), produces the highest effect size: d = 6.35.

The more difficult the operation, the lower the baseline, and the lower the baseline, the larger the effect size. This is the same pattern observed geographically in Section 10.1, the same pattern that will be formally quantified as the inverse effectiveness gradient in Section 10.4, now appearing at the level of individual operations. It is not a coincidence appearing in multiple forms. It is a single principle manifesting consistently across every dimension of this dataset.

---

### Effect Size by Province: The Story Behind the National Average

The national figure of d = 5.06 is an average that conceals rich variation beneath it. When the analysis descends to the provincial level, patterns hidden within the national figure begin to emerge, and the story they tell is far richer than a single number can convey.

Three findings from this table deserve specific attention.

**Central Kalimantan at d = 37.76** stands at the top of the list by a very large margin. However, this value must be read with appropriate caution: Central Kalimantan is represented by only 2 districts in the dataset, making its effect size highly sensitive to inter-district variation. It is a compelling signal, not yet a stable conclusion. More districts are needed before a provincial generalisation can be made.

**Central Java at d = 14.53 is the most significant finding in this table**, and it deserves more attention than it typically receives. Central Java is not a 3T region. It has relatively good educational infrastructure. Its baseline was 23.8, close to the national average. Yet it produced the second-highest effect size in the dataset, with an average post-test of 90.0, the highest nationally, with very high inter-district consistency. This proves that high effect sizes are not the exclusive domain of disadvantaged regions. When the GASING method is implemented well, it produces extraordinary results regardless of the starting conditions.

**Riau (d = 3.19) and West Sumatra (d = 2.32)** sit at the bottom of the chart and require attention. Both still far exceed the "very large" threshold (d > 1.0) by international standards, but both produced post-test averages below the national competency threshold of 70. West Sumatra at d = 2.32 with a post-test of 56.2 is the lowest nationally on both metrics. Both provinces signal that implementation conditions there may require additional support or a second training cycle, not that the method failed, but that the journey from their particular starting points requires more than two weeks to complete.

Critically: even d = 2.32, the lowest value in this entire chart, is still more than double the "extraordinary" threshold in global educational research. Not a single province in this table produces a mediocre effect size by any international standard.

---

### A Positive Anomaly in Global Literature

GASING method effect sizes ranging from 1.85 to 6.35, with overall averages of d = 3.04 for teachers and d = 5.06 for students, are not merely "large." They occupy a territory that the global educational research community describes as a **positive anomaly**: results that compel a re-examination of assumptions about the limits of human learning capacity.

To place these figures in the most honest context: effect sizes above 2.0 are already categorised in the literature as "extraordinary and requiring verification." The GASING method produces effect sizes ranging from 1.85 to 6.35, from data on 23,633 participants across 125 to 127 districts in 33 provinces, with p < 0.001 for all operations and both participant groups.

These are not figures from one selected school under ideal conditions. They are figures from national-scale implementation, from Aceh to Highland Papua, in real classrooms with all their limitations.

Compared with typical educational interventions (d = 0.2 to 0.4), the GASING method demonstrates **10 to 15 times greater effectiveness**. Compared with intensive one-on-one tutoring programmes, the most effective and most expensive intervention known (d = 0.8 to 1.0), the GASING method produces **4 to 8 times greater impact**, in far larger groups, in far shorter time.

---

> *Policy Implication: When an intervention demonstrates effect sizes 10 to 15 times larger than the global average of educational interventions, with p < 0.001 from data of 23,633 participants across 33 provinces, the question is no longer "Is this worth considering?"*
>
> **The question is: why has this not yet become a national priority?**

---

## 10.4 Analysis of the Inverse Effectiveness Gradient

### A Pattern That Runs Counter to Intuition

Most educational interventions share a quiet bias. They tend to benefit most those who are already closest to competency: students with a partial foundation to build on, teachers with enough prior understanding to absorb new material quickly, regions with adequate infrastructure to implement programmes reliably. The result is a predictable dynamic: the strong get stronger, the gap between the advantaged and the disadvantaged widens, and the children who most needed the intervention benefit from it least.

The GASING method data shows the opposite.

The lower the starting point, the greater the transformation. The further a district is from competency at the outset, the further it travels toward it in two weeks. The regions that every national education report has described as "difficult to intervene in" are precisely the regions that responded most dramatically when the right method arrived.

This is not an impression from a few striking examples. It is a statistically measurable pattern, consistent without exception across 125 districts and all four arithmetic operations, confirmed by two independent correlation methods that arrive at the same conclusion from different mathematical directions.

This pattern has a name: the **Inverse Effectiveness Gradient**.

---

### Definition and Mechanism

The Inverse Effectiveness Gradient is a phenomenon in which an intervention produces its greatest impact on the group that is furthest behind. The word "inverse" refers to the relationship that runs counter to the general pattern in educational interventions: rather than widening the gap between the strong and the weak, it narrows it. Rather than helping the capable advance more rapidly, it lifts the most disadvantaged furthest.

In the context of GASING method data, this means: districts with the lowest pre-test scores consistently show the highest improvement ratios after training. Conversely, districts with the highest pre-test scores show more moderate improvement ratios, though their absolute post-test scores remain high.

The contrast between two districts makes this concrete before the statistics do:

---

> **Puncak** (Central Papua): pre = 1.0 → post = 46.9 → improvement ratio **46.86×**, the highest in the entire dataset.
>
> **Dairi** (North Sumatra): pre = 58.2 → post = 87.6 → improvement ratio **1.50×**, the lowest in the entire dataset.
>
> *Both districts used an identical method, for the same duration. The only difference was the starting point.*

---

### Statistical Evidence: Two Methods, One Conclusion

The inverse effectiveness gradient is not a visual impression derived from extreme cases. It is a pattern measurable through two different statistical approaches, both of which arrive at the same conclusion from independent mathematical directions.

**A. Pearson Correlation: The Linear Relationship**

Pearson correlation measures the strength of the linear relationship between two variables. For the 125 districts in this dataset, the correlation between pre-test scores and improvement ratios yields **r = −0.620** with **p = 1.33 × 10⁻¹⁴**.

Three figures to understand:

**r = −0.620**: a strong negative correlation. The lower the baseline, the higher the improvement ratio. The negative sign is the mathematical signature of the inverse gradient.

**p = 1.33 × 10⁻¹⁴**: the probability of this relationship occurring by chance is, for practical purposes, zero. For context: p < 0.001 alone is already considered "highly significant" in academic research. This value is fourteen orders of magnitude below that threshold.

**R² = 0.384**: pre-test scores alone explain 38.4% of the variation in improvement ratios across districts. This is a very large influence from a single variable, especially in field data where dozens of uncontrolled factors are simultaneously at work.

**B. Spearman Correlation: The Rank Relationship**

Spearman correlation is a more robust version because it does not assume a normal distribution and is not sensitive to extreme outliers. It measures whether districts with lower pre-test *rankings* tend to have higher improvement ratio *rankings*, regardless of the actual values involved. The result: **ρ = −0.937** with **p < 10⁻⁵⁷**.

A Spearman value of ρ = −0.937 is near-perfect rank correspondence. If 125 districts are sorted from lowest to highest baseline score, that ordering is almost identical to the ordering from highest to lowest improvement ratio. The two lists are nearly mirror images of each other.

The difference between the Pearson (−0.620) and Spearman (−0.937) values contains important information. The lower Pearson is caused by extreme outliers like Puncak (46.9×) that have high leverage in a linear model. The near-perfect Spearman shows that even when those outliers are set aside, the rank pattern remains consistently strong across the entire spectrum of data. This means the inverse effectiveness gradient is not produced by a few dramatic cases at the extreme. It is a systematic law operating from the lowest baseline to the highest.

**Table 10.9.** Summary of Correlation Analysis Results: Pre-Test vs. Improvement Ratio (n = 125 Districts/Cities).

| Metric | Value | p-Value | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pearson r | −0.620 | 1.33 × 10⁻¹⁴ | Strong negative correlation |
| Spearman ρ | −0.937 | < 10⁻⁵⁷ | Near-perfect rank correlation |
| R² (Pearson) | 0.384 | --- | 38.4% of variance explained |
| Regression slope | −0.352 | < 0.001 | Each +1 pre-test point → −0.35× ratio |
| Regression intercept | 12.924 | < 0.001 | Predicted ratio when pre = 0 |

---

### The Scatter Plot: Where the Pattern Lives

*(See Figure 10.16: Inverse Effectiveness Gradient: scatter plot of the relationship between pre-test scores and improvement ratios for 125 districts/cities. Dark teal = Papua; medium teal = Other 3T; light teal = Non-3T. The red regression line demonstrates a consistent negative trend.)*

Figure 10.16 makes the pattern visible in a way that no table can replicate. Each of the 125 districts appears as a single dot, positioned by its pre-test score on the horizontal axis and its improvement ratio on the vertical axis.

The immediately apparent pattern is the concentration of dark teal dots in the upper-left corner: Papua districts cluster on the left, where pre-test scores are lowest, and simultaneously at the top, where improvement ratios are highest. Puncak stands alone in the far upper-left corner with a ratio of 46.86×, a point so extreme it almost cannot share a chart with the rest of the data.

Moving right along the horizontal axis, the dots move downward following a clear and consistent trend. Non-3T districts cluster on the right side, with pre-tests of 20 to 40 points and more moderate ratios generally below 5 times. The descending red regression line from upper-left to lower-right summarises this mathematically: each additional point in the pre-test score is associated with a 0.352-point decrease in the improvement ratio.

What is most statistically significant is the Spearman ρ = −0.937. This means that if 125 districts are ranked by pre-test score from lowest to highest, the resulting ordering is nearly identical to the ordering of improvement ratios from highest to lowest. The strength of this rank correspondence, which is not sensitive to the extreme outliers that pull the Pearson value down, proves that this pattern is not an artifact of a few dramatic cases. It is a law operating consistently across the entire data spectrum, from districts with a pre-test of 1.0 to those beginning at 58.2.

*(See Figure 10.17: Before and after GASING training in Puncak District, Central Papua. Addition. Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VMFZgIEIGG4)*

---

### Group Analysis: Four Segments, One Consistent Story

To understand the inverse effectiveness gradient more concretely, the 125 districts can be divided into four groups based on their pre-test scores. The table below shows how improvement ratios vary dramatically, and consistently, across these groups.

**Table 10.10.** Analysis by Initial Ability Group for Students (n = 125 Districts/Cities).

| Group | n Districts | n Students | Pre | Post | % ≥70 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Very Low (< 10) | 31 | 3,744 | 6.2 | 68.6 | 68% | 14.04× |
| Low (10–20) | 55 | 6,459 | 15.4 | 75.9 | 78% | 5.08× |
| Moderate (20–30) | 22 | 2,836 | 24.5 | 80.6 | 82% | 3.33× |
| High (> 30) | 17 | 2,611 | 39.0 | 86.9 | 100% | 2.30× |
| NATIONAL | 125 | 15,650 | 17.9 | 76.4 | 79% | 6.61× |

Three findings from this table merit careful attention.

**First: the ratio decreases monotonically from the bottom group to the top.** The very-low-baseline group produces a ratio of 14.04×, nearly six times the ratio of the high-baseline group (2.30×). This decrease is consistent with no exceptions: every higher group produces a lower ratio. This is the statistical signature of a genuine gradient, not a pattern created by a few outliers.

**Second: the absolute gain is nearly equal across all groups, even though the ratios differ enormously.** The very-low group achieves an increase of 62.4 points (6.2 → 68.6), while the high group achieves 47.9 points (39.0 → 86.9). The difference in absolute gain is only about 14 points, even though the baselines differ by 33 points. The GASING method produces nearly equal absolute improvements regardless of starting point. The dramatic difference in ratios is a mathematical consequence of different denominators, not evidence that the method works fundamentally differently for different groups. It works consistently. The lower starting point simply amplifies the visible impact.

**Third: all groups reach or approach the competency threshold of 70.** The high-baseline group achieves a 100% success rate: all 17 districts, without exception, brought students to a post-test of ≥70. The very-low group achieves 68%, meaning that of the 31 districts with the most challenging initial conditions in the entire dataset, 21 still succeeded in crossing the competency threshold within two weeks. The remaining 10 districts require further training cycles, not as a sign of failure, but as a precisely identified direction for the next intervention.

*(See Figure 10.18: Average improvement ratios per initial ability group compared with the national average, and pre-test/post-test scores per group.)*

*(See Figure 10.19: Proportion of districts achieving post-test ≥70 per initial ability group. The high-baseline group achieves 100%; the very-low-baseline group achieves 68%, demonstrating that even the most disadvantaged districts largely achieved competency.)*

---

### The Ten Districts of Greatest Transformation

Three things in this table are worth noting. First, eight of the ten districts have pre-test scores below 6, far below the national average of 17.9. Second, seven of the ten come from Papua provinces, with three from Central Papua alone. Third, the two Non-3T districts in this list, Kotabaru and North Penajam Paser, appear not because of their administrative classification but because of their very low baselines: 1.9 and 3.9 respectively. Geographic or administrative status is not the primary predictor. The depth of the pre-existing deficit is.

These ten districts share one thing in common: they came to training with a near-blank mathematical slate. The GASING method, which builds from the most fundamental foundation in an enjoyable and multimodal way, produced its most dramatic impact precisely here. This is the essence of the inverse effectiveness gradient: not that the method works better in difficult places, but that it is designed to unlock potential that has long been locked, and that potential is most locked in the places that have been longest neglected.

---

### A Predictive Model: What Can Be Anticipated Before Training Begins

One of the practical implications of this strong correlation is that it enables a predictive model: given a district's pre-test score, what improvement ratio can realistically be anticipated?

---

> **Regression Equation:**
>
> **Predicted Ratio = −0.352 × Pre-Test + 12.923**
>
> *(r = −0.620, R² = 0.384, p < 0.001, n = 125)*

---

**Table 10.12.** Predicted Improvement Ratios Based on Pre-Test Scores.

| Pre-Test Score | Predicted Ratio | Example Region | Practical Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 12.5× | Puncak (1.0) | From near-zero to approaching competency |
| 5 | 11.2× | Dogiyai (3.2) | Highest potential zone |
| 10 | 9.4× | Highland Papua (8.3) | Very high responsiveness |
| 20 | 5.9× | North Sulawesi (21.5) | Above national average |
| 30 | 3.9× | North Sumatra (31.6) | Significant improvement |
| 40 | 1.8× | East Java (36.4) | Meaningful by global standards |

This equation is a practical tool for implementation planning. An education coordinator who knows the average pre-test score of their students can use this model to anticipate training impact and plan accordingly. The prediction figures are conservative lower estimates, not optimistic upper limits: actual outcomes from districts like Puncak show that real results can far exceed the model's predictions when initial conditions are most extreme.

The model also has a boundary: it should not be extrapolated beyond the range of data on which it is built. A pre-test score of 50 would yield a negative predicted ratio, which is mathematically impossible and reflects the limits of a linear model, not a real expectation about programme outcomes.

---

### Why Does This Gradient Occur? Four Mechanisms

This pattern does not emerge from a vacuum. At least four mutually reinforcing mechanisms explain why the GASING method is systematically more effective for groups with lower baselines.

**First: greater room for change.** This is the simplest mechanism mathematically. A student with a score of 5 has 65 points of room to reach the maximum, while a student with a score of 50 has only 50. In ratio terms, the difference is far more dramatic: from 5 to 70 is a 14-fold increase, while from 50 to 70 is only 1.4-fold. The floor effect is real, but it alone is insufficient to explain the full magnitude of this gradient, because if the floor effect were the only mechanism, every good method would show the same pattern. The GASING method's gradient is stronger than would be expected from mathematics alone.

**Second: dissolution of accumulated mathematics anxiety.** Students with very low baselines have almost certainly accumulated deep mathematics anxiety over years. Ashcraft (2002) showed that mathematics anxiety directly burdens working memory, reducing the cognitive capacity available for calculation. The GASING method, with its playful atmosphere, songs, celebrated successes, and non-judgmental learning environment, is one of the few interventions explicitly designed to reduce this anxiety. For students with the deepest anxiety, this unblocking effect is most dramatic, and its impact on learning capacity is immediate and measurable.

**Third: unlocking latent potential.** Students in disadvantaged regions with low baselines do not lack cognitive capacity. They have simply never encountered a method suited to how their brains learn. When the right method finally arrives, potential that has long been locked releases rapidly. This is distinct from the floor effect: the floor effect concerns mathematical room, while this mechanism concerns previously obstructed cognitive access that the right conditions can open.

**Fourth: stronger multimodal effect on a thin foundation.** Students with an already-sufficient foundation have formed mathematical representations, even if not always correct ones. Modifying existing representations is often harder than building new ones. Students with very low baselines, by contrast, arrive with little or no established representation that needs to be replaced. GASING's multimodal approach can build correct representations from scratch, without competing against ingrained habits or partially-formed misconceptions.

All four mechanisms reinforce each other. The student with the lowest baseline experiences the greatest anxiety reduction, has the most room for growth, arrives with the least entrenched incorrect representation, and receives the full benefit of multimodal construction. The result is the pattern visible in every dimension of this dataset.

---

### Four Policy Implications

---

> **1. Prioritise the most disadvantaged regions.** Data shows the highest return on investment is precisely in regions with the lowest baselines. Prioritising Papua and 3T areas is not only an equity decision. It is also, empirically, the optimal investment strategy.
>
> **2. Do not measure success only by absolute post-test scores.** A district moving from 5 to 50 has achieved a tenfold transformation that is extraordinary, even if the absolute score remains below 70. Progress metrics must account for the starting point.
>
> **3. This gradient is mathematical proof that the GASING method actively reduces educational inequality.** Spearman ρ = −0.937 is not a narrative claim. It is a verified statistical finding.
>
> **4. Plan convergence through multiple cycles.** Districts in the very-low-baseline group now have post-test scores (68.6) approaching the national competency threshold. A second training cycle can bring them to full parity. The gradient does not disappear after one cycle; it continues to operate.

---

### What This Means for Indonesian Education

The inverse effectiveness gradient is not merely an interesting academic finding. It reframes the most persistent narrative in Indonesian education policy about disadvantaged regions.

**Papua is not a problem. Papua is the biggest opportunity.** The old narrative about Papua as a region "difficult to intervene in" educationally has no empirical foundation. Data shows the opposite: Papua is the region with the highest responsiveness to the GASING method, with a mean ratio of 10.04×, a median of 6.73×, and a Cohen's d against Non-3T of 0.950. Six of the ten districts with the greatest transformation nationwide come from Papua.

**3T status is a predictor of responsiveness, not a barrier.** Statistical tests show that Papua and Other 3T areas do not differ significantly from each other (p = 0.085), but both differ significantly from Non-3T regions (Papua: p = 4.65 × 10⁻⁸; Other 3T: p = 1.53 × 10⁻⁵). This means the characteristic that has long been read as a limitation is, in the context of the GASING method, a predictor of high responsiveness.

**The convergence is empirical evidence of educational equity in action.** The gap between territory types narrows from 13.6 points before training to 8.9 points afterward. All groups achieve post-tests above 70. The GASING method does not only lift all groups; it lifts the lowest proportionally more, so the distance between groups narrows with each training cycle.

In a country with geographic inequality as vast as Indonesia's, this property goes beyond ordinary educational metrics. It is a verified, replicable mechanism for closing a gap that has been widening for decades.

---

> *"When the same intervention gives the group with the lowest starting point a ratio of 14× and the group with the highest starting point a ratio of 2×, it is not just a teaching method. It is a statistically verified instrument for reducing educational inequality."*

---

Perubahan utama dari versi asli, Pak Yohanes:

**Pembuka** diganti dari definisi teknis langsung menjadi narasi tentang bias diam-diam yang ada di sebagian besar intervensi pendidikan, sehingga "inverse" gradient terasa genuinely mengejutkan sebelum datanya disajikan.

**Dua metode korelasi** (Pearson dan Spearman) dijelaskan secara berurutan dengan argumen mengapa keduanya diperlukan, dan apa artinya perbedaan antara keduanya: Pearson dipengaruhi outlier, Spearman tidak, dan keduanya sampai pada kesimpulan yang sama. Itu justru memperkuat argumen.

**Group analysis** sekarang memiliki tiga temuan yang diuraikan dengan presisi, khususnya temuan kedua tentang absolute gain yang hampir sama di semua kelompok, yang merupakan insight paling penting tapi paling mudah terlewat.

**Empat mekanisme** diberikan narasi yang lebih kaya, terutama "dissolution of accumulated mathematics anxiety" yang dihubungkan ke Ashcraft (2002).

**Penutup policy** dipertegas dengan kalimat "3T status is a predictor of responsiveness, not a barrier" yang merupakan reframing yang paling kuat dari seluruh section ini.
