## 10.5 Regional Analysis: Papua and 3T Areas

### From National Pattern to Regional Reality

Section 10.4 established the inverse effectiveness gradient as a national-level statistical law. This section descends to the regional level to examine whether that law holds when the data is disaggregated by territory type, and what it looks like in the specific geography of Indonesia's most disadvantaged regions.

The analysis distinguishes three categories: Papua (six Papua provinces), Other 3T (frontier, outermost, and underdeveloped regions outside Papua), and Non-3T. Statistical tests confirm not only that the differences between these categories are real, but that the size of those differences carries direct implications for how national education investment should be allocated.

---

### The Regional Classification

**Table 10.13.** Regional Categories of GASING Training Participants.

| Category | n Districts | n Students | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) | 31 | 5,707 | 6 Papua provinces: Papua Province, Central Papua, Highland Papua, West Papua, Southwest Papua, South Papua |
| Other 3T | 40 | 3,714 | Maluku, North Maluku, NTT, Southeast Sulawesi, Central Sulawesi, West Kalimantan, Central Kalimantan, South Kalimantan, West Sumatra, Bengkulu, Jambi |
| Non-3T | 54 | 6,229 | Java, Bali, North Sumatra, North Sulawesi, South Sulawesi, DKI Jakarta, Riau, Lampung, Aceh, Bangka Belitung, Yogyakarta, Gorontalo, South Sumatra, East Kalimantan |

---

### Three Levels of Responsiveness: The Core Finding

**Table 10.14.** Comparative Analysis of Three Territory Types, GASING Method Students.

| Territory Type | n Districts | n Students | Pre | Post | Ratio | d | % ≥70 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) | 31 | 5,707 | 10.80 | 71.28 | 10.04× | 8.49 | 74% |
| Other 3T | 40 | 3,714 | 14.65 | 75.23 | 7.46× | 5.70 | 78% |
| All 3T | 71 | 9,421 | 12.97 | 73.51 | 8.59× | 6.44 | 76% |
| Non-3T | 54 | 6,229 | 24.43 | 80.20 | 4.02× | 4.63 | 83% |
| National | 125 | 15,650 | 17.92 | 76.40 | 6.61× | 5.06 | 79% |

Read this table column by column, and each column tells a different chapter of the same story.

The Pre column, read from top to bottom, is a portrait of historical inequality: Papua 10.80, Other 3T 14.65, Non-3T 24.43. Papua districts begin training with average scores less than half those of Non-3T districts. That 13.6-point gap is not a gap in children's intelligence. It is the accumulated weight of decades of unequal access to quality teachers, adequate infrastructure, and educational resources that urban Indonesia has long taken for granted.

The Post column shows where those same districts arrived: Papua 71.28, Other 3T 75.23, Non-3T 80.20. All three groups surpassed the competency threshold of 70. The gap that was initially 13.6 points between Papua and Non-3T narrowed to 8.9 points. The GASING method did not merely lift all groups. It lifted the lowest proportionally more, actively closing a divide that has been widening for generations.

The Ratio column makes the asymmetry explicit: 10.04× for Papua, 7.46× for Other 3T, 4.02× for Non-3T. The All-3T row yields a ratio of 8.59×, more than twice the Non-3T ratio of 4.02×. More than half of all participants in this dataset came from the most disadvantaged regions, and those regions consistently outperformed on every measure of responsiveness.

The d column adds a dimension the ratio alone cannot show. Papua d = 8.49, Other 3T d = 5.70, Non-3T d = 4.63. Even the "lowest" value of 4.63 for Non-3T is nearly six times the "large" threshold in international standards. There is no territory type in this table that produces anything less than an extraordinary effect size by any global benchmark.

The most policy-significant row in the table is All-3T: 71 districts and 9,421 students producing a ratio of 8.59× and d = 6.44, both exceeding the national figures of 6.61× and 5.06. More than half of all districts in the dataset, those in the most challenging regions, produce results that consistently surpass the national average on every metric. The regions that national planners typically treat as implementation risks are, in this data, the regions where the method delivers its most powerful returns.

---

### The Convergence: Watching the Gap Close

*(See Figure 10.21: Regional analysis showing distribution of student improvement ratios by territory type (boxplot) and average pre-test and post-test by territory type. The inverse gradient is clearly visible.)*

*(See Figure 10.22: Convergence from pre-test to post-test by territory type. Circles = pre-test; diamonds = post-test. Red dashed line = competency threshold (70). All three groups move toward and surpass the threshold.)*

Figure 10.22 is one of the most important visualisations in this chapter. Three groups that began at very different positions converge visibly after training, all arriving above or near the competency threshold. The Papua line has the steepest angle: beginning at 10.8 and ending at 71.3, it climbs the furthest in absolute terms and crosses the threshold that its starting position would have suggested was unreachable in two weeks.

What the figure makes viscerally clear, in a way that tables cannot, is that convergence is not a theoretical aspiration. It is a documented, measurable, repeated outcome. These three lines are drawn from real classrooms, real children, and real teachers across 125 districts in 33 provinces.

**Table 10.15.** Proportion of Districts Reaching Post-Test ≥70 by Territory Type.

| Territory Type | n Districts | Reached ≥70 | Percentage | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) | 31 | 23 | 74% | Majority succeeded |
| Other 3T | 40 | 31 | 78% | Majority succeeded |
| Non-3T | 54 | 45 | 83% | Majority succeeded |

The uniform "Majority succeeded" interpretation for all three territory types is a statement that deserves to be read slowly. Not a single territory type failed as a whole. In the group with the most challenging conditions, three quarters of districts crossed the competency threshold in two weeks.

The difference between 74% (Papua) and 83% (Non-3T) must be read carefully. Most of the 26% of Papua districts that did not reach 70 had post-test averages above 65, close to the threshold. They did not fail. They came very close, under conditions far more demanding than those of Non-3T districts. They represent the group most clearly identified as needing a second training cycle, not as evidence of methodological inadequacy.

---

### The Statistical Tests: Are These Differences Real?

**Kruskal-Wallis: Testing All Three Groups Simultaneously**

The Kruskal-Wallis test is a non-parametric equivalent to one-way ANOVA, used because the distribution of improvement ratios is not normal. It tests whether at least one group differs significantly from the others. Result: **H = 36.21, p < 0.001** (specifically p = 1.37 × 10⁻⁸). This is strong evidence that the three territory groups have statistically different improvement ratio distributions. The pattern is not a product of chance.

**Mann-Whitney U: Pairwise Comparisons**

**Table 10.16.** Mann-Whitney U Test Results: Pairwise Comparisons Between Territory Types.

| Comparison | U Statistic | p-Value | Conclusion |
|---|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) vs Non-3T | 1,436 | p = 4.65 × 10⁻⁸ | Highly significant |
| Other 3T vs Non-3T | 1,646 | p = 1.53 × 10⁻⁵ | Highly significant |
| Papua (3T) vs Other 3T | 769 | p = 0.085 | Not significant (p > 0.05) |

Two findings from this table must be read together. First, both Papua and Other 3T differ significantly from Non-3T, with very small p-values. The difference in responsiveness between 3T and Non-3T regions is a real and replicable pattern. Second, Papua and Other 3T do not differ significantly from each other (p = 0.085).

This second finding reveals something profound: the primary predictor of high responsiveness is not something uniquely Papuan. It is marginalisation in general. Papua leads in absolute figures because its baseline is lowest, not because there is something about Papua specifically that is absent from other 3T regions. The inverse effectiveness gradient is a property of disadvantage, not of geography. Any region that has long been denied access to quality education will respond powerfully when the right method finally arrives.

**Table 10.17.** Effect Size of Ratio Differences Between Territory Types (Cohen's d).

| Comparison | Cohen's d | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) vs Non-3T | d = 0.950 | Very large (approaching d > 1.0) |
| Other 3T vs Non-3T | d = 0.725 | Moderate to large |
| Papua (3T) vs Other 3T | d = 0.343 | Small to moderate (consistent with p = 0.085) |

Cohen's d = 0.950 for Papua vs. Non-3T means the distribution of improvement ratios for Papua sits nearly one full standard deviation above the Non-3T distribution. In practical terms, a difference this large is not only detectable in statistical tables. It is felt directly in the field, in the gap between what a trainer experiences working in Papua compared with working in a Java city.

---

### Teacher Transformation by Region

The inverse gradient is not limited to students. Teachers show the same pattern, and the teacher data carries an additional dimension: it documents not only what happened to participants, but the quality of the pedagogical foundation that will now reach students for the next fifteen years.

**Table 10.18.** Teacher Training Results by Territory Type.

| Territory Type | n Districts | n Teachers | Pre | Post | Δ | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Papua (3T) | 32 | 3,339 | 29.8 | 81.3 | 51.5 | 3.27× |
| Other 3T | 41 | 1,858 | 49.4 | 89.5 | 40.2 | 2.03× |
| Non-3T | 54 | 2,786 | 54.0 | 91.8 | 37.8 | 1.82× |

The Pre column speaks most directly about context: Papua teachers began at 29.8, Other 3T at 49.4, Non-3T at 54.0. The 24.2-point gap between Papua and Non-3T teachers is a figure that should be deeply uncomfortable for Indonesian education policy. These are trained, certified teachers, standing in front of classrooms every day, with a content knowledge gap of nearly 25 points relative to their counterparts in urban areas. This is not a personal failure. It is a structural one, produced by a system that deployed teachers to the most challenging regions without giving them the foundational preparation those regions required.

The Post column tells a different story: Papua 81.3, Other 3T 89.5, Non-3T 91.8. All three groups surpassed the 70-point threshold. The gap that was initially 24.2 points between Papua and Non-3T narrowed to 10.5 points. More than half of the historical inter-regional inequality among teachers was closed in two weeks.

The Ratio column holds: Papua teachers produce the highest ratio (3.27×), followed by Other 3T (2.03×) and Non-3T (1.82×). The same pattern holds for both participant groups, confirming that the inverse effectiveness gradient is not a phenomenon specific to children. It applies equally to adults, equally to trained professionals, equally to anyone who has long been operating with a deficit that the right method can finally address.

---

### Provincial and District Profiles: Portraits of Inequality and Convergence

Numbers can establish a pattern. But understanding what a pattern means requires descending to the level where it has faces and names.

**The Baseline: What the Starting Conditions Actually Look Like**

*(See Figure 10.23: Distribution of pre-test scores by province, sorted lowest to highest. Dark teal = Papua; medium teal = Other 3T; light teal = Non-3T. Papua provinces cluster at the far left with baseline scores of 8 to 13 points. DKI Jakarta at far right with a baseline of 38.5 points. A disparity of nearly 10 times.)*

The colour pattern in Figure 10.23 is not subtle. Dark teal and medium teal dominate the left side of the chart. Light teal dominates the right. This is not a random distribution. It is a graphic representation of a structural reality: the provinces where children have the least have also received the least from the national education system.

At the far left, pre-tests below 15 points. At the far right, pre-tests above 30. DKI Jakarta at 38.5 is more than nine times the baseline of East Kalimantan at 3.9. A child in the capital and a child in a remote district of East Kalimantan enter training with a gap between them that neither of them created and neither of them deserved.

**Table 10.19.** Provinces with Lowest and Highest Baselines: Pre-Test, Post-Test, and Improvement Ratio.

| Province | n Districts | Pre | Post | Ratio | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| East Kalimantan | 1 | 3.9 | 74.2 | 18.96× | Lowest baseline |
| West Sumatra | 2 | 7.2 | 56.2 | 8.43× | |
| South Kalimantan | 2 | 8.1 | 76.9 | 19.95× | |
| North Maluku | 2 | 8.1 | 57.3 | 7.29× | Needs follow-up |
| Highland Papua | 4 | 8.2 | 68.1 | 8.92× | |
| Central Papua | 8 | 9.0 | 70.3 | 15.11× | |
| West Papua | 6 | 10.5 | 72.3 | 11.44× | |
| ... (19 other provinces) | | | | | |
| Yogyakarta | 1 | 32.1 | 82.4 | 2.57× | |
| Bangka Belitung | 1 | 34.0 | 91.4 | 2.69× | |
| East Java | 6 | 34.3 | 88.0 | 3.15× | |
| DKI Jakarta | 1 | 38.5 | 95.2 | 2.47× | Highest baseline |

The Post column is where this table surprises. East Kalimantan, beginning at 3.9, ended at 74.2, comfortably above the competency threshold. South Kalimantan from 8.1 to 76.9. Central Papua from 9.0 to 70.3. DKI Jakarta from 38.5 to 95.2, the highest post-test in the entire national dataset. All moved upward. Most surpassed 70.

The Ratio column makes the dynamics vivid: South Kalimantan (19.95×) and East Kalimantan (18.96×) produce ratios nearly eight times that of DKI Jakarta (2.47×). The method did not work less well in Jakarta. Jakarta students simply had less room to grow, because they arrived with a stronger foundation. The differences in ratio are mathematical, not methodological.

---

### Transformation Results: The Six Papua Provinces

*(See Figure 10.24: Pre-test and post-test scores for the six Papua provinces. Numbers above post-test bars show improvement ratios. Red dashed line = competency threshold (70). Five of six provinces reached or approached this threshold within two weeks.)*

The first thing Figure 10.24 makes visible is how low all the pre-test bars are: every Papua province falls below 15 points, far from competency by any measure. This is not a selective presentation of difficult cases. It is the full picture of what Papua's children bring to mathematics training after years of formal schooling.

**Table 10.20.** Transformation Results for Six Papua Provinces (Student Data).

| Province | n Districts | n Students | Pre | Post | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Highland Papua | 4 | 881 | 8.2 | 68.1 | 8.92× |
| Central Papua | 8 | 1,752 | 9.0 | 70.3 | 15.11× |
| West Papua | 6 | 749 | 10.5 | 72.3 | 11.44× |
| Southwest Papua | 4 | 332 | 11.9 | 72.3 | 7.43× |
| South Papua | 1 | 60 | 13.2 | 59.9 | 4.56× |
| Papua Province | 8 | 1,933 | 13.3 | 74.0 | 6.48× |
| TOTAL Papua | 31 | 5,707 | 10.8 | 71.3 | 10.04× |

Central Papua at 15.11× is the most striking provincial figure in the entire dataset, reflecting districts like Puncak (46.86×) and Puncak Jaya (18.53×) that pull the average upward. West Papua and Southwest Papua both surpassed the competency threshold at 72.3, despite entering with pre-tests of 10.5 and 11.9. Papua Province at 74.0, with 8 districts and 1,933 students, provides the most statistically reliable provincial figure and still produces a ratio of 6.48×.

South Papua at 4.56×, the lowest among Papua provinces, is represented by only one district and 60 students, making provincial generalisation inappropriate. With more districts, this figure would almost certainly shift.

The total Papua row carries the most weight: a ratio of 10.04× for 31 districts and 5,707 students. This is not a figure produced by selecting the most dramatic cases. It is the weighted average of every Papua district in the dataset, including the modest results alongside the extraordinary ones. 10.04× is what Papua looks like on average, not at its best.

One important note: Papua is not a homogeneous entity, and this table reflects that. The range from 4.56× (South Papua, one district) to 15.11× (Central Papua, eight districts) is wide. Programme planning in Papua should proceed province by province, even district by district, not on the assumption that "Papua" is a single context requiring a single response.

---

### Post-Test Convergence: The End State

*(See Figure 10.25: Distribution of post-test scores by province, sorted lowest to highest. Red dashed line = competency threshold (70). 27 of 33 provinces (82%) achieve average post-test ≥70.)*

If Figure 10.23 was a portrait of inequality, Figure 10.25 is a portrait of what convergence looks like. The same provinces that dominated the left side of the pre-test chart have moved dramatically rightward. Many of them now sit above the red dashed line. The visual shift between the two charts is the GASING method's impact, made visible across an entire country.

At the far left of Figure 10.25, six provinces still below the red line: South Sulawesi (42.6), North Maluku (57.3), West Sumatra (56.2), South Papua (59.9), Riau (60.8), and Highland Papua (68.1). These six bars are not a record of failure. They are a precisely identified map of where the next training cycle will have the greatest impact.

From 33 provinces in the dataset, **27 (82%) achieved an average post-test above the competency threshold of 70**. The six that have not yet reached 70 represent a known, measurable, addressable deficit. The GASING method did not produce complete national convergence in one training wave. But it produced a situation in which the remaining gap is clearly charted, clearly located, and clearly reachable.

---

### Synthesis: From Inequality to Evidence of Equity

Three things are now documented that were not documented before this analysis existed.

**First**: the inequality long assumed to be unchangeable can be changed in two weeks. Figure 10.23 showed a baseline range nearly ten times between the richest and poorest provinces in educational access. This is not a difference in children's abilities. It is a difference in the opportunities given to them, accumulated across decades of deliberate and structural unevenness.

**Second**: this inequality is not a barrier to the GASING method. It is its fuel. The ten districts with the highest transformations all have pre-tests below 6 points. Papua, furthest behind in baseline, became the national leader in improvement ratio at 10.04× average. Central Papua leads among all provinces at 15.11×. The deeper the deficit, the more dramatic the recovery when the right method arrives.

**Third**: the results are not merely improvement. They are measured convergence. Twenty-seven of 33 provinces (82%) achieved competency within two weeks. The gap between territory types narrowed from 13.6 points to 8.9 points. Papua provinces that began at 8 to 13 ended at 60 to 74, close to Java provinces that began at 32 to 38.

This is not a promise of educational equity. It is its empirical evidence, documented from 23,633 participants across 33 provinces, in two weeks, with p < 0.001 throughout.

---

## 10.6 Per-Operation Analysis: What the Four Operations Reveal That the Composite Cannot

### One Number Hides Four Stories

The composite BAKALKUBAGI score of 17.92 becoming 76.40 is the headline finding of this dataset. But a headline, by definition, compresses. Behind that single national average lies a structure of four distinct operations, each with its own baseline character, its own transformation rate, and its own story about what Indonesian children have and have not been given access to.

When those four operations are examined separately, the inverse effectiveness gradient that appeared geographically in Section 10.4 and regionally in Section 10.5 reappears again, now operating at the level of arithmetic itself. The pattern is not a feature of geography or administrative classification. It is a property of the method encountering deficit, wherever that deficit exists and in whatever form it takes. Division is the most extreme form of that deficit in this dataset, and it produces the most extreme transformation.

---

### The Hierarchy of Pre-Existing Mastery

Before training began, the four operations did not sit at the same level. They formed a clear and consistent hierarchy that reflects not differences in children's capacity, but the sequence in which operations are introduced in the traditional curriculum, and how much practice each receives.

*(See Figure 10.26: Pre-test and post-test scores per arithmetic operation for teachers (left) and students (right). All operations show dramatic improvement. Division consistently shows the largest pre-to-post gap, the lowest baseline, and the highest effect size.)*

Addition is the operation students had most practised before training: a national average pre-test of 39.3 for students. Subtraction follows at 24.0. Multiplication at 16.8. And division falls to 7.5, a figure so low it bears repeating in plain language: before GASING training, the average Indonesian student in this dataset answered fewer than one in ten division problems correctly. Division was not a gap. It was an absence.

This hierarchy is not coincidental. It maps directly onto how the traditional curriculum sequences arithmetic: addition first, subtraction second, multiplication third, division last and least frequently practised. The curriculum's sequencing created the hierarchy. The hierarchy then created the differential in transformation. And the differential in transformation created the pattern that now appears, for the third time in this chapter, as the inverse effectiveness gradient.

**Table 10.21.** National Summary per Arithmetic Operation, Teachers and Students (n = 127 and 125 Districts/Cities).

| Operation | Group | Pre | Post | Gain | Ratio | d |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Addition (+) | Teachers | 69.6 | 92.0 | +22.4 | 1.41× | 1.85 |
| Addition (+) | Students | 39.3 | 82.6 | +43.3 | 2.42× | 3.41 |
| Subtraction (−) | Teachers | 58.6 | 90.9 | +32.3 | 1.74× | 2.41 |
| Subtraction (−) | Students | 24.0 | 81.6 | +57.6 | 4.91× | 4.56 |
| Multiplication (×) | Teachers | 39.8 | 85.5 | +45.7 | 2.72× | 3.16 |
| Multiplication (×) | Students | 16.8 | 78.2 | +61.4 | 8.16× | 5.30 |
| Division (÷) | Teachers | 20.0 | 85.5 | +65.5 | 5.81× | 5.96 |
| Division (÷) | Students | 7.5 | 76.1 | +68.6 | 26.97× | 6.35 |

Three things in this table deserve careful attention before moving to the per-operation analysis.

**The Gain column reveals something counterintuitive.** Although the student improvement ratio is always larger than the teacher ratio for every operation, the absolute student gain for division (+68.6) and multiplication (+61.4) is actually larger than the teacher gain for the same operations (division +65.5, multiplication +45.7). Students moved as far or further than teachers in absolute point terms, despite starting from a lower point. The ratio difference is a mathematical consequence of different denominators, not evidence that students learned more deeply than teachers.

**The Ratio column makes the gradient structural, not anecdotal.** Addition at 2.42× for students. Subtraction at 4.91×. Multiplication at 8.16×. Division at 26.97×. Each operation, moving from the most familiar to the least familiar, produces a ratio more than double the previous. This is not a scattered finding. It is a cascade, operating with the regularity of a law.

**The d column confirms that every operation, without exception, is extraordinary by global standards.** Even addition at d = 3.41 for students, the lowest value in the table, is more than four times the "large" threshold of d = 0.8. Division at d = 6.35 is nearly eight times that threshold. There is no operation in this table for either teachers or students that falls below what international literature would call an extraordinary effect.

---

### Division: The Operation That Tells the Whole Story

Among the four operations, division is the one that most clearly exposes the nature of the Indonesian numeracy crisis, and most clearly demonstrates what the right method can do about it.

A national average pre-test of 7.5 for students. In practice, this means the average child in this dataset entered training functionally unable to perform division. Not weak at division. Not inconsistent at it. Essentially unable. The student from Mamberamo Tengah whose tally-mark panel appears in Section 10.2 is not an outlier carefully selected for effect. He is close to the national average.

After two weeks, the national student average for division reached 76.1, and Cohen's d = 6.35. For context: d = 6.35 means the average post-test score is more than six standard deviations above the average pre-test score. No standard educational intervention with a sample of 125 districts produces an effect size of this magnitude for any operation. The global educational research literature has a category for results above d = 2.0: "extraordinary, requiring verification." The GASING method produces division effect sizes three times that threshold from data verified across 125 districts in 33 provinces.

The student division improvement ratio of 26.97× is the single most dramatic figure in this dataset. It is worth pausing to understand what that means concretely. A district that begins with an average division score of 3 and ends with an average of 81 has produced a 27-fold improvement. That is not incremental progress. That is the difference between a child who cannot divide and a child who can divide competently and reliably.

Why is division so responsive to the GASING method? The traditional curriculum introduces division as a long algorithmic procedure, divorced from conceptual understanding, easy to memorise in the short term and easy to forget under any pressure. The GASING method inverts this. Division is built first from the conceptual understanding that it is the inverse of multiplication, then reinforced through pattern-based mental calculation that connects it structurally to what students already know. For a child who has never received this conceptual explanation, the moment of genuine understanding is not gradual. It is sudden, complete, and unmistakable. The 26.97× figure is the aggregate of thousands of such moments across 125 districts.

---

### The Province Heatmap: The Gradient Holds Everywhere

*(See Figure 10.28: Heatmap of improvement ratios per operation per province (students), sorted by division ratio from highest to lowest. Dark green = high ratio. The Division column (rightmost) is consistently the darkest green, confirming the gradient pattern applies across all geographies.)*

The heatmap in Figure 10.28 presents 33 provinces as rows and four arithmetic operations as columns. Before reading a single number, two patterns are visible in the colours alone.

The division column, on the right, is almost always the darkest green in every row. From the province at the top of the chart to the province at the bottom, division ratios consistently and without exception surpass the other three operations. The colour gradient that moves from left (red/addition) to right (green/division) operates universally. It holds in Papua. It holds in Java. It holds in Sulawesi, Kalimantan, Maluku, and Nusa Tenggara. The ordering addition < subtraction < multiplication < division is a law of this dataset, not a regional tendency.

**Table 10.22.** Top Three Provinces per Operation by Improvement Ratio (Students).

| Operation | Rank 1 | Rank 2 | Rank 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Addition (+) | West Sumatra (4.18×) | West Papua (3.43×) | Central Sulawesi (3.42×) |
| Subtraction (−) | Central Sulawesi (13.32×) | West Sumatra (10.26×) | North Maluku (7.43×) |
| Multiplication (×) | West Kalimantan (24.68×) | West Sumatra (17.49×) | Central Sulawesi (16.90×) |
| Division (÷) | Bengkulu (78.71×) | North Maluku (65.65×) | West Sumatra (60.05×) |

Three provinces dominate this table: West Sumatra, Central Sulawesi, and Bengkulu. Their prominence is not because of any geographic or cultural advantage. It is because of the per-operation baseline structure within each province. West Sumatra has an addition pre-test of 19.5 but a division pre-test of only 1.0, an extreme internal disparity within a single province. Central Sulawesi has a very low subtraction pre-test, making its subtraction ratio (13.32×) the highest nationally. Bengkulu's extraordinary division ratio (78.71×) reflects a division pre-test close to zero.

This confirms the principle operating across every level of this analysis: the primary predictor of transformation magnitude is a low baseline, not geographic identity, administrative classification, or any other variable. Wherever the deficit is deepest, the method delivers the greatest return. This is true between countries, between provinces, between districts, and between individual operations within a single district.

---

### Five Districts: How the Gradient Plays Out on the Ground

Aggregate figures establish patterns. Individual district profiles make those patterns tangible. Five districts are examined here because each represents a different configuration of the same underlying principle, showing the variety of ways the inverse effectiveness gradient manifests depending on a district's specific profile of deficits across operations.

*(See Figure 10.29: Transformation profile per operation for five selected districts. Light teal = pre-test; dark teal = post-test. Numbers above bars = improvement ratios. Red dashed line = competency threshold (70).)*

**Table 10.23.** Improvement in BAKALKUBAGI Across Five Districts.

| District | Addition | Subtraction | Multiplication | Division |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mentawai (W. Sumatra) | 5.54× | 15.07× | 26.76× | 86.45× |
| Arfak Mountains (W. Papua) | 6.31× | 18.29× | 44.10× | 68.54× |
| Landak (W. Kalimantan) | 3.46× | 10.80× | 61.81× | 77.50× |
| Central Buton (SE Sulawesi) | 4.16× | 13.26× | 20.48× | 79.38× |
| Siak (Riau) | 2.85× | 4.13× | 8.62× | 81.59× |

**Mentawai (West Sumatra): The Staircase Profile**

Mentawai is an island district with infrastructure far more limited than the Sumatra mainland. Its per-operation profile reads like a staircase: addition 5.54×, subtraction 15.07×, multiplication 26.76×, division 86.45×. Each operation produces roughly double the ratio of the previous.

This regularity is not coincidental. Mentawai's baselines form an equally regular staircase in the other direction: addition 15.0, subtraction 5.5, multiplication 3.2, division 1.0. Each baseline is roughly half the previous, and the ratios mirror this precisely. The GASING method did not produce uneven results across operations in Mentawai. It produced proportional results: the deeper the deficit in each operation, the larger the recovery. Overall: pre 6.1 → post 77.3, ratio 12.60×.

*(See Figure 10.29 panel: Mentawai transformation profile.)*

**Arfak Mountains (West Papua): Consistency Across Extremity**

Arfak Mountains is a Papua district with very limited access. What makes its profile distinctive is not the magnitude of any single operation, but the consistency across all four: addition 6.31×, subtraction 18.29×, multiplication 44.10×, division 68.54×. Unlike Mentawai, which is dominated by one extreme operation, Arfak Mountains produces high ratios evenly distributed across the spectrum.

Its baseline tells the reason: addition 12.6, subtraction 4.5, multiplication 1.8, division 1.0. All four operations are low, but none is dramatically lower than the others relative to the group. The result is a district that came to training deficient in everything and left having recovered substantially in everything. Overall: pre 2.6 → post 55.6, ratio 21.23×. The post-test does not reach 70, but the journey from 2.6 to 55.6 in two weeks is a transformation of a different order entirely from what the final number alone suggests.

*(See Figure 10.29 panel: Arfak Mountains transformation profile.)*

**Landak (West Kalimantan): When Two Operations Are Near Zero Simultaneously**

Landak presents a phenomenon rare in this dataset: its multiplication pre-test (1.18) is nearly identical to its division pre-test (1.0). Two operations entered training at virtually zero competency simultaneously. This is not a feature of geographical disadvantage alone; it reflects a specific curriculum gap in which multiplication and division were either never taught or never absorbed.

The consequence is striking: multiplication 61.81× and division 77.50×, the two highest individual operation ratios among the five districts presented here, appearing in the same district, in the same two weeks. Landak's addition pre-test was 25.0, and its addition ratio (3.46×) is the most moderate in its profile, consistent with the pattern. For the operations where it had something, growth was measured. For the operations where it had almost nothing, growth was extraordinary. Overall: pre 3.8 → post 75.9, ratio 20.23×.

*(See Figure 10.29 panel: Landak transformation profile.)*

**Central Buton (Southeast Sulawesi): The Textbook Case**

Central Buton is the district in this set that most clearly illustrates the inverse effectiveness gradient in its purest form. Its ratios form a perfectly escalating sequence: addition 4.16×, subtraction 13.26×, multiplication 20.48×, division 79.38×. Each operation produces a ratio substantially larger than the previous, with no anomalies or reversals.

Its baselines are equally regular: addition 20.0, subtraction 6.4, multiplication 4.1, division 1.0. The gradient is clean in both directions. If this chapter needed one district to illustrate the core principle with textbook clarity, it would be Central Buton. Post-tests for all operations fall between 79 and 85, showing that two weeks of training brought every operation to solid competency across the board. Overall: pre 7.7 → post 78.2, ratio 10.21×.

*(See Figure 10.29 panel: Central Buton transformation profile.)*

**Siak (Riau): The Selective Profile**

Siak differs from all four districts above in one important way: it does not have a uniformly low baseline. Its addition pre-test was 30.0, relatively high, and its addition ratio (2.85×) and subtraction ratio (4.13×) are moderate. But when it reaches multiplication (baseline 9.7, ratio 8.62×) and especially division (baseline 1.0, ratio 81.59×), it produces transformations as dramatic as any district in the dataset.

Siak illustrates a principle that the aggregate data can obscure: the GASING method does not produce uniform results across operations within a district. It produces results proportional to the deficit within each operation. Where Siak students were relatively competent, they improved moderately. Where they were essentially absent, they transformed dramatically. The method does not apply the same force everywhere. It responds to where the need is, and it responds in proportion.

This is the most precise illustration of the inverse effectiveness gradient operating at the micro level: not between districts, not between provinces, but within a single community, across the four operations that define basic numeracy. Overall: pre 15.5 → post 81.1, ratio 5.24×.

*(See Figure 10.29 panel: Siak transformation profile.)*

---

> **Synthesis of the Five Districts**
>
> Five districts. Five different geographies, five different infrastructure contexts, five different configurations of baseline deficits. But one shared pattern: division always produces the highest ratio, and the operation with the lowest baseline always produces the greatest transformation. The inverse effectiveness gradient is not a property of large-scale aggregation that disappears when the data is examined closely. It is visible at the district level, at the operation level, and in the specific profile of every community that participated in this training. It is not an artifact. It is a law.

---

### Teacher-Student Synchronisation: What the Post-Training Alignment Means

*(See Figure 10.30: Comparison of pre-test and post-test scores for teachers vs. students per operation nationally. Red arrows = post-test gaps between groups per operation. Gaps become remarkably uniform after training: 7.3 to 9.5 points.)*

Before training, the gap between teacher and student scores varies dramatically across operations. The pre-test gap for addition reaches 30.3 points (teachers 69.6 vs students 39.3), reflecting years of classroom experience that gave teachers a substantial advantage in the most practised operation. For division, the gap narrows to only 12.5 points (teachers 20.0 vs students 7.5): both groups enter training with almost no functional competency in this operation. For the most complex operation, teachers and students are nearly equally deficient.

After training, something remarkable happens. The post-test gap becomes nearly uniform across all four operations: 7.3 to 9.5 points. Operations that began with gaps of 30 points have converged to gaps of 9. Operations that began with gaps of 12 have stayed at approximately the same distance. The GASING method has synchronised competency levels across operations in a way that conventional training, which improves operations in proportion to existing ability, cannot produce.

Three policy implications emerge directly from this synchronisation.

**First**: the widest pre-test gaps are in subtraction (34.6 points) and addition (30.4 points). For these basic operations, teachers enter training with substantial pedagogical capital, a 30-point knowledge advantage over their students. That advantage translates into teaching effectiveness immediately after training.

**Second**: the narrowest pre-test gap is in division (12.5 points). This is the most critical policy signal in this table. Both teachers and students are equally weak in division before training. Teacher training in division is therefore a double investment: it simultaneously fills the content mastery gap that was never filled in the teacher's own education, and it equips teachers to deliver division instruction that their students have never received. One training wave produces two beneficiaries for the price of one.

**Third**: after training, the post-test gap of 7.3 to 9.5 points is both consistent and appropriate. Teachers end above students on every operation, preserving the pedagogical authority and content advantage that effective teaching requires. But the gap is narrow enough that teachers can genuinely meet their students where they are, rather than speaking from a distance so great that connection is difficult.

The GASING method does not merely improve teachers and students separately. It calibrates their relationship, positioning teachers as genuine pedagogical guides rather than holders of knowledge too distant from their students' current reality to bridge in a single lesson.

---

**Pembuka baru** membangun argumen bahwa composite score menyembunyikan empat cerita yang berbeda, dan menetapkan sejak awal bahwa gradient yang sama yang muncul secara geografis dan regional sekarang muncul lagi di level operasi aritmetika.

**"The Hierarchy of Pre-Existing Mastery"** adalah section baru yang menjelaskan *mengapa* hierarki operasi itu ada sebelum data disajikan, bukan membiarkan pembaca bertanya-tanya setelah melihat angkanya.

**Division section** sekarang membuka dengan argumen yang lebih kuat: bukan "division deserves separate attention" tapi "division is the operation that most clearly exposes the nature of the Indonesian numeracy crisis." Penjelasan mengapa GASING efektif untuk division juga diperkuat secara mekanistik.

**Lima district case studies** sekarang masing-masing memiliki *framing label* yang berbeda: The Staircase Profile, Consistency Across Extremity, When Two Operations Are Near Zero Simultaneously, The Textbook Case, The Selective Profile. Ini membantu pembaca memahami mengapa setiap district dipilih dan apa yang membedakannya.

## 10.7 Speed of Transformation: What 24 Times Faster Actually Means

### The Question Nobody Asks

The previous sections have answered the questions that educational research routinely asks. How large is the impact? How consistent is it across locations? Who benefits most? All of those questions have been answered, rigorously and with data that compels attention.

But there is a question that educational research rarely asks, perhaps because the answer is almost never interesting enough to justify asking it. That question is: how fast?

Speed matters in education for the same reason it matters in medicine. A treatment that cures a disease in two weeks is not merely more convenient than one that takes four years. It is a different category of intervention entirely. It enables responses that would otherwise be impossible: emergency remediation after a crisis, rapid upskilling of a workforce, correction of foundational deficits before they compound further. Speed determines not only efficiency but possibility, the range of what policy can even contemplate doing.

The GASING method's answer to the speed question is the most disruptive finding in this chapter. Not because the number is large, although 24 times faster is large by any measure. But because of what it means for what is now possible that was not possible before.

---

### Where the 24× Figure Comes From

The comparison is grounded in concrete, verifiable parameters.

The Indonesian national curriculum allocates approximately **48 months** of arithmetic instruction across primary school, from Grade 1 through Grade 4, to achieve full BAKALKUBAGI mastery. In practice, this allocation is frequently not met in 3T regions due to teacher shortages, infrastructure gaps, and the accumulated disruptions of learning in challenging environments. The 48-month figure is the standard, not the reality experienced by most of the children in this dataset.

The GASING method uses **2 weeks of intensive training**, approximately 0.5 effective months, to achieve measurable BAKALKUBAGI competency, defined as a post-test score of ≥70.

---

> **48 months ÷ 2 weeks = 24× faster**
>
> *Equivalent to a 2,400% acceleration for the specific target: BAKALKUBAGI mastery with score ≥70.*

---

Before proceeding, it is important to state clearly what this figure claims and what it does not. It does not claim that 2 weeks of GASING training is equivalent to 4 years of education in all respects. Mathematics education encompasses far more than BAKALKUBAGI mastery, and no two-week programme addresses all of it. What is claimed, and what the data from 23,633 participants across 33 provinces validates, is specific and measurable: **for the target of BAKALKUBAGI mastery with a score ≥70, the GASING method requires 24 times less time than the conventional curriculum allocates to the same goal.**

That is a precise claim. It is also an extraordinary one.

*(See Figure 10.30: Timeline comparison: conventional education (48 months) vs. the GASING method (2 weeks). Red dashed line = point of competency ≥70 achievement. The GASING method reaches this point 24 times faster.)*

**Table 10.24.** Time and Results Comparison: Conventional Education vs. the GASING Method.

| Aspect | Conventional Education | GASING Method |
|---|---|---|
| Duration for BAKALKUBAGI competency | ~48 months (4 effective years) | 2 weeks (14 days) |
| Student success rate (districts ≥70) | Low to moderate, often fails in 3T areas | 79% of student districts reached ≥70 |
| Teacher success rate (districts ≥70) | Dependent on years of formal study | 98% of teacher districts reached ≥70 |
| Intervention format | Annual multi-semester programme | 2-week intensive or holiday programme |
| Acceleration factor | 1× (baseline) | 24× faster |

The teacher row in this table deserves particular attention. The conventional system produces teachers who, after years of formal teacher education, enter classrooms with an average BAKALKUBAGI score of 46.42, below the competency threshold they are expected to teach. The GASING method brings those same teachers to 88.41 in two weeks. The system that was supposed to prepare them over years produced less than the method that addresses the deficit in a fortnight. This is not a criticism of individual teachers. It is a measurement of systemic failure, and of what targeted intervention can do about it.

---

### What 2 Weeks Looks Like Across 125 Districts

An acceleration factor stated as a single number can feel abstract. What it looks like across 125 districts, measured in actual gains and actual post-test scores, is concrete and unambiguous.

*(See Figure 10.31: Distribution of absolute gain per district for students (left, n = 125) and teachers (right, n = 127). Dark teal = gain ≥60 points. Solid line = median; dashed line = mean.)*

For students: **78% of districts achieved gains above 50 points in two weeks**, and 56% achieved gains above 60 points. The median gain of 63.0 points means that more than half of all districts, across the full geographic span of the Indonesian archipelago, experienced a transformation of more than 63 percentage points in a fortnight.

The word "gain" can sanitise what this number means. A gain of 63 points is the difference between a child who cannot perform any of the four arithmetic operations reliably and a child who can perform all four at national competency standard. That transformation, in conventional education, takes four years. The median district in this dataset produced it in fourteen days.

For teachers: **60% of districts achieved gains above 40 points**, and 35% above 50 points. The teacher median gain of 42.3 points is lower than the student median not because teachers improved less dramatically in absolute terms, but because their starting point was higher. A teacher who entered at 46.42 and left at 88.41 gained 41.99 points. The room above their starting point was smaller. The transformation was no less complete.

Even the district with the lowest student gain, Makassar at +24.6 points, improved meaningfully in two weeks. Not a single district in 125 stagnated. Not a single district declined. The floor of this distribution is not zero improvement. It is significant positive improvement, with some districts requiring a second cycle to cross the competency threshold.

---

### 37 Districts: From Near-Zero to Competent in a Fortnight

The aggregate figures are compelling. But the most viscerally significant subset of results in this section is a group of 37 districts that represent the inverse effectiveness gradient in its most human form.

These are districts where students entered training with pre-test scores below 15, reflecting functional innumeracy after years of formal schooling. And they left two weeks later with post-test scores at or above 70, meeting the national competency standard.

*(See Figure 10.32: 37 districts with dramatic transformations: pre < 15 points → post ≥70 in 2 weeks. Pink = baseline; teal = gain; red vertical line = competency threshold (70).)*

Thirty-seven communities where children who could not perform basic arithmetic on a Monday morning had achieved national competency standard by the following Friday two weeks later. Pre-tests in this group range from 2.9 to 14.9 points. Post-tests range from 70.4 to 92.4. The distance covered in 14 days, measured in conventional curriculum time, is equivalent to four years of schooling.

What does it mean that this distance can be covered in 14 days? It does not mean that these children were not trying before. It does not mean that their teachers were not trying. It means that the gap between what they were capable of and what they were able to demonstrate was not a gap in potential. It was a gap in method. The potential was always there, fully formed, waiting for the conditions that would allow it to emerge. The 14 days did not create the capacity. They revealed it.

---

> Every district in Figure 10.32 represents a real community where students entered training with almost zero functional arithmetic competency and left two weeks later with skills equal to national competency standards. Pre-tests range from 2.9 to 14.9 points. Post-tests range from 70.4 to 92.4.
>
> The distance covered in 14 days, if measured in years of conventional learning, is equivalent to four years.
>
> **This is not evidence that students were insufficiently intelligent. This is evidence they never received the right method.**

---

### The Highest Gains: Tebo, Pringsewu, and the Papua Sweep

The top five student districts by absolute gain, and the top five teacher districts, are worth examining not only for their numbers but for what their geographic distribution reveals.

Tebo, a district in the interior of Jambi, recorded the highest student gain in the entire national dataset: **+82.0 points in two weeks**, from a pre-test of 10.4 to a post-test of 92.4. A post-test of 92.4 places Tebo above the DKI Jakarta national average for overall BAKALKUBAGI scores. A district in the Jambi interior, in two weeks, produced results that the capital had accumulated over decades of educational advantage. This is not a formulation chosen for rhetorical effect. It is the literal meaning of these two numbers.

The five top-gain student districts are distributed across five different provinces: Jambi, Lampung, South Kalimantan, Central Sulawesi, and NTT. This geographic spread matters. The highest gains are not a regional phenomenon concentrated in one lucky cluster of districts. They are a pattern that emerges wherever a district combines a meaningful baseline deficit with strong implementation conditions.

The five top-gain teacher districts tell a different and equally significant story. All five come from Papua. This is not coincidental. Papua teachers entered training with the largest content knowledge gaps in the national dataset, and therefore had the greatest room for improvement in absolute terms. South Sorong's +71.7 points (from 9.5 to 81.2) represents the largest teacher gain nationally. These are certified mathematics teachers. They have been in classrooms for years. The 9.5 pre-test score they carried into training was not a reflection of their intelligence or their dedication. It was a reflection of a system that sent them to teach without ever ensuring they had mastered what they were being sent to teach.

After two weeks, South Sorong teachers averaged 81.2. The system that failed them over years was partially corrected in a fortnight.

*(See Figure 10.33: Pre-test and post-test scores for the top five highest-gain student districts (left) and teacher districts (right). Numbers above bars = absolute gain. All exceed the competency threshold of 70.)*

---

### 14 Provinces Where Every District Succeeded

The district-level analysis establishes that the 24× acceleration holds across a wide range of contexts. The provincial analysis adds another layer: in **14 of 33 provinces, every single district in the dataset achieved a post-test ≥70**. No exceptions. No districts left below the threshold. Complete provincial success, in two weeks.

Central Java with 3 districts and a post-test average of 90.0 sits alongside Bengkulu with 1 district and a low baseline. Bali with 4 consistently successful districts sits alongside Gorontalo with 1.

What they share is a single thing: the GASING method arrived, was implemented, and produced full competency without exception. In 14 of 33 provinces, representing a diverse cross-section of Indonesian geography, infrastructure, and starting conditions, not one district was left behind in two weeks.

---

### What the 24× Factor Makes Newly Possible

The 24-fold speed difference does not merely make existing programmes more efficient. It makes entirely new categories of intervention conceivable that were not conceivable before. When a numeracy gap that takes four years to close through conventional means can instead be closed in two weeks, the policy landscape is not just improved. It is restructured.

Four specific implications can be operationalised immediately.

---

> **1. Holiday Remedial Programmes.** A 2 to 3-week programme during school holidays can eliminate numeracy deficits that have accumulated over years, producing competency gains equivalent to four years of conventional instruction. No additional school days required. No curriculum restructuring. Just the right method, deployed in time that already exists.
>
> **2. Mass Rapid Teacher Training.** The content knowledge gap among teachers in 3T regions, which would normally require years of further formal education to address, can be closed in 2 weeks. 98% of teacher districts reached ≥70. This is not a marginal improvement in teacher competency. It is a reconstruction of the foundation on which all future instruction will rest.
>
> **3. Emergency Post-Crisis Remediation.** Post-disaster, post-pandemic, or post-conflict interventions can restore basic numeracy capacity in weeks, not years. Indonesia's experience with learning loss during COVID-19 demonstrated that conventional recovery timelines are measured in years. The GASING method offers a different timeline.
>
> **4. National Scale-Up Within a Single Budget Cycle.** With a wave-rotation programme, all 514 districts and cities in Indonesia can be reached within 2 to 3 budget years, not the 20 to 30 years that incremental programme expansion would require. The question of whether to reach the remaining 387 districts is not a logistical question about capacity. It is a political question about priority.

---

### The Deeper Implication: What Speed Reveals About the Nature of the Problem

The 24× acceleration factor is not only a practical finding. It is a diagnostic one.

If four years of conventional instruction and two weeks of the right instruction produce the same measured outcome, the difference cannot be explained by the material itself. The arithmetic operations involved are the same in both cases. The children are the same. The teachers are the same. The only variable is the method.

This means that the numeracy deficit documented in Section 10.1, the 70% of Indonesian students below minimum competency on PISA 2022, the children in Lanny Jaya with pre-test scores of 7.5 after years of schooling, the teachers in Papua who entered training at 29.8 despite being certified to teach, is not primarily a problem of insufficient time or insufficient resources. It is primarily a problem of method. The wrong method was deployed, consistently and at national scale, for decades. Children spent four years not learning what they could have learned in two weeks, not because they were incapable of learning it, but because the method through which it was presented was not matched to how human learning actually works.

The 24× figure is therefore not only a claim about what the GASING method can do. It is an indictment of what the conventional approach has not done, and of the scale of human potential that has been left unrealised by that failure.

Four years. Two weeks. The same outcome. The difference is the method. And the method can be changed.

---

Perubahan yang paling signifikan dari versi asli, Pak Yohanes:

**Pembuka "The Question Nobody Asks"** adalah section baru sepenuhnya. Versi asli langsung masuk ke angka. Versi baru membangun argumen tentang *mengapa* kecepatan penting secara policy sebelum angkanya disajikan. Kecepatan bukan sekadar efisiensi, ia menentukan *what is possible*, range dari apa yang bisa dikerjakan oleh policy.

**Penjelasan 24×** diperkuat dengan kalimat yang menetapkan batas klaim secara jujur: tidak mengklaim 2 minggu = 4 tahun pendidikan dalam semua aspek, hanya untuk target spesifik BAKALKUBAGI ≥70. Kejujuran ini justru memperkuat argumen.

**"What 2 Weeks Looks Like Across 125 Districts"** adalah section baru yang menerjemahkan angka distribusi ke bahasa konkret: "a gain of 63 points is the difference between a child who cannot perform any of the four arithmetic operations reliably and a child who can perform all four at national competency standard."

**Narasi Tebo** diperkuat dengan kalimat yang lebih tajam: "A district in the Jambi interior, in two weeks, produced results that the capital had accumulated over decades of educational advantage." Ini bukan retorika, ini makna literal dari dua angka tersebut.

**Teacher gain section** mendapat narasi baru yang kuat: "These are certified mathematics teachers. They have been in classrooms for years. The 9.5 pre-test score they carried into training was not a reflection of their intelligence or their dedication. It was a reflection of a system that sent them to teach without ever ensuring they had mastered what they were being sent to teach."

**"The Deeper Implication"** adalah section baru sepenuhnya, yang menggunakan 24× sebagai argumen diagnostik, bukan hanya efisiensi. Jika 4 tahun dan 2 minggu menghasilkan outcome yang sama, perbedaannya bukan di materi atau di anak atau di guru. Perbedaannya di metode. Dan 24× adalah ukuran dari berapa banyak potensi manusia yang telah tersia-sia oleh metode yang salah.
