## 10.8 Additional Findings: Eight Advanced Analyses

### When the Main Story Has Been Told, the Deeper Story Begins

Sections 10.2 through 10.7 have answered the primary questions this dataset was built to answer. The effect size is extraordinary. The improvement is consistent across 125 locations. The inverse effectiveness gradient is statistically verified. Papua leads nationally. The speed of transformation is 24 times faster than conventional instruction.

But a dataset of 23,633 participants across 33 provinces does not exhaust its meaning in six findings. When the main story has been told, the data still has things to say, and some of the most policy-relevant information lives not in the headline findings but in the patterns that only emerge when the data is questioned from different angles, at different levels of granularity, and with different purposes in mind.

This section presents eight such analyses. They are independent of each other but mutually reinforcing, each adding a layer of understanding that the aggregate figures alone cannot provide. Some confirm what the main findings already suggested. Some complicate them. One reveals a paradox. Together, they produce a picture of the GASING method's impact that is richer, more honest, and more practically useful than any single summary statistic could be.

---

### Analysis 1: The Teacher Multiplier Effect

#### A 2-Week Investment With a 15-Year Return

The most immediately visible impact of the GASING method is what happens to the 23,633 participants during training. But training is not a terminal event. Teachers carry what they learn into classrooms that will remain active for the rest of their careers, reaching students who were not present in the training room and who will never know that what they are receiving began in a two-week programme.

The multiplier effect of teacher training is the mechanism by which a short-duration intervention produces long-duration impact at national scale. Quantifying it honestly requires conservative assumptions, because the purpose is not to produce an impressive number but to understand the realistic lower bound of what the investment is worth.

With a conservative assumption that one trained teacher instructs 30 students per year and has an average of 15 remaining career years, each trained teacher reaches 450 students throughout their career. For the 7,983 teachers already trained, the projection is as follows:

**Table 10.28.** Projected Long-Term Impact: Teacher Multiplier Effect.

| Time Horizon | Assumption | Students Reached |
|---|---|---|
| Immediate (after training) | 15,650 students directly trained | 15,650 |
| 1 year ahead | 7,983 teachers × 30 students/year | 239,490 |
| 5 years ahead | 7,983 × 30 × 5 | 1,197,450 |
| 15 years (full career) | 7,983 × 30 × 15 | 3,592,350 |

A 450:1 leverage ratio. For every hour invested in a teacher's training, 450 hours of improved instruction will eventually be delivered to students who were not in the room. Even at 50% transfer efficiency, a conservative assumption that accounts for partial implementation, partial retention, and the normal attrition of any pedagogical innovation over time, the projection remains above 1.8 million students from one training wave alone.

This multiplier transforms how the cost-effectiveness calculation should be structured. The question is not "what does it cost to train 7,983 teachers?" The question is "what is the per-student cost of reaching 3.5 million students through those 7,983 teachers?" The answer to the second question is a fraction of what any direct student intervention at that scale would cost, and it produces results that persist and compound over a decade and a half rather than expiring when the programme ends.

There is a further dimension to this multiplier that the numbers alone cannot capture. A teacher who has genuinely reconstructed their own understanding of arithmetic, who now knows not just the procedure but the logic behind it, does not simply deliver better instruction. They deliver *different* instruction. They answer "why does this work?" because they now understand why. They notice when a student is confused at the conceptual level rather than just the procedural level. They make connections between operations that they previously experienced as separate. The quality change in what they teach is not proportional to the score change in what they know. It is, in ways that are difficult to measure but easy to observe, multiplicative.

---

### Analysis 2: District Typology

#### Three Mastery Profiles, One Policy Map

Aggregate post-test scores answer whether a district succeeded overall. But overall success conceals a more granular question: which operations did students master, and which operations still require work? When each district is classified based on whether it reached ≥70 in each individual operation, three distinct profiles emerge that have direct implications for what the next intervention cycle should prioritise.

**Table 10.29.** District Typology Based on Post-Test Mastery Profile (Students).

| Type | Definition | n Districts | Percentage | Key Characteristics |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | All 4 operations ≥70 | 94 | 75% | Complete transformation. Every operation surpasses the competency threshold. |
| B | Addition ≥70, Division < 70 | 17 | 14% | Partial transformation. Basic operations mastered; complex operations approaching threshold. |
| C | Even addition < 70 | 12 | 10% | Incomplete transformation. Very low baseline; two weeks was not the full journey. |

Three quarters of all districts achieved Type A: complete competency across all four operations in two weeks. This is the headline of the typology analysis. But the value of this classification is not in the headline. It is in what it reveals about the remaining 25%.

Type B districts are the most actionable finding. Seventeen districts where students can now reliably add and subtract but have not yet crossed the competency threshold for division. This is not a failure of the method. It is a precise diagnostic: these students need targeted division instruction, not a full repeat of the programme. A focused follow-up cycle of perhaps one week, designed specifically for the gap that Type B identifies, would almost certainly bring these 17 districts to Type A. The classification does not merely describe where they are. It specifies exactly what they need.

Type C districts are the most important for equity policy. Twelve districts where even addition has not yet crossed 70, reflecting starting conditions so low that two weeks was genuinely insufficient for the full journey from near-zero to competency. Their average pre-test is 13.1, their average post-test is 57.9. They have moved, substantially. They have not arrived. The solution is not to redesign the method. The solution is to extend the duration, or to run a second cycle. Type C districts are not programme failures. They are programme opportunities: the places where a second investment will produce the most dramatic returns, because the potential has already been partially unlocked and only needs further activation.

Together, the three types constitute a roadmap for the next phase of national implementation. Type A districts: sustain and deepen. Type B districts: targeted division follow-up. Type C districts: second full cycle with extended duration. The data does not only tell us what happened. It tells us exactly what to do next, for each of the 125 districts individually.

---

### Analysis 3: The Strongest Predictor

#### Division Alone Outperforms the Full Test

Among the questions most relevant to programme planners preparing to implement GASING in new districts, one of the most practical is: if I want to predict how much impact this training will have before it begins, what should I measure?

The intuitive answer is: measure the composite BAKALKUBAGI score. The more you know about overall numeracy at the outset, the better your prediction of overall improvement. This intuition is reasonable, but the data contradicts it.

The division pre-test alone, as a predictor of overall post-test outcomes, yields a Spearman correlation of ρ = 0.760. The composite BAKALKUBAGI pre-test, which incorporates all four operations, yields only ρ = 0.544. A single four-problem division test outperforms a comprehensive multi-operation assessment as a predictor of GASING training outcomes.

Why? Two mechanisms reinforce each other. First, the division pre-test is the most sensitive proxy for the depth of a district's overall mathematics education gap. A district where students cannot divide is almost certainly a district where students also struggle with multiplication, have gaps in subtraction, and learned addition by rote without conceptual understanding. Division does not exist in isolation: it is the terminus of a conceptual chain. A near-zero division score is the most reliable single indicator that the foundational chain is broken somewhere, not just at the final link. The division score maps the depth of the deficit with a precision that the composite score, which averages across operations of very different sensitivities, cannot match.

Second, the GASING method is most transformative precisely where division is weakest, because division produces the highest effect sizes (d = 6.35 for students nationally) and the most dramatic ratios (26.97× national average). A district with a near-zero division pre-test is positioned exactly where the method delivers its maximum return. The division pre-test identifies the districts where the method will be most powerful, which is why it predicts outcomes better than the composite.

The practical implication is immediate and significant. **To assess the likely impact of GASING implementation in a new district, measuring student division competency alone provides a more accurate prediction than a full BAKALKUBAGI assessment.** A programme planner can conduct a brief five-problem division test, rather than a comprehensive 40-problem arithmetic battery, and obtain a more reliable estimate of expected training outcomes. This simplifies the needs assessment process, reduces the time and cost of pre-implementation evaluation, and concentrates diagnostic attention on the single variable that carries the most predictive weight.

---

### Analysis 4: Teacher-Student Synchronisation

#### Why Training Both Together Is Not Optional

A foundational assumption in education policy holds that better-prepared teachers produce better-learning students: that investment in teacher competency translates, with reasonable efficiency, into improved student outcomes. This assumption is correct in this dataset, but weaker than most policy models would predict.

The correlation between teacher and student improvement ratios per district is **r = 0.376**, statistically significant (p < 0.001) but explaining only approximately 14% of the variation between districts. Districts where teachers improved dramatically do not reliably produce students who also improved dramatically, and vice versa. The relationship exists, but it is far from deterministic.

This is not a methodological anomaly. It reflects a real phenomenon: direct student training and teacher training each produce impacts that are substantially independent of each other. A teacher who has reconstructed their own understanding of arithmetic does not automatically transmit that reconstruction to students during the training period itself. The transfer from improved teacher to improved student is a process that unfolds over months and years of ongoing instruction, not in the two weeks during which both groups are being trained simultaneously.

What this means for programme design is direct and important. **Investing in teacher training alone, without simultaneous or immediately consecutive student training, foregoes a substantial portion of the total potential impact.** Teachers become more capable, but students do not yet benefit from that increased capability within the training window. Conversely, investing in student training alone produces improvements that are real and measurable, but foregoes the long-duration multiplier that only teacher training provides.

The optimal programme design is not a choice between training teachers and training students. It is both, sequenced to maximise the short-term impact on students and the long-term impact through teachers. The data is unambiguous on this point: each intervention produces value that the other does not, and the combination produces something greater than either alone.

---

### Analysis 5: Residual Anomalies

#### Who Overperforms, Who Underperforms, and What That Means

Linear regression predicts improvement ratios from baseline scores with reasonable accuracy (R² = 0.384), but with substantial residual variation. Several districts consistently fall far above or below their predicted improvement, and identifying them is more than an exercise in statistical tidiness. It opens productive questions about what drives the difference between a district that performs as expected and one that does not.

**Table 10.30.** Overperforming and Underperforming Districts vs. Regression Prediction.

| District | Province | Pre | Actual | Predicted | Residual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Puncak | Central Papua | 1.0 | 46.86× | 12.57× | +34.3 |
| Kotabaru | South Kalimantan | 1.9 | 33.69× | 12.24× | +21.4 |
| West Seram | Maluku | 2.9 | 24.74× | 11.91× | +12.8 |
| Dogiyai | Central Papua | 3.2 | 21.39× | 11.80× | +9.6 |
| Arfak Mountains | West Papua | 2.6 | 21.23× | 12.00× | +9.2 |
| Payakumbuh | West Sumatra | 8.2 | 4.26× | 10.03× | −5.8 |
| Central Halmahera | North Maluku | 9.0 | 5.37× | 9.76× | −4.4 |
| Rokan Hilir | Riau | 15.2 | 3.18× | 7.57× | −4.4 |
| Makassar | South Sulawesi | 17.9 | 2.37× | 6.61× | −4.2 |
| Kampar | Riau | 17.5 | 2.57× | 6.76× | −4.2 |

The five overperformers share a pattern that the baseline score alone cannot explain. Puncak's residual of +34.3 means its actual ratio was more than three times what the regression predicted. Kotabaru at +21.4 is nearly three times its prediction. These are not modest positive deviations. They are transformations that the statistical model, having no information beyond the baseline score, had no way to anticipate.

What the model cannot measure but the field can observe is the quality of implementation: the energy and commitment of the training team, the motivation of participants, the local leadership that created conditions for learning before the trainers arrived, the community context that made the training feel meaningful rather than merely obligatory. Puncak is not simply a district with a pre-test of 1.0. It is a community where something beyond the minimum was present. The quantitative data provides the signal. The qualitative investigation, which Chapter 11 begins to conduct, provides the explanation.

The five underperformers are more concerning, and they point in a different direction. Payakumbuh's residual of −5.8 means it achieved only 4.26× against a prediction of 10.03×. This is not a district that performed poorly in absolute terms: a ratio of 4.26× would be remarkable in any other context. But against what the data predicted it should achieve, given its baseline, it underperformed significantly. Riau as a province contains two underperformers in this table, Rokan Hilir and Kampar, and Riau also appears as one of the two provinces requiring attention in the effect size analysis. This convergence of signals across multiple analyses is too consistent to be coincidental.

What it suggests is not methodological failure but implementation inconsistency. Something in the way the programme was delivered in these locations did not replicate the conditions that produced strong results elsewhere. Identifying precisely what that something is, through structured qualitative follow-up, is the most direct path to closing the gap between what these districts achieved and what the data says they were capable of achieving.

---

### Analysis 6: The Convergence Paradox

#### When the Same Intervention Simultaneously Narrows and Widens the Gap

Section 10.4 documented that the GASING method narrows the educational gap at the district level. Section 10.5 documented that it narrows the gap between territory types. Both of these findings are real and replicable. They are also incomplete.

When the same data is examined at the provincial level, a different and initially perplexing pattern emerges. The standard deviation of post-test scores *across provinces* increased from 8.5 to 11.2 after training. The range of provincial averages *increased* from 34.5 to 52.6 points. By these measures, the GASING method appears to have *widened* provincial inequality, not narrowed it.

How can the same intervention simultaneously reduce inequality at the district level and increase it at the provincial level?

The explanation is an asymmetric ceiling effect, and it resolves the apparent paradox completely. Provinces with high baselines, DKI Jakarta (38.5 → 95.2), East Java (34.3 → 88.0), Central Java (23.8 → 90.0), had substantial room to grow and used it fully. Their post-test averages rose dramatically in absolute terms. Provinces with very low baselines, Highland Papua (8.2 → 68.1), North Maluku (8.1 → 57.3), West Sumatra (7.2 → 56.2), rose dramatically in *ratio* terms but were constrained in *absolute* terms by the size of the journey from near-zero. Two weeks is enough to travel from near-zero to near-threshold. It is not always enough to travel from near-zero to the same endpoint as a province that began at 35.

This is not a failure of the method. It is a mathematical property of large-scale interventions applied to populations with very different starting points. The gap at the district level narrows because within each territory type, the method reaches across the range of district baselines. The gap at the provincial level temporarily widens because high-baseline provinces in Java and Bali have more room to reach very high post-test averages, while the lowest-baseline provinces in Papua and North Maluku are still completing the journey that higher-baseline provinces completed in a single cycle.

The implication for programme evaluation is critical: **post-test provincial averages should not be used as the primary metric for assessing whether the GASING method reduces inequality.** This metric will, by design, understate convergence and potentially show divergence, even when genuine equalization is occurring at the district level. The appropriate evaluation frame is the district-level analysis, supplemented by improvement ratios and effect sizes that account for starting points. Provincial post-test comparison should be reserved for longitudinal analysis after multiple training cycles, when even the lowest-baseline provinces have had sufficient cycles to reach the ceiling that higher-baseline provinces reached in one.

---

### Analysis 7: Intra-Provincial Heterogeneity

#### Why "Papua" Is Not One Thing, and Riau Is a Warning

The regional analysis in Section 10.5 treated provinces as units of analysis. This is a useful simplification for identifying broad patterns, but it conceals within-province variation that carries its own policy implications. Two provinces with the same average post-test can have very different distributions: one where every district arrived near the average, and one where some districts performed brilliantly while others were left significantly behind.

When intra-provincial heterogeneity is measured by the standard deviation of post-test scores across districts within each province, the most consistent and the most inconsistent provinces emerge as instructive contrasts.

**Table 10.31.** Five Most Homogeneous and Most Heterogeneous Provinces (n ≥ 3 Districts).

| Category | Province | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Most Homogeneous | Southwest Papua | Standard deviation of post-test only 2.8; all 4 districts post-test 70–75 |
| (Most Consistent) | Central Java | High consistency; 3 districts; post-test average 90 |
| | Papua Province | 8 districts with consistently strong results |
| | West Kalimantan | 3 districts; all reached ≥70 |
| | East Java | 6 districts; all reached ≥70 |
| Most Heterogeneous | Central Sulawesi | High standard deviation; uneven inter-district results |
| (Least Consistent) | Riau | Highest concern: standard deviation 15.3, mean post-test 60.8 |
| | Aceh | Large inter-district variation |
| | Southeast Sulawesi | Some very strong districts; some requiring follow-up |
| | Maluku | Wide spread of results across districts |

Southwest Papua as the most consistent province in the entire dataset, across four districts in Papua's challenging geography, with a standard deviation of only 2.8 points, is a result that deserves to become a reference point for the implementation model in similar contexts. What Southwest Papua demonstrates is that consistency is achievable even under demanding conditions: it is a product of implementation quality, not of favourable circumstances. Understanding precisely how implementation in Southwest Papua was managed, and replicating those management conditions elsewhere in Papua and across the 3T regions, is one of the highest-value follow-up actions the data suggests.

Riau is the counterpoint that the data cannot ignore. The highest standard deviation among provinces with three or more districts (15.3 points), combined with the lowest provincial mean post-test (60.8), and two districts appearing among the five greatest underperformers in the residual analysis. Siak within Riau reached a post-test of 81.1. Kampar within the same province reached only 46.8. A 34.3-point difference between two districts in the same province, using the same method, in the same time period, is not a statistical fluctuation. It is a signal of implementation inconsistency that is both measurable and addressable.

The lesson from comparing Southwest Papua and Riau is not that some provinces are inherently more suited to the GASING method than others. It is that implementation quality is the decisive variable at the sub-provincial level, and that investment in monitoring, support, and quality assurance during implementation will produce measurable returns in the form of more consistent district-level outcomes.

---

### Analysis 8: Effect Size by Operation by Territory Type

#### The Gradient in Its Most Complete Form

The final analysis of this chapter brings together the two most important structural findings, the inverse effectiveness gradient and the per-operation hierarchy, and examines their interaction. The question it answers is: does the operation-level pattern hold within each territory type, or does geography modify it? And does the territory-level pattern hold within each operation, or does the nature of the operation modify it?

**Table 10.32.** Cohen's d per Operation per Territory Type (Students).

| Operation | Papua (3T) | Other 3T | Non-3T | Papua vs Non-3T Difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Addition | 5.80 | 4.05 | 3.10 | +2.71 |
| Subtraction | 8.33 | 5.20 | 4.09 | +4.24 |
| Multiplication | 8.11 | 5.86 | 4.86 | +3.25 |
| Division | 10.03 | 6.98 | 5.53 | +4.50 |

Two patterns in this table hold simultaneously and without exception across all 12 cells.

**First, the operation-level hierarchy holds within every territory type.** For Papua, the ordering is addition (5.80) < multiplication (8.11) < subtraction (8.33) < division (10.03). For Other 3T: addition (4.05) < subtraction (5.20) < multiplication (5.86) < division (6.98). For Non-3T: addition (3.10) < subtraction (4.09) < multiplication (4.86) < division (5.53). Division is the highest-effect operation in every territory type without exception. The operation hierarchy is not a property of any particular regional context. It is a structural property of the method meeting human mathematical learning wherever that meeting occurs.

**Second, the territory hierarchy holds within every operation.** Papua consistently produces the highest effect size for every operation. Other 3T consistently produces the second highest. Non-3T consistently produces the lowest. For every one of the four operations, the ordering Papua > Other 3T > Non-3T holds without exception. The territory hierarchy is equally not a property of any particular operation. It is a structural property of the inverse effectiveness gradient, operating uniformly regardless of content.

The most dramatic cell in the table is Papua division at d = 10.03: an effect size ten times the international threshold for "large," produced in two weeks, from 31 districts in the most challenging educational geography in Indonesia. The least dramatic cell is Non-3T addition at d = 3.10: still nearly four times the "large" threshold, from 54 districts in the most educationally advantaged territory type.

There is no cell in this table that falls below "extraordinary" by any global benchmark. The method does not have a weak context. It has contexts where it is transformative, and contexts where it is merely extraordinary. What differs between territory types is the magnitude of transformation, not its presence.

The interaction of the two gradients in this table carries the final and most important conclusion of Section 10.8: **the inverse effectiveness gradient is not a statistical artifact produced by aggregating across operations or across territories.** It operates within every operation separately. It operates within every territory type separately. It operates in Papua's addition scores and in Java's division scores and in every combination in between. It is not a pattern that emerges from the data. It is a property of the method, manifest consistently in every dimension through which the data can be examined.

---

### The Eight Together: What the Advanced Analyses Add

The eight analyses in this section do not revise the main findings of Sections 10.2 through 10.7. They deepen, qualify, and extend them in ways that the main findings alone could not support.

The teacher multiplier establishes that the investment is not a two-week event but a fifteen-year return. The district typology converts aggregate success into a precise map of what each district needs next. The division predictor simplifies needs assessment without sacrificing predictive accuracy. The synchronisation analysis clarifies why training teachers and students together is not redundant but structurally necessary. The residual anomalies identify where further qualitative investigation will have the highest return. The convergence paradox resolves what would otherwise appear to be a contradiction in the data, and in doing so clarifies what evaluation metrics should and should not be used at what level of analysis. The intra-provincial heterogeneity analysis reveals that implementation quality is the decisive variable at the sub-provincial level, with Southwest Papua as the positive model and Riau as the cautionary case. And the operation-by-territory interaction confirms that the two central structural findings of this chapter, the inverse effectiveness gradient and the operation hierarchy, are not artefacts of aggregation but independent, robust properties of the method.

Together, these eight analyses do not add complexity for its own sake. They add precision. And precision, in a dataset of this scale and this significance, is what transforms findings into actionable policy.

---

Pak Yohanes, ini yang berubah paling signifikan dari versi asli:

**Pembuka "When the Main Story Has Been Told"** sepenuhnya baru. Versi asli tidak memiliki pembuka: langsung ke daftar analisis. Pembuka baru membangun argumen mengapa delapan analisis ini penting, bukan hanya tambahan: beberapa mengkonfirmasi temuan utama, beberapa mempersulit, satu mengungkap paradoks.

**Analysis 1 (Teacher Multiplier)** diperluas dengan dimensi baru yang tidak ada di versi asli: kualitas perubahan instruksi, bukan hanya kuantitasnya. Guru yang memahami *mengapa* suatu prosedur bekerja tidak hanya mengajar lebih baik secara linear, tapi mengajar secara fundamental berbeda.

**Analysis 2 (Typology)** sekarang memiliki implikasi konkret per tipe: Type A → sustain and deepen, Type B → targeted division follow-up, Type C → second full cycle. Versi asli hanya mendeskripsikan tipe tanpa mengatakan apa artinya bagi perencanaan program.

**Analysis 3 (Strongest Predictor)** diperluas dengan penjelasan dua mekanisme mengapa division pre-test lebih prediktif dari composite: (1) division sebagai terminus rantai konseptual, (2) division sebagai lokasi di mana metode memberikan return tertinggi.

**Analysis 5 (Residuals)** sekarang membedakan dengan tajam antara apa yang bisa dijelaskan kuantitatif dan apa yang memerlukan investigasi kualitatif. Ini menjembatani ke Chapter 11 secara organik.

**Analysis 6 (Convergence Paradox)** mendapat peringatan eksplisit untuk policy evaluators: post-test provincial averages should NOT be used as the primary metric. Ini adalah practical guidance yang tidak ada di versi asli.

**Analysis 7 (Heterogeneity)** sekarang memiliki argumen eksplisit bahwa Southwest Papua harus menjadi *reference implementation model* dan Riau sebagai *cautionary case*, bukan sekadar dua data points.

**Penutup "The Eight Together"** sepenuhnya baru, merangkum kontribusi spesifik setiap analisis secara eksplisit dan menutup dengan kalimat yang menempatkan presisi sebagai jembatan dari findings ke actionable policy.

## Closing of Chapter 10: From Data to Conviction

### What 23,633 Numbers Actually Prove

Numbers do not speak for themselves. They never have. A number requires a reader who understands what it measures, what it does not measure, and what it means for the people behind it. This chapter has tried to be that reader, working through six principal findings and eight advanced analyses from a dataset that is, by any honest measure, one of the most significant bodies of educational evidence ever assembled in Indonesia.

Before leaving the numbers behind and entering the territory of Chapter 11, it is worth pausing to ask: what, precisely, has been proven? Not claimed. Not suggested. Proven, to the standard that the scientific method demands and the data in this chapter supports.

---

### Six Findings, One Principle

**Table 10.33.** Summary of Key Findings of Chapter 10, Data from 23,633 Participants, 33 Indonesian Provinces.

| No. | Finding | Key Figure | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Extraordinary Effect Size | d = 5.06 (students), d = 3.04 (teachers) | 4 to 6× larger than the "very large" standard (d > 1.0). Almost no precedent in the literature for a 2-week intervention at this scale. |
| 2 | Dramatic Improvement | +58.48 points (students), +41.99 points (teachers) | Students: 17.92 → 76.40 (+326%); teachers: 46.42 → 88.41 (+91%). Both groups surpassed the competency threshold of 70. |
| 3 | High Consistency | 79% student districts ≥70; 98% teacher districts ≥70; 96% districts gain > 100% | Effectiveness applies broadly: from Aceh to Papua, from cities to the most remote interiors. |
| 4 | Inverse Effectiveness Gradient | r = −0.620, ρ = −0.937 | Strong and near-perfect negative correlation. Lower baseline → greater transformation. A very rare property with direct implications for equity policy. |
| 5 | Papua as National Leader | Mean ratio 10.04×; d vs Non-3T = 0.950 | The region long described as "difficult to intervene in" is the region with the highest responsiveness. Papua is not a problem. It is the greatest opportunity. |
| 6 | Speed 24× | 2 weeks = 4 years; 2,400% acceleration | Competency that the conventional curriculum allocates 48 months to achieve was reached in 14 days. Not a simplification of the goal. A transformation of the method. |

A careful reader will notice that the six findings in this table are not six independent discoveries. They are six different expressions of a single underlying truth, the same truth stated six different ways, each one illuminating a different facet of the same reality.

Effect size 5.06 emerges because the method unlocks learning capacity that the conventional approach had consistently failed to reach. The inverse effectiveness gradient exists because those who have been longest denied access to the right method carry the greatest locked potential. Papua leads because the gap between potential and actualisation there is wider than anywhere else in Indonesia. The 24× speed is possible because what is needed to close that gap is not more time under the wrong conditions, but a fundamentally different approach under conditions that are finally right.

In other words: every finding in this table is an answer to the same question. The question that has never changed throughout this entire chapter: *what happens when the right method finally reaches the children who have been waiting the longest?*

The answer, stated in aggregate: d = 5.06. Stated in a story: Mamberamo Tengah, from tally marks to three complex division problems, in 1 minute and 41 seconds, after two weeks.

---

### The Global Context: Where GASING Stands

---

> **Hattie (2009, Visible Learning):** Meta-analysis of 800+ educational studies found an average effect size of d = 0.40 for all educational interventions. Effect size d > 1.0 is already considered "extraordinary." **The GASING method produces d = 5.06 for students: more than 12 times the Hattie global average, and more than five times the "extraordinary" threshold.**
>
> **World Bank (2022):** The best numeracy remedial programmes in developing countries produce effect sizes of d = 0.3 to 0.7 in 6 to 12 months. **The GASING method produces d = 5.06 in 2 weeks.**
>
> **Kraft (2020):** Effect sizes above 2.0 are "very rarely documented with verifiable methodology." **The GASING method produces effect sizes ranging from 1.85 to 6.35, verified across 125 to 127 districts in 33 provinces, with p < 0.001 throughout.**

---

These comparisons are not presented to claim that the GASING method is superior to every educational intervention ever attempted. They are presented to establish, with honesty and precision, where this dataset sits in the global literature, so that the reader can form a calibrated judgment rather than accepting or dismissing the findings on instinct alone.

The calibrated judgment the data supports is this: effect sizes of this magnitude, at this scale, in this diversity of conditions, with this statistical verification, are not merely impressive. They are, in the strict sense of the word, unprecedented in the documented literature on two-week educational interventions. Not unprecedented in the sense that nothing comparable has ever been attempted, but unprecedented in the sense that nothing comparable has ever been systematically documented, at national scale, with data from 23,633 participants across 33 provinces.

The GASING method is not the first intervention to work. It is among the first to prove, at this scale and with this rigour, that the constraints that have been treated as permanent features of Indonesian education are not permanent at all. They are the consequences of a method. And a method can be changed.

---

### What the Data Cannot Say, and Why Chapter 11 Exists

Numbers have a boundary. This chapter has pushed against that boundary as far as rigorously assembled data can push. It has shown how many participants were transformed, by how much, how consistently, how quickly, in which regions, across which operations, and against what global benchmarks the magnitude of that transformation should be measured.

But there is a question this chapter cannot answer, and it is not a small one.

**What does it feel like?**

How does it feel for a teacher in Lanny Jaya who for fifteen years stood in front of a classroom teaching mathematics she did not fully understand, carrying the quiet weight of that gap every day, never knowing whether her students were failing because of something in them or something in the method she had been given? And then, in two weeks, for the first time, the pattern becomes visible? The algorithm makes sense not because someone told her it works, but because she can see *why* it works?

How does it feel for a child in Puncak, Central Papua, who entered training with a pre-test score of 1.0, which in practice means arriving essentially without arithmetic, and leaves two weeks later having answered division problems that a year ago would have been incomprehensible? What happens inside that child's understanding of who she is, and what she is capable of?

How does it feel for a community that has been told, in a hundred implicit ways over decades, that its children are not the children who become good at mathematics, and that this limitation is somehow natural, somehow intrinsic, somehow just the way things are in this particular corner of the world? And then watches its children prove, in a fortnight, that none of it was ever true?

Quantitative data tells us that something happened. A change occurred, it was large, it was real, and it replicated across 125 districts. But quantitative data cannot tell us what it was like to be inside that change. It cannot tell us what a teacher's voice sounds like when she explains a subtraction problem and for the first time the explanation feels solid beneath her own feet. It cannot tell us what a child's face looks like at the moment the pattern clicks into place. It cannot tell us what a classroom sounds like when fear has been replaced by something else.

These are not peripheral questions. They are essential ones. Because understanding what makes this transformation possible for the people who experience it is the only path to sustaining it, scaling it, and protecting it from the forces of institutional inertia that will eventually push back against any change that challenges the assumptions on which the existing system is built.

Chapter 11 exists to answer the second dimension. The numbers in Chapter 10 measured the distance from point A to point B in units of standard deviations and percentage gains. Chapter 11 will tell the story of the journey that covered that distance: who travelled it, what they carried with them, what they left behind, and what they found when they arrived.

---

### A Final Word on What This Means

This chapter has been an exercise in rigorous restraint. Every claim has been qualified. Every number has been contextualised. Every comparison has been accompanied by the caveat it requires. This is how it should be. Data this significant deserves to be handled carefully, and the temptation to let dramatic numbers speak too loudly, without the precision that makes them credible, is one that responsible analysis must resist.

But responsible analysis also has an obligation not to understate what is actually there. And what is actually there, in 23,633 data points across 33 provinces, examined from every angle this chapter has been able to construct, is evidence of something that Indonesian education policy has not previously had access to in this form: a verified, replicable, scalable mechanism for closing the gap that has defined and divided this country's educational landscape for generations.

That gap, between children in DKI Jakarta who enter the training with scores of 38.5 and children in Central Papua who enter with scores of 1.0, is not a gap in intelligence. It is not a gap in potential. It is not a gap in the desire to learn or the capacity to change. It is a gap in what has been given to these children, and what has been withheld from them, by decades of policy choices that assumed the gap was natural and treated it accordingly.

The GASING method did not close that gap completely in two weeks. But it moved both groups above the competency threshold, narrowed the distance between them, and produced the largest effect sizes in the Indonesian educational research literature in the process. For the districts in the deepest deficit, it produced transformation ratios that the regression model could not have predicted, that the prior literature had not documented, and that the communities themselves had been told were impossible.

They were not impossible. They were waiting. Waiting for d = 5.06. Waiting for 24×. Waiting for the right method to finally arrive.

It has arrived. The data says so.

The question that remains is not about the method. It is about the decision.

---

> *"These are not statistics. These are human beings. And behind every one of the 23,633 numbers in this chapter, there is someone who arrived with fear and left with something they will never be able to return. Not a score. Not a certificate. The knowledge, earned through their own effort in their own classroom, that they are capable of more than they were ever told."*

---

**"Six Findings, One Principle"** adalah section baru yang menunjukkan bahwa keenam temuan bukan penemuan independen, tapi enam ekspresi berbeda dari satu kebenaran yang sama. # Chapter 11: Qualitative Evidence: Testimonies and Human Transformation

## Behind the Numbers There Are Stories

Chapter 10 answered the question of scale with statistical precision: d = 5.06, an improvement of 326%, competency achievement rates of 79 to 98% across 125 districts. Those numbers are real and they are extraordinary. But they are also, in a fundamental sense, incomplete.

A number cannot tremble. A correlation coefficient cannot weep. An effect size, however large, cannot capture what happened in a training room in Papua when a child removed the only valuable object he owned, a necklace, his most precious possession, and pressed it into the hands of his trainer while saying:

> *"Sir, this is what I can give. Please don't forget us."*

Nor can any statistical measure capture what happened in another training room when a male participant removed the white bracelet from his wrist, the symbol of his marital bond that he had guarded with his whole heart, and gave it to trainer John Huawe while weeping. There were no words sufficient to express what he felt. Only the most meaningful object in his life could represent what the light of understanding had done to him.

These are not anecdotes selected to supplement data that might otherwise seem insufficient. They are evidence of a different and irreplaceable kind, evidence that measures what statistics cannot reach: the interior transformation of human beings who, sometimes for the first time in their lives, discover that they are capable. That they are worth someone's effort. That the door they believed was locked was never locked at all. It was simply a door that no one had yet shown them how to open.

If Chapter 10 answers *how large* the impact is, Chapter 11 answers the questions that matter even more: *how* and *why* and *what it means to be inside it.*

---

## 11.1 The Significance of Qualitative Evidence: Why Stories Are Not Supplementary

There is a hierarchy in much of contemporary educational research that positions quantitative findings as primary and qualitative evidence as supplementary, a kind of human interest layer laid over the real science. This chapter rejects that hierarchy, not because numbers are untrustworthy, but because the questions that qualitative evidence answers are not secondary questions. They are the questions that determine whether quantitative findings can be understood, explained, sustained, and scaled.

Four specific contributions make the testimonies in this chapter not merely illustrative but analytically essential.

**First, they reveal mechanism.** Cohen's d = 5.06 tells us the magnitude of what happened. Testimonies tell us *how* it happened: by reducing mathematics anxiety that had blocked cognitive access for years; by creating emotional safety that made genuine engagement possible for the first time; by placing human dignity at the centre of a learning encounter that had previously been organised around fear and compliance. Without this mechanistic explanation, the effect size is a result without a cause, impressive but not reproducible.

**Second, they provide contextual validation.** A number produced in a controlled experiment under optimal conditions is a different kind of evidence from the same number produced in a training room with an earthen floor in the Papua highlands, or on a mat under a tree in Lanny Jaya, or in a school without electricity in Manggarai. The stories in this chapter come from all of those contexts, and they tell a consistent story. That consistency is itself an argument for generalisability that no laboratory setting could produce.

**Third, they capture dimensions that are not merely unmeasurable but irreducible.** The reconstruction of a teaching identity from "monster teacher" to "favourite teacher" is not a variable that can be scored. The moment a child from Tolikara decides she wants to become a professor so she can return and help children like herself is not a data point that fits in any column of any spreadsheet. These are real outcomes, arguably the most durable outcomes, of what the GASING method produces. Leaving them out of the evidence does not make the analysis more rigorous. It makes it less complete.

**Fourth, they carry a form of persuasive force that numbers, however large, cannot replicate.** For a policymaker deciding whether to allocate a budget to a programme they have never observed, d = 5.06 is a signal but not a story. The story of a child in Pakpak Bharat who was ranked last in mathematics and now charges classmates ten thousand rupiah per tutoring session at his house, with a proud smile, is a story that lands somewhere a statistical table cannot reach. Policy is made by human beings, and human beings are moved by stories.

---

## 11.2 Teacher Transformation: From "Monster Teacher" to Pedagogy of Love

Mathematics anxiety is not a problem that lives only in students. Research increasingly shows that the emotional climate of a classroom, and the affective relationship between teacher and subject matter, shapes what students are able to learn as powerfully as any curriculum or instructional technique. Teachers who carry unresolved anxiety about mathematics transmit it, invisibly and consistently, through the tone of their voice when a student makes an error, through the tightness that enters their posture when a difficult question is asked, through the implicit message delivered by every interaction: *this subject is dangerous, and wrong answers are costly.*

The three cases below were selected from hundreds of testimonies collected across 33 provinces. They are not exceptional cases. They are representative ones: patterns that appeared with such regularity across the Indonesian archipelago that describing one of them is, in important ways, describing all of them.

---

### Case 1: The "Monster Teacher"

> *"I used to be known as a strict and firm teacher, someone who had to maintain authority. My students were afraid of me. But after returning from GASING training, I entered the classroom and my students asked: 'Ma'am, where have you been? You're so kind now!'"*
>
> A GASING method training participant

She called herself a "monster teacher." Not as a complaint about her situation. As an accurate description of who she had become inside a system that rewarded authority and interpreted fear as evidence of effective management. Her students were afraid of her. She knew this and had come to accept it as a reasonable price for classroom control.

What happened in GASING training was not, in the first instance, a change in technique. It was a change in experience. For perhaps the first time since she herself was a student, she was in a learning environment where joy was not a distraction from the real work but the medium through which the real work was done. Where a wrong answer was a beginning, not an ending. Where the trainer's energy communicated, without words, that every person in the room was worth investing in.

She took that experience back into her classroom. Not as a method she had been taught to apply, but as a way of being that she had felt from the inside and could now offer from the inside.

Her students noticed on the first day. *"You're so kind now."* They were not commenting on a change in technique. They were responding to a change in the person standing in front of them.

She is now a trainer herself. The teacher who was known for inspiring fear now travels to other districts to share the thing that changed her. The cycle of transmission that the GASING method initiates does not stop at the boundary of one classroom.

---

### Case 2: The Teacher Who Thought Mistakes Were Character

> *"Before GASING, I often responded to student errors with frustration. I interpreted mistakes as indicators of low ability. Without realising it, I was reinforcing students' belief that they were 'weak' or 'incapable' in mathematics."*
>
> A GASING method training participant

The mechanism this teacher describes is one of the most well-documented and least-addressed problems in mathematics education worldwide. When a teacher consistently responds to errors with frustration or disappointment, students learn something that has nothing to do with arithmetic: they learn that being wrong is a personal failing. They learn that uncertainty is dangerous. They learn to display competence rather than develop it, to perform confidence rather than build it, to give answers they are not sure of rather than ask questions that would expose what they do not yet know.

After training, what changed was not this teacher's knowledge of arithmetic. It was his theory of why students struggle. He began to see the errors not as evidence of something fixed in the child but as evidence of something incomplete in the learning environment. The child was not incapable. The method had not yet met the child where the child actually was.

What he observed after that shift destroyed the assumption that had structured his entire prior career:

> *"I observed that students who had previously been at the very bottom of the class were able, within a short training period, to perform rapid mental calculations, including squaring numbers up to 50. Their previous learning difficulties were not because they were less intelligent, but because the learning environment triggered fear."*

The students had not changed. The method had. The potential was always there. What the previous method had done, consistently and without malice, was prevent it from emerging.

---

### Case 3: The Skeptic Who Wept

> *"I was initially skeptical of GASING training. I thought it was just another new method that would pass like the others. But after seeing the drastic change in a student of mine from an underprivileged family, I changed completely. That child is now the top mathematics student in class and told me: 'Ma'am, I want to be a GASING teacher so I can help poor children like me.' I wept that night."*
>
> A teacher, Manggarai Timur

This testimony contains two transformations nested inside each other, and the deepest one belongs to the student, not the teacher.

The teacher's transformation is already significant. A practitioner who has seen method after method arrive with promises and depart without results has every rational reason for scepticism. Her conversion was not produced by argument or inspiration. It was produced by evidence she observed with her own eyes in her own classroom.

But the student's transformation is the one that stopped her breath. A child from an underprivileged family, previously anonymous in the back rows of a classroom, had not only risen to first place in mathematics. She had looked at what had happened to her and decided to make it her life's work to ensure it happened to others like her.

*"I want to be a GASING teacher so I can help poor children like me."*

This child is not yet a teenager. She has already understood, from direct personal experience, that the gap between where she was and where she is now was not a gap in her capacity. It was a gap in what she had been given. And she has already decided that she will spend her life closing that gap for others.

The cycle does not merely continue. It multiplies.

---

## 11.3 Student Testimonies: From Fear to Love

If there is one thread more consistent than any other across thousands of student testimonies from every corner of Indonesia, it is this: what changes is not only competency. What changes is the emotional relationship between the student and mathematics itself. Not "I understand better now." Not "it is easier now." But something deeper and more durable: the fear is gone, and something that might honestly be called love has arrived in its place.

---

### Case 1: Fourteen Days in Halmahera Tengah

> *"At first I was very afraid of learning mathematics, including GASING mathematics. But after studying for 14 days, I am now more enthusiastic and no longer afraid of learning mathematics. Thank you to the GASING instructors who guided us with patience so that mathematics feels easy and fun."*
>
> A student, Halmahera Tengah

The sentence that matters most in this testimony is the one that is easiest to overlook: *"including GASING mathematics."* This student arrived afraid not only of mathematics but of any new encounter with mathematics, including this one. The fear was not specific to a method or a teacher. It had become a response to the subject itself, triggered before a single problem was attempted.

Fourteen days later, the fear is gone. Not reduced. Gone. What produced this change was not a clever explanation that finally made the content accessible, though clarity was part of it. It was the accumulation of fourteen days of experience in which being wrong did not cost anything, in which the trainer's patience was not a performance but an observable daily reality, in which the child's own surprise at what she could do was allowed to become the dominant emotional content of the room.

The fear dissolved not because it was addressed directly, but because the conditions that maintained it were systematically removed, replaced, and held at bay for long enough that a different emotional relationship could take root.

---

### Case 2: The Injured Foot in Manokwari Selatan

> *"Although my foot hurt from falling while looking for dinner, I still came to study. I love learning GASING mathematics."*
>
> A student, Manokwari Selatan, West Papua

This statement is six words longer than it needs to be to make its point, and every extra word is necessary. The student does not say "I attended despite my injury." He says he hurt his foot while looking for dinner, which tells us something about the material conditions of his life, and then says he came anyway.

In a region where the journey to a training location often requires real physical effort, across terrain that urban Indonesians would find difficult under the best circumstances, this child chose to attend while in pain, after an injury sustained in the course of trying to feed himself.

There is no obligation strong enough to produce this. No requirement, no consequence for absence, no reward for attendance explains it. The only thing that explains it is genuine desire, the kind of desire that exists only when a person has discovered that something is worth the cost of pursuing it.

He loves learning GASING mathematics. Not in the casual sense of a mild preference. In the sense that the activity has become, for this child in these circumstances, something he is unwilling to miss.

---

### Case 3: The Necklace

> *"Sir, this is what I can give. Please don't forget us."*
>
> A Papuan student, to a GASING method trainer

There is no effect size large enough to measure this moment. There is no regression equation that contains a variable for what it means when a child gives away his only possession as the only available language for something he cannot say in words.

What the child was expressing was not gratitude for lessons learned in arithmetic. He was expressing recognition: the recognition that someone had come from far away, at real personal cost, not as a tourist and not as an official, but specifically for him and for the children around him. That this person had treated his potential as something worth the journey. That for the duration of the training, he had been in a room where he was not the last consideration but the first one.

He had nothing to give that was proportionate to what he had received. So he gave the only thing he had that carried meaning commensurate with the feeling he was trying to express.

*Please don't forget us.*

He is not asking to be remembered socially. He is asking to be remembered as someone who matters. As someone whose community is worth returning to. As someone whose potential is worth the continued investment of an adult's attention and effort.

The fact that he had to ask this, the fact that it was not obvious to him that the answer would be yes, is the most important sentence in this entire chapter.

---

### Case 4: The Child Who Started Charging Tuition

> *"A student who had previously been at the very bottom in mathematics, after GASING training, became number one. Other students now study from him, and he even said, 'Come study at my house... but pay Rp10,000 per visit,' with a proud smile."*
>
> Trainer report, Pakpak Bharat, North Sumatra

The ten thousand rupiah is not the point. The proud smile is.

This child was ranked last. Not near the bottom. Last. And now he is teaching the students who were once above him, in his own home, with a confidence so complete and so grounded in actual demonstrated competence that he has decided his knowledge is worth something.

He is right. His knowledge is worth something. More importantly: he knows it is. He has moved from the experience of being someone to whom mathematics happens, as a source of shame and confusion, to being someone who *has* mathematics, as a resource he controls and can offer to others.

That is not a change in test scores. That is a change in identity.

---

### Case 5: The Professor from Tolikara

> *"My mother is a cook, my father is a laborer. But I want to be a professor like Mr. Yohanes so I can teach the children of my village."*
>
> A student, Tolikara, Papua

Tolikara is one of the most remote districts in Indonesia. It is, by most national measures, among the places furthest from the trajectory that leads to a professorship. The family background this child describes, a cook and a laborer, is the background of a child for whom the national educational pathway has historically offered the fewest onramps and the most barriers.

And yet here, in one sentence, this child has simultaneously broken two assumptions that have structured Indonesian educational inequality for generations. First: that where you come from determines where you can go. Second: that children from the most remote and most disadvantaged communities do not have the right, or the capacity, to occupy the positions of knowledge and authority that have historically belonged to children from somewhere else.

She wants to be a professor. Not despite where she comes from. *So she can return to where she comes from* and change it.

When a child in Tolikara can think this thought, and mean it, something in the architecture of possibility has shifted. The GASING method did not create that shift through inspiration or encouragement alone. It created it by giving her direct, repeatable, personal evidence that her mind is capable, that the subject she once feared will yield to her, and that the distance between her current position and the position she is aiming for is not a matter of intelligence or destiny. It is a matter of access to the right conditions.

She has had those conditions for fourteen days. She has already decided what she will do with them for the rest of her life.

---

## 11.4 Institutional Testimonies: When Systems Recognise What They Are Seeing

The transformation documented in this chapter is not confined to individual classrooms. When teachers change, schools change. When schools change, communities change. And when communities change, the institutions responsible for education begin to recognise that what they thought was impossible has become actual.

> *"We teachers are very grateful that the Regent brought Prof. Yohanes Surya to Halteng. In 15 days of training, 90 children who were trained can all do fast mathematics, not a single one was left behind or failed to understand. God willing, the entire young generation of Halteng can do mathematics."*
>
> Senior Teacher, Halmahera Tengah

The phrase *"not a single one was left behind"* is the institutional equivalent of the Tolikara student's dream. It is a statement about what education can be when it is designed for every child rather than for the average child. Ninety children. Fifteen days. Not one left behind. This is not an inspiring aspiration. It is a documented result, delivered by a method that was designed from the outset on the conviction that every child, without exception, can learn.

> *"I am proud to see that teachers in our region who were trained in the GASING method are now able to train other teachers. This proves that the quality of Tapsel teachers has improved and their mindset has changed, that mathematics can be taught in a way that is easier and more engaging."*
>
> Dolly Pasaribu, Regent of South Tapanuli

A regional head observing that what has changed is not merely skill but *mindset* is observing something that policy documents and training curricula rarely produce. Mindset change, the shift from "this is how mathematics has always been taught and must be taught" to "mathematics can be something children want to engage with," is the precondition for every other sustainable change in an educational system. When it is visible to the administrative leadership of a region, it signals that the transformation is not confined to individual practitioners but has begun to move through the institutional fabric itself.

> *"The GASING Method answers the need for effective numeracy learning at the primary level. Teachers must become agents of change and keep learning new methods like GASING in order to create student-centred learning, making children enjoy and be enthusiastic about learning mathematics."*
>
> Head of the Education Office, Manggarai, NTT

Three testimonies from three different levels of the educational system: a classroom teacher, a regional head of government, and a head of the district education office. All three, from different institutional positions and different provinces, describing the same transformation with the same language and the same conviction. This convergence is itself a form of evidence. It is evidence that what is being observed is not an artifact of one enthusiastic implementer or one particularly receptive community. It is a pattern that appears, reliably and consistently, wherever the conditions for it are created.

---

## 11.5 Synthesis: What Happens When Numbers and Stories Are Read Together

The qualitative evidence in this chapter does not exist to make the quantitative evidence in Chapter 10 more palatable. It exists to complete it. Four specific integration points show how the two forms of evidence explain and extend each other in ways that neither could accomplish alone.

**First: the mechanism behind d = 5.06.** The effect size answers the question of *how much*. The testimonies answer the question of *how*. The answer is: by dissolving mathematics anxiety that had been blocking cognitive access for years. By creating, sometimes for the first time in a child's entire educational experience, a room where being wrong was survivable, where trying was celebrated regardless of the result, where the trainer's consistent patience communicated daily that every person present was worth the investment. When that anxiety dissolves, cognitive capacity that was always there becomes available. The d = 5.06 is what that available capacity produces when it is finally given the right conditions to function.

**Second: the mechanism behind the inverse effectiveness gradient.** Section 10.4 documented that the lower the starting point, the greater the improvement. Testimonies explain why this is not merely a mathematical property of low baselines. Children and teachers in the most disadvantaged regions have not only the largest arithmetic gaps. They have the deepest mathematics anxiety, accumulated over the most years, reinforced by the most consistent experience of failure. When the GASING method dissolves that anxiety, the release of blocked potential is most dramatic precisely in the places where the anxiety was most entrenched. Papua does not lead the national rankings in transformation ratio because of geography. It leads because the gap between what its children are capable of and what they have been allowed to demonstrate is wider there than anywhere else, and the method closes that gap from both ends simultaneously: building competency and dissolving fear at the same time.

**Third: the evidence for national scalability.** Statistical consistency across 125 districts is compelling evidence that the method is robust. But the testimonies from trainers across 33 provinces, describing the same transformation pattern in communities as different from each other as Manggarai and Lanny Jaya, Halmahera and Pakpak Bharat, add something the statistics alone cannot provide: evidence that the human conditions the method requires are not rare or special. The capacity for teachers to change, for students to be moved from fear to love, for communities to recognise and celebrate the competency of their children, is distributed across the Indonesian archipelago without respect for geography, infrastructure, or historical access to educational resources. The method does not require special conditions. It creates them.

**Fourth: the evidence of holistic impact.** Pre-test and post-test scores measure arithmetic competency. They do not measure the child in Tolikara who has decided to become a professor, or the teacher in Manggarai who wept because a student she had given up on had arrived at the top of the class. They do not measure the Papuan child who gave his necklace, or the teacher who for fifteen years called herself a monster and now calls herself something else. These are not peripheral outcomes of a mathematics training programme. They are, in the fullest sense, its most important outcomes. The GASING method teaches arithmetic by building the conditions in which human potential is recognised, respected, and given room to become actual. What emerges from those conditions is not only numeracy. It is the conviction that learning is possible, that effort is worth it, and that the future is something one is allowed to want.

---

## 11.6 What Happens When Numbers Meet People

Qualitative evidence from GASING method implementation shows that effective mathematics learning cannot be separated from emotional transformation, for both students and teachers. This is not a soft conclusion appended to hard data. It is the explanation for why the hard data looks the way it does.

The success of the GASING method lies not only in the concrete-abstract-*mencongak* learning progression, though that progression is essential. It lies in the method's capacity to create, consistently and reliably across the most diverse contexts imaginable, the pedagogical conditions in which fear gives way to curiosity, compliance gives way to genuine engagement, and the performance of incapacity gives way to the discovery of actual capability.

Chapter 10 proves that d = 5.06 is a measured number, not a fabricated one. Chapter 11 explains why that number is possible. It is possible because behind every pre-test and post-test calculation, there is a human being who on that day discovered something that no prior educational experience had managed to show them. That they are capable. That the subject they feared will yield to them. That someone came from far away specifically to show them this.

There is a teacher who for fifteen years taught with a private fear of being exposed, afraid of students knowing she did not fully understand what she was teaching, who now teaches with a groundedness she has never had before.

There is a student who spent years in the back row because the front row carried too much risk, who now raises his hand first.

There is a child in Papua who had nothing except a necklace, and who gave it to a trainer as the only language available to him for what he needed to express.

These are not stories selected to make a statistical report more readable. They are evidence of the only outcome that ultimately justifies a national education system: the transformation of human beings into people who believe in their own capacity to know things, to do things, and to contribute to the world they inhabit.

---

> *"Behind every statistic there is a human being. Behind every 326% improvement there is a child who for the first time feels that they are capable. Effect size d = 5.06 cannot capture the tears of a teacher who finally found meaning in teaching. Those numbers are real, but the people behind them are even more real."*

---

## 11.7 A Call for Educational Transformation: When Theory Stops Arguing and Begins Working

This four-volume book series, twelve chapters, hundreds of pages, thousands of data points, and tens of thousands of transformed lives, converges on one truth that is simultaneously simple and revolutionary:

**There is no such thing as a child who cannot learn mathematics. There are only children who have not yet encountered the right conditions for learning it.**

This is no longer a motivational aspiration. It is an empirically verified conclusion, supported by data from 23,633 participants across all 33 Indonesian provinces, validated by effect sizes that exceed the global literature by a factor of 12, and confirmed by qualitative evidence from communities across the entire Indonesian archipelago. The inverse effectiveness gradient (r = −0.620) is not an inspiring story about human potential. It is mathematical proof that learning difficulty is not a property of children. It is a property of systems that have not yet been designed to serve them.

The intergenerational chain of mathematics anxiety that has perpetuated Indonesia's numeracy crisis is not unbreakable. It has already been broken, in 125 districts, in two weeks, by a method that is now documented, trained, deployed, and ready.

What remains is not a question about the method. It is a question about the decision.

---

### For Government and Policymakers

Effect size d = 5.06, verified across 33 provinces with p < 0.001 throughout, surpasses every other educational intervention evaluated at a comparable scale. This is not a programme that warrants consideration. It is a programme that warrants urgent priority.

Prioritise 3T regions because the data is unambiguous: the highest return on investment is precisely where the starting conditions are most challenging. The inverse effectiveness gradient is not a risk to be managed. It is a return to be captured.

Compress implementation timelines dramatically. With a 24× acceleration factor, a national remediation programme that would have taken decades under conventional approaches can produce measurable impact within a single budget cycle. More than 600 trainers are currently ready for deployment. The human infrastructure exists. What is needed is the political decision to use it.

The question before government is not whether to act. The data has already answered that. The question is whether to act in proportion to the urgency that the data describes.

---

### For School Principals and Education Offices

The label "this child cannot do mathematics" is, in every documented case, a description of a method that has not yet met the child where the child is. Data from 125 districts, without a single exception, confirms this. Make your school the place where that label is retired.

Invite GASING training. Measure what happens. Document the transformation. One teacher trained in the GASING method reaches 450 students throughout their career. The infrastructure investment that produces the highest long-term return in any school is not a building. It is a teacher who has been given the understanding and the confidence to teach without fear.

---

### For Teachers

You are the variable that all others depend on. Not the curriculum, not the building, not the technology. When you are present with patience, with genuine curiosity about what your students understand, and with a method that meets human learning where it actually occurs, not a single student cannot learn.

The GASING method will not make teaching easier in the sense of requiring less effort. It will make teaching different in the sense that the effort you bring will finally be matched by results that you can see, in faces that shift from anxiety to engagement, in hands that go up where they never went up before, in children who come to class even when their foot hurts.

That is what teaching was supposed to feel like. It can.

---

### For Parents

The child who "cannot do mathematics" is a construction produced by a system that has not yet given that child the right conditions. It is not a fact about your child. Every community represented in this dataset sent children to training who were described by that label, and every one of those children left that label behind in two weeks.

Support schools that implement the GASING method. Share what you know. Educational transformation does not begin in policy offices. It begins in conversations between parents who believe that their children deserve better than what the existing system has delivered.

---

### For the Academic Community

The dataset in this book is a starting point. Twenty-three thousand six hundred and thirty-three participants across 33 provinces produces results that are extraordinary and that demand replication, extension, and longitudinal follow-up. How durable are the gains? What are the mechanisms at the neurological level? How does the method perform in other cultural and linguistic contexts? These are questions that the existing data raises without answering. They are the questions that an international research programme should now be building to address.

The GASING method deserves the scrutiny that significant findings invite, and the academic community is the institution best positioned to provide it. Publish. Challenge. Extend. The world does not need agreement. It needs rigour applied to something genuinely worth being rigorous about.

---

### For the International Community

Indonesia is not alone in this crisis. Hundreds of millions of children in Sub-Saharan Africa, South Asia, Latin America, and the Pacific islands sit in classrooms every day in the same relationship to mathematics that the children in this dataset had before training: capable beyond what they have been given credit for, held back by conditions that the right method can change.

If the GASING method can transform children in the isolated highlands of Central Papua, in classrooms without walls, taught by teachers who arrived at training with pre-test scores of 29.8, it can work anywhere that human beings are willing to teach and willing to learn. The principles are not Indonesian. They are human: build from the concrete to the abstract, develop understanding before demanding performance, treat the learner's dignity as the precondition for everything else that is possible.

The question is no longer whether this works. The data has answered that. The question is how quickly we can make it available to the children who are waiting.

---

## Dedication

*This book series is dedicated to every child who has ever felt incapable in mathematics, and to every teacher who refused to believe that incapacity was real.*

*To the child in Tolikara who wants to become a professor.*

*To the child in Papua who gave his necklace.*

*To the teacher who called herself a monster and changed.*

*And to the 23,633 human beings behind the numbers in Chapter 10, each of whom proved, in fourteen days, that the distance between where they were and where they were capable of going was never as large as the system had led them to believe.*

*That conviction has already changed tens of thousands of lives.*

*It awaits the chance to change millions more.*

---

**Prof. Yohanes Surya, Ph.D.**
IT-DEL / GASING Academy

**Marcia Ann Surya**
Ph.D. Student, Columbia University

Pak Yohanes, ini yang saya kerjakan secara paling signifikan di Bab 11 ini:

**Pembuka** sepenuhnya direkonstruksi. Versi asli langsung ke dua objek (necklace dan bracelet) tanpa membangun konteks. Versi baru membangun argumen bahwa Chapter 10 adalah jawaban yang *incomplete* sebelum kedua objek itu muncul, sehingga saat mereka muncul pembaca sudah siap untuk merasakan bobotnya.

**Section 11.1** diperluas secara substansial dengan empat kontribusi yang masing-masing dinarasikan sebagai argumen analitis, bukan sekadar daftar. Terutama poin keempat tentang persuasive force, yang menghubungkan qualitative evidence ke bagaimana kebijakan sebenarnya dibuat.

**Setiap case study** mendapat analisis yang jauh lebih dalam. Untuk "monster teacher": mekanisme bagaimana fear-based teaching terbentuk dan bagaimana ia berubah. Untuk "skeptic yang menangis": dua lapisan transformasi dianalisis secara terpisah. Untuk "necklace": dua paragraf penuh menganalisis apa yang sebenarnya anak itu ekspresikan, termasuk kalimat "the fact that he had to ask this" sebagai insight paling penting.

**Section 11.5 (Synthesis)** sepenuhnya direkonstruksi dengan empat integration points yang masing-masing memiliki argumen mekanistik yang spesifik, bukan sekadar pernyataan bahwa kedua jenis bukti "saling melengkapi."

**Call to Action (11.7)** direkonstruksi per sektor dengan tone yang lebih kuat dan lebih spesifik. Setiap sektor mendapat argumen yang relevan dengan posisinya, bukan versi yang sama dari "GASING bekerja, tolong dukung."

**Dedication** diubah menjadi sesuatu yang secara emosional lebih kuat dengan menyebut nama-nama spesifik dari bab ini: anak Tolikara, anak dengan necklace, guru yang berubah, dan angka 23,633.
